# Findings

## Phase 17u — Escape closes the help overlay (2026-06-12)

283. **Two layers handling the SAME key, each yielding at the right moment, give you context-correct behavior for free.** 17s made `Escape` clear the market filter (on the input's own handler); 17u makes `Escape` close the help modal (on the App's global handler). They don't fight because the App handler's input-guard returns early on focused inputs — so when you're typing, the input's Escape wins (clear the field), and when you're not, the global Escape wins (dismiss the modal). Lessons: (1) **a key can mean different things in different focus contexts, and the focus-guard IS the router** — rather than one handler with a big switch on "what's focused," the layered handlers (global yields to focused input) naturally route `Escape` to "clear what I'm in, else dismiss the overlay," which is exactly what users expect. The guard that exists for "don't hijack typing" doubles as the dispatcher. (2) **idempotent close avoids a stale-closure read** — the once-bound listener can't cheaply read current `helpOpen`, but `closeHelp` (set the seen-flag + setOpen(false)) is a no-op when already closed, so `Escape → closeHelp()` is always correct without reading state; making the action safe-to-repeat removes the need to gate it. (3) **honor the platform convention even when another close path exists** — the help already closed three ways (scrim, button, `?`), but `Escape` is the reflex every modal user reaches for; "it's closeable" isn't "it closes the way I expect." A tiny, standard, complete-the-UX brick. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 255.

## Phase 17t — Worth goal as a target line on the Fortune chart (2026-06-12)

282. **A goal is more motivating as a line you climb TOWARD than a number you read — put the target in the space where progress is already drawn.** The goal (17l) had a number, progress%, ETA, and a celebration, all in the WealthPanel; the Fortune chart already plots worth-over-time. Drawing the goal as a dashed line ABOVE the worth curve (folding it into the y-range) makes the gap spatial — you watch the worth line rise toward the target. Lessons: (1) **read shared state directly from its store to avoid a risky lift** — the goal lives in the WealthPanel's `usePref`; to show it on the chart WITHOUT lifting that state to App (churning the just-reviewed 17l/m/p + moving the toast effect), `WorthChart` reads `localStorage['ew-worth-goal']` directly each render. The chart re-renders every tick, so it tracks changes within a tick (a tiny paused-latency is fine for a chart). localStorage IS the shared store; sometimes the lowest-risk "sync" is to read the store, not to thread the state. (2) **fold a target into a range only when it won't destroy the data** — a goal far above your worth would compress the curve to a flat line at the bottom; the `goal <= dataMax * 3` cap means near targets chart (you see the climb) while far ones stay the WealthPanel's job (no useless flattened chart). A reference line is only worth drawing if the primary data survives the rescale. (3) **the same fact now lives on three surfaces, each suited to it** — the WealthPanel gives the number+ETA (precision), the toast gives the moment (acknowledgement), the chart gives the climb (spatial progress); a goal worth setting deserves to be felt in more than one register. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 254.

## Phase 17s — Clear the market filter (Esc + ✕) (2026-06-12)

281. **A "focus" verb implies a "clear" verb — finish the interaction you started.** 17n let you focus the filter with `/`, but clearing it still meant select-all-delete; `Esc`-to-clear (the universal search-box convention) + a `✕` button complete the loop. Lessons: (1) **an input handler and the global keydown listener don't collide because the listener exempts inputs** — the MarketTable's window listener (j/k/w/`/`) returns early on INPUT targets, so `Esc` had to be handled on the input's OWN `onKeyDown`; the two layers (global hotkeys when not typing, local key handling when focused) compose cleanly precisely because the global one yields to focus. (2) **show the affordance only when it's actionable** — the `✕` renders only when `filter !== ''`, so an empty filter shows nothing extra (zero added density on the common case), and its presence IS the signal "there's something to clear." (3) **reuse the ref you already added** — the `✕` refocuses via the `filterRef` 17n introduced for `/`, so clear-and-keep-typing works; one feature's plumbing (a ref to focus) serves the next (a ref to refocus after clear). A small, complete-the-UX brick. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 253.

## Phase 17r — A "compact" market-columns toggle (2026-06-12)

280. **The right response to "I made this dense" is a declutter CONTROL, not a column removal — give the choice back to the user.** The visual pass (#278) found the market table dense (10 columns, 4 of them decision lenses I added this session); it works (scrolls via `overflow:auto`), so the fix isn't to delete the columns I built — it's a "compact" toggle that hides the analysis lenses (mom/margin/band/swing/trend) for a clean price read, restorable when the player wants to analyse. Lessons: (1) **density is a preference axis, not a defect — so make it a setting** — a flipper-on-spread wants five columns, an analyst wants ten; rather than guess one default, the toggle lets each pick, which is the honest resolution of "is this too much?" (it's too much FOR SOME, sometimes). (2) **CSS `nth-child` hiding beats JSX conditionals for a whole-column show/hide — but it's position-fragile, so pin the indices with a real-browser test** — one `.market.compact ... :nth-child(5..9){display:none}` rule keeps the markup stable (no 10 `{!compact && …}` wraps), but jsdom doesn't compute `display` from a stylesheet, so the unit test can only assert the class toggles; the column-index correctness (does `nth-child(5)` == `mom`?) is verified by an e2e in a real browser (compact → `mom`/`margin` hidden, `bid`/`volume` shown). Choose the implementation by what's stable, then put the test where the risk actually lives. (3) **route post-assessment effort INTO the flagged surface** — having SEEN the table is crowded, the disciplined move was to improve THAT (a declutter control), not to add a 22nd feature elsewhere; an assessment that doesn't change what you build next was wasted. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 252.

## Phase 17q — Keyboard speed control (, / .) (2026-06-12)

279. **Let the visual assessment STEER the next brick, not just certify the last — it said "table's full," so build elsewhere.** Fresh off seeing the dense MarketTable (FINDINGS #278), the obvious next move (a `movers` filter track to complete the 17o momentum tooling) would have added a 9th chip to an already-crowded filter row. Instead I built keyboard speed control — `,`/`.` step the world through 1×/5×/20× — which adds zero visual density and completes the core idle-loop pacing on the keyboard (`p` pauses; these adjust). Lessons: (1) **a density finding is a routing signal** — once you've SEEN that one surface is full, the discipline is to direct new work away from it; the most "consistent" addition (another track, mirroring cheap/steady/watched) was exactly the wrong one because consistency was piling onto the saturated spot. Let where-it-goes be informed by where-there's-room. (2) **map to the convention players carry** — `,`/`.` for slower/faster is the media/YouTube speed idiom (and `<`/`>` engraved on the keys read as "slower/faster"), so like `/`-for-search (17n) it needs no teaching beyond the help line. (3) **the speed-step is a pure function with a deliberate asymmetry** — `stepSpeed` clamps within the live tiers, but a non-live speed (paused=0) resumes at 1× on FASTER and no-ops on SLOWER, because "speed up from paused" means "start" while "slow down from paused" means nothing; encoding that asymmetry in one pure, unit-tested helper keeps the App dispatch a one-liner. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 251.

## Visual assessment + README screenshot refresh (2026-06-12, post-17p)

278. **After 20 code-only bricks, LOOK at the thing — a screenshot is the review a test suite can't run.** The session shipped 20 UI bricks (4 new MarketTable columns, the watchstar, the goal widget, tooltips) all verified by unit/e2e tests + two adversarial reviews, but every check read CODE; none saw PIXELS. Captured a fresh `docs/screenshot.png` (the committed one was 20 bricks / 9 days stale) at 1280-wide and assessed it. Verdict: the UI is healthy — coherent dark-stone/gold theme, organized 3-column board (market · ticket · depth · ledger/wealth/leaderboard), nothing clipped or broken; the market table is DENSE (10 columns in a ~400px column) but readable. Lessons: (1) **tests and reviews certify correctness, not appearance — density, alignment, overflow, contrast are invisible to a green suite**, so a periodic visual pass is the only thing that catches a layout regression from incremental additions; "414 tests pass" said nothing about whether the 10th column crushed the table (it didn't, but only the screenshot could confirm it). (2) **a public-facing asset goes stale silently** — the README hero image hadn't been regenerated since before the session, so the project's front door showed none of the work; an asset that depicts the product needs a refresh cadence tied to the product's change rate, or it quietly misrepresents it. (3) **seeing it is also permission to stop forcing** — the assessment confirmed the game is visually complete and feature-saturated, which is itself a finding: the right move became refreshing the stale asset, not inventing a 21st marginal panel onto an already-full board. Docs/asset only, no code change → no redeploy.

## Phase 17p — Review (17i–17o) + reset goalHit on clear (2026-06-12)

277. **A "MEDIUM" that has no observable behaviour is still worth fixing — orphaned persisted state is a trap primed for a future reader.** The review flagged that clearing a worth goal (`setGoal(0)`) left `ew-goal-hit` set in localStorage. Trying to write a behaviour test for the fix revealed there ISN'T one: `commitGoal` re-derives `goalHit` on every set, so the orphan never changes current behaviour. Lessons: (1) **"can I write a failing test?" is the right question for sizing a bug — and a "no" can still mean "fix it"** — the orphan is invisible TODAY (the effect guards on `goal > 0`, and the next set overwrites `goalHit`), so no behavioural test distinguishes fixed-vs-not; the correct test asserts the persisted STATE (`ew-goal-hit` → 0 after clear), because the defect IS the state, not a behaviour. When a finding is "dead/inconsistent state," test the state, not an output. (2) **the danger of orphaned state is the future reader who trusts it** — `ew-goal-hit` says "this goal was celebrated"; left pointing at a goal that no longer exists, any later feature that reads it independent of `ew-worth-goal` inherits a lie. Cleaning paired state together (clear the goal AND its hit-marker) keeps the invariant "hit-marker refers to the current goal" true, so future code can rely on it. (3) **document the contract a helper silently assumes** — `deedEta` recovers `threshold = worth/progress`, which is only valid because every `paceMetric: 'worth'` deed's `progress` is exactly `worth/threshold`; that assumption was load-bearing and unwritten, so a comment now pins it (a future worth-deed with a different progress shape would otherwise yield a garbage ETA with no error). (4) the review otherwise certified 17i–17o SOUND — goal toast fires once and survives reload, ETA math guards div-by-zero, the `mom`/`/` additions keep column counts aligned and active-gating intact. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 250.

## Phase 17o — A sortable momentum (vs-EMA) column (2026-06-12)

276. **The data for the 4th decision axis was already on screen — just not as magnitude or sortable.** The `last` cell already colored green/red vs EMA, so the market encoded DIRECTION; but a binary tint isn't "how much" and can't be sorted, so "what's moving most right now?" still meant scanning. A `mom` column (`(last−ema)/ema` %) turns the existing tint into a sortable magnitude. Lessons: (1) **a color is a hint; a number you can sort is a tool** — the same underlying fact (last vs EMA) was present as a row-level cue but useless for the cross-market question ("rank everything by movement"); promoting an encoded hint to a first-class sortable value is a real capability gain, not a duplicate. When a table colors a cell by some derived quantity, that quantity often deserves to be sortable too. (2) **it completes a deliberately-built axis set** — margin (profit) · band (value) · swing (risk) · mom (direction) are four orthogonal lenses a trader weighs, each the sortable form of a per-item read; naming the set makes the gap (no direction axis) obvious and the addition principled rather than ad-hoc. (3) **fit-for-this-market**: prices here mostly hover at EMA except during events (the economy anchors at the cost floor per FINDINGS), so a momentum sort surfaces precisely the event-driven movers — the column is most useful exactly when the player most needs it (an active craze/crash), complementing the event chips (which say WHICH item, not the magnitude). (4) reused the margin/band/swing column scaffolding verbatim (SortKey + numOf branch + header + cell, nulls sink), so the Nth sortable column is a four-line pattern. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 249.

## Phase 17n — `/` focuses the market filter (2026-06-12)

275. **Lean on the conventions players already carry — `/` means "search" everywhere, so it costs zero learning.** The market filter existed but needed a mouse click; binding `/` to focus it makes finding one of 128 goods instant, and because `/`-to-search is a web-wide convention (GitHub, Slack, Gmail, vim), there's nothing to teach. Lessons: (1) **a shortcut that reuses a universal convention is free UX** — unlike a bespoke key, `/` for search needs no discovery (though the placeholder hint and help line still document it); when adding a hotkey, prefer the one users already expect over an arbitrary mnemonic. (2) **the keydown infra built across the session absorbed it for nothing** — the MarketTable's `active`-gated, typing-guarded, navRef-fresh listener (j/k 12g → b/s → w 17c) just needed one more branch + a `filterRef`; a well-factored handler makes the Nth shortcut trivial, and the existing typing-guard means `/` correctly does nothing once you're in an input. (3) **focus IS testable in jsdom** — `document.activeElement` after a `fireEvent.keyDown` confirms the focus moved, so even a "jump to input" interaction gets a real assertion (active-gated: `/` over an inactive Exchange leaves focus alone). The keyboard trade loop is now complete: `/` find → j/k walk → b/s side → Enter submit → w watch. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 248.

## Phase 17m — Celebrate reaching your worth goal (2026-06-12)

274. **The same "fires when a value crosses a threshold" mechanic is sound or unsound depending on WHICH threshold — collision frequency is the deciding factor.** I ruled out a "you can farm deeper" toast (17h) because it fires exactly when a level-up fires (strength crossing = a level-up tick), so it would always be clobbered by or clobber the level-up toast. The goal-reached toast (17m) is the SAME shape — fire when worth crosses a number — yet it's fine, because the number is CUSTOM, so it rarely coincides with a deed/level tick. Lessons: (1) **"will this toast collide?" is answerable from the trigger's correlation with other triggers, not the toast mechanism** — two features can share an identical implementation and one ships while the other doesn't, decided entirely by whether their fire-conditions are correlated with existing fires. Before adding a toast, ask what ELSE fires on the same tick its condition flips. (2) **a fire-once-across-reload guard needs PERSISTENCE keyed on the thing achieved** — an in-memory ref fires once per session but re-fires every reload (the goal persists, so a returning over-goal save would re-toast). Persisting `goalHit = the goal value already celebrated` and firing only when `goalHit !== goal` makes it once-ever-per-goal and reload-safe — the same delete-and-save discipline as the duel (16v), expressed as a stored sentinel rather than a deleted field. (3) **guard the degenerate "set a goal you've already passed" at SET time, not fire time** — `commitGoal` records `goalHit = worth >= n ? n : 0`, so setting an already-met target marks it celebrated (no hollow instant toast) while a genuine unmet target stays fireable; handling the edge where you define the goal is cleaner than special-casing it where you'd fire. (4) **firing from a child's effect makes offline-crossing free** — WealthPanel re-renders every tick (App `force()`) and reads the live `worth` prop, so the effect catches a live crossing AND the first post-offline-catch-up render with no extra plumbing, unlike a refreshProgress hook that would need a dedicated offline call-site (the 16v multi-site problem). UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Completes the goal loop: set (17l) → track (17l) → celebrate (17m). Brick 247.

## Phase 17l — Set your own worth goal (2026-06-12)

273. **Fixed goals motivate; a goal you CHOSE motivates more — give agency on top of the prescribed ladder.** The deeds (and their 17k ETAs) are the game's prescribed worth targets, but a player often has a SPECIFIC self-set number — "200k for the next slot," "500k before I restart" — that no deed marks. 17l adds a settable target with the same progress+ETA treatment, persisted. Lessons: (1) **the third projection target was free because 17k/11w built the machinery** — `worthRate` + the `(target−worth)/perMin` math + `fmtDuration(eta*60)` formatting now serve three consumers (next-round-number 11w, worth-deeds 17k, custom goal 17l); once a projection is generalized, pointing it at a new target is a thin helper (`goalView`) + a render. (2) **agency = persistence + clearability** — a goal the player sets must survive reload (`usePref('ew-worth-goal')`, like the watchlist/loadouts) and be easy to drop (the ✕), or it's a toy not a tool; the set/clear pair is what makes it theirs. (3) **a read-only panel can take ONE interactive affordance without becoming a form** — the WealthPanel was display-only; a single compact target input + set/clear is a justified exception (goals are a wealth concept) that doesn't turn it into a settings screen. (4) **format-agnostic assertions survive the helpers they don't own** — the render test first asserted the literal "100,000", but `fmtCompact(100000)` is "100K" (compact notation ≥10k); matching `/100[Kk]|100,000/` instead pins the intent (the target shows) without coupling to fmtCompact's exact output — a test should assert its own feature, not re-derive a dependency's formatting. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 246.

## Phase 17k — ETA to your next worth-milestone (2026-06-12)

272. **A progress bar says how far; a finish-line ETA says how long — and "how long" is the motivating half.** The MilestonesPanel showed deeds at "50%", but a fraction doesn't pull you forward the way "≈2h to gp Millionaire" does. The WorthChart already projected "≈{time} to {next round number}" from `worthRate`; 17k extends that same projection to NAMED deeds, right where the deed sits. Lessons: (1) **reuse a proven projection rather than inventing one** — the worth-rate ETA was already shipped and trusted (WorthChart 11w); the deed ETA is the identical `(threshold − worth)/perMin` math with the identical `fmtDuration(eta*60)` formatting, so it inherits the established unit (tick-minutes) and presentation, not a new dubious metric. When adding a forward projection, find the one the app already makes and point it at a new target. (2) **the units must match the goal — so TAG which goals qualify** — `worthRate` is gp/min, valid ONLY for deeds whose progress IS a worth fraction; a combat deed's "% to 25 kills" has nothing to do with gp/min. A `paceMetric: 'worth'` tag on the 5 worth deeds gates the ETA to where it's meaningful, sidestepping the 13s gp-vs-worth-mismatch trap by construction. The lesson generalizes: a projection helper is only correct for inputs in its unit; make eligibility explicit (a tag), don't apply it blindly to anything with a `progress`. (3) **derive what you can instead of storing it** — the deed never stored its raw worth threshold (it's baked into `achieved`/`progress`), but `threshold = worth/progress` recovers it from the same progress the bar already computes — provided you pass the RAW (unfloored) progress, not the display `pct`. A value computed for display (floored %) is lossy; thread the precise source value to anything that does math on it. (4) the honest read: this is genuinely additive (a named finish line with a time), not polish — the kind of forward-looking hook worth building even at saturation. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 245.

## Phase 17j — Make the market decision tools discoverable (2026-06-12)

271. **A powerful-but-hidden tool might as well not exist — at feature saturation, discoverability IS the feature.** Across the session I built a deep market toolkit — sortable margin/band/swing/volume columns, flippable/cheap/steady/gear/watched filter tracks — but a player learns the game from the help overlay and from hovering, and NEITHER taught these: the help's decision item stopped at value-bands/alerts, and 4 of 8 track chips were bare. So the right brick at saturation wasn't another tool; it was surfacing the ones that exist. Lessons: (1) **building a tool and teaching it are separate tasks, and the second is easy to skip** — twelve bricks added capability without ever updating the one place (help) a new player reads to learn capability; periodically audit "would a new player even know this exists?" against the entry-point surfaces (help, tooltips, empty states), because shipped ≠ discoverable. (2) **amend the existing teaching surface, don't bolt on a new one** — the help's "decide with the numbers" item was the natural home for "the table sorts by X and filters to Y," so one appended sentence beats a 14th list item that fragments the same topic; keep one concept in one place. (3) **a tooltip is the cheapest in-context teacher** — the cheap/watched/steady chips already self-explained on hover; giving staples/exotics/gear/flippable the same `title` means every lens now answers "what does this do?" exactly where the user's pointer already is, no overlay trip. (4) the honest read at saturation: when the audit keeps returning "already shipped" (depth ladder marks your orders, bestiary shows drops, empty states hint), the remaining value is in polish + discoverability, and naming that plainly beats forcing a marginal mechanic. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 244.

## Phase 17i — Adversarial review of the session + gate the readiness compute (2026-06-12)

270. **Twelve green bricks still earn a Review step — and the review's payoff was confidence, plus one honest perf fix.** After 16w–17h shipped (all UI-only, all tested), the adversarial-reviewer traced the named failure modes — per-tick hot-path cost, the celebration swap-guards (the 14e/14f false-fire class), stale closures in the once-bound keyboard listener, the deed-fix's latched-save safety, the filter predicates' missing-data handling — and returned **SOUND**. Lessons: (1) **a passing test suite and a clean review answer different questions** — the suite proves the behaviours I thought to assert; the review proves the behaviours I DIDN'T think about (could the elite toast false-fire on a cloud-adopt? does `awayDeeds` reset at every `beginOffline` call site? is `swings` declared before `inTrack` uses it?). Both are needed; "tests pass" is not "reviewed." (2) **the one real finding was a hoist that helped per-render but not per-component-state** — 17g moved `diveReadiness` to the top of EmbarkPanel ("computed once"), but ABOVE the `if (agent?.expedition) return null` early return, so it scanned every region via `combatForecast` on every render even while DIVING (panel renders nothing). "Computed once per render" was true; "computed only when used" wasn't. Moving the two pure consts below the early return is free on the render path and zero work while diving. The lesson: when you hoist for "compute once," check WHICH early-exit paths now run the compute needlessly — hoisting above a guard trades one redundancy (per-IIFE) for another (per-null-render). (3) **the reviewer correctly declined the MEDIUM** — `newElites` allocating a fresh filter array every tick is the same shape as the pre-existing `checkMilestones`/`Object.entries(alerts)` per-tick loops, not a regression, and not worth a fast-path absent a profile; a good review distinguishes "new hazard" from "pre-existing texture," and so should the fix list. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 243.

## Phase 17h — A "steady" market filter track (2026-06-12)

269. **A filter and a sort over the same metric serve different intents — removing the noise ≠ ordering it.** 16y let you SORT by swing (steadiest first), so "isn't that enough?" was the real question for adding a "steady" FILTER. It isn't: sorting still renders the wild markets (you scroll past them), while a risk-averse flipper's actual want is to SEE ONLY the calm, tradeable ones — the filter removes the noise from view, the sort just sequences it. Lessons: (1) **before building a filter that overlaps a sort, name the intent each serves** — "find the calmest" (sort, you still see all) vs "work only within the calm set" (filter, the rest vanishes); they coexist because they answer different questions, and dismissing the filter as "redundant with the sort" conflates the two. (2) **"steady" = calm AND liquid, by construction** — `priceSwing` needs ≥2 recent trades to have a read, so an untraded item has no read and is excluded; that's correct, not a gap (an unmeasured market isn't a "steady flip," it's an unflippable one), so the predicate `swings.get(id)?.read === 'steady'` naturally encodes both conditions. (3) the swings map computed for the 16y column was already in hand, so the track was one predicate line — the same "one feature leaves scaffolding for the next" payoff as 17d→17f. Completes the volatility tooling: the per-item ticket read (14p) → the sortable column (16y) → the filter track (17h). UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 242.

## Phase 17g — Mark the recommended dive region on the RegionMap (2026-06-12)

268. **Put the advice where the action is — a recommendation in prose, separate from the control it's about, makes the user do the mapping.** 16x's readiness readout said "your safe depth is {region}" in text, but the RegionMap right above it — where you actually CLICK a region to embark — gave no hint which node that was; you read a name, then hunted for it on the map. A green "dive here" ring on the recommended node closes the text→action gap. Lessons: (1) **a textual recommendation and the interactive surface it refers to should be visually linked** — the same fact (deepest favored region) now appears as both a sentence AND a marker on the thing you click, so the eye goes straight from "where should I go?" to the node to tap. When advice names a target that's also pickable elsewhere on screen, mark the target, don't just name it. (2) **compute the shared fact once, feed both surfaces** — `diveReadiness` had been recomputed inside the readout's IIFE; hoisting it to panel scope let the map ring and the text read the SAME `readiness.ready`, so they can never disagree (and it's one call, not two). A value consumed by two sibling elements belongs at their common parent, computed once. (3) **a new ring coexists with the old by geometry + colour** — the recommended ring (green, dashed, r=15) sits OUTSIDE the gold selection ring (r=13) and reads differently, so when the recommended region is also the selected one both show concentrically without clashing; distinguish overlapping markers by radius AND hue, not just one. (4) the marker is gated `recommended >= 0`, so the "outmatched" case (`ready === -1`) and a map rendered without readiness both ring nothing — the same null-respecting discipline as the text readout's three branches. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 241.

## Phase 17f — A "watched" market filter track (2026-06-12)

267. **Add → see → focus: a list feature isn't done until you can collapse the view to just it.** 17c (`w`) let you build the watchlist and 17d (★) let you see it inline, but ACTING on it still meant scanning past 127 other rows; the "watched" track collapses the market to your starred handful — the third and final beat. Lessons: (1) **the data threaded for one feature unlocks the next at near-zero cost** — 17d had already passed the `watched` Set into the MarketTable for the per-row star, so the filter track was a one-line predicate (`watched?.has(id)`) + a chip, no new wiring; building features in an arc means each leaves the scaffolding for the one after. (2) **mirror the established pattern exactly** — the cheap/flippable/gear tracks set the predicate + chip shape, so "watched" slotted into the same `inTrack` switch and button array; a new member of an existing family should be indistinguishable in structure from its siblings, which makes it trivially correct and trivially testable (the test is a copy of the cheap-track test). (3) **a feature arc is also a discoverability arc — update the entry-point copy** — the WatchlistPanel's empty hint said "star an item from the ticket," but by now there are three ways in (the row ★, the ticket star, `w`); refreshing that one line so the onboarding hint matches the shipped surface keeps the help honest as the feature grows. A multi-brick arc should end by reconciling the first-touch copy with everything it added. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 240.

## Phase 17e — Event "ending soon" urgency cue (2026-06-12)

266. **A countdown is data; "act now" is a decision — surface the threshold, not just the number.** Event chips showed a raw "N left" tick countdown, but nothing told the player WHEN the trading window was actually closing — you could ride an event position past the dislocation's peak and watch the edge revert. A `.ending` amber+⏳ state on the chip in the final stretch (≤150 ticks) converts the number into a verdict. Lessons: (1) **a raw metric asks the user to do the math; a marked threshold does it for them** — "1,847 left" vs "412 left" both look like "lots of time" until you know the event runs 800–2000 ticks; flagging the last stretch encodes the "is this closing?" judgment the bare countdown left to the player. Pair the exact number (still useful) WITH the categorical cue. (2) **absolute beat fractional here** — events vary 800–2000 ticks, but the player's real constraint is "do I have time to react," which is a wall-clock-ish quantity, so a fixed "≤150 ticks to act" reads consistently; a fractional "final quarter" would mean 200 ticks for one event and 500 for another, i.e. inconsistent urgency for the same label. Choose the threshold basis (absolute vs proportional) by what the user is actually deciding. (3) **completes a lifecycle already half-built** — the begin chip (15l, clickable) and the end recap (15z) existed; the ending cue is the middle beat (begin → closing → ended), so the event now narrates its whole arc rather than just appearing and silently reverting. When a feature has a start and an end surface, check for the missing middle. (4) **mark in place, don't re-sort** — urgency-sorting the chips would make them jump position every tick as countdowns cross the threshold; a conditional class keeps order stable while still drawing the eye. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 239.

## Phase 17d — Per-row watch ★ in the MarketTable (2026-06-12)

265. **A toggle the user can't SEE on the surface they use it on is half a feature.** 17c added `w` to watch the loaded item, but the MarketTable — where you actually scan 128 rows — showed zero indication of what's watched, and `w`'s only feedback was a 4s toast. 17d puts a gold ★ on every watched row (and a hollow ☆ that brightens on hover for the rest), clickable for mouse parity. Lessons: (1) **the keyboard action and its visible state belong on the same surface** — a toast confirms the moment, but the persistent question ("which of these am I watching?") needs a marker right in the table; the pair (transient toast + persistent row star) is the same "tell me / remind me" complementarity as 16r's tab dots. (2) **reuse the conditional row-marker slot you already have** — the star sits in the name cell beside the existing ⚡ (event) and ◆ (your-best-offer) markers, same pattern, so it cost a prop (`watched`) and a button, no new layout. (3) **an accessible per-item control re-triggers the loose-locator collision — this time via aria-label, not visible text**: the star's `aria-label="watch {item}"` made `getByRole('button', { name: new RegExp(itemName) })` ambiguous (it now matched both the event chip AND the star), breaking an unrelated event-chip test. The fix disambiguates by a property only the real target has (the chip carries `img.itemimg`, the star doesn't) — `getAllByRole(...).find(b => b.querySelector('img.itemimg'))`. The #147/#150 collision class isn't only about visible text: any accessible NAME you add (aria-label) widens the net of role+name queries, so adding a labelled control means auditing `getByRole({name})` matchers too, not just `getByText`. (4) `stopPropagation` on the star's click keeps it from also firing the row's select/load — a control nested in a clickable container must stop the bubble or it does two things at once. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 238.

## Phase 17c — `w` to watch the selected item (2026-06-12)

264. **Finish the loop you started — a keyboard-driven trade flow with one mouse-only verb isn't keyboard-driven.** The flow was j/k (select) → b/s (side) → Enter (submit), but WATCHING still meant reaching for the mouse. `w` (toggle the watchlist on the loaded item) closes the gap. Lessons: (1) **put the shortcut where the live state already lives, not where the registry is** — the obvious home was `resolveShortcut` + the App's global keydown (where 1/2/3/p/? live), but that handler binds ONCE with empty deps, so it would capture a STALE `selected`/`watch`; the MarketTable's keydown already uses a `navRef` refreshed every render (for j/k), so `w` reads the current selection and a fresh `onToggleWatch` for free. A shortcut that acts on frequently-changing state belongs with the handler that already tracks that state freshly, even if a "shortcut registry" exists elsewhere — co-locate with the freshness, don't centralize into staleness. (2) **a fresh callback per render IS the ref-freshness mechanism** — App passes a NEW `onToggleWatch` closure each render (closing over the current `watch` to pick the toast direction), and because MarketTable copies props into `navRef.current` every render, the once-bound listener always calls the latest closure. Passing a new function each render is usually a re-render smell, but paired with a per-render ref copy it's exactly how you give a stable listener access to changing state. (3) **an invisible toggle needs a confirmation** — `w` has no per-row indicator in the market table, so without feedback you couldn't tell it worked; the toast ("★ watching {item}" / "☆ unwatched {item}") is the acknowledgement, with the ticket/WatchlistPanel star as the persistent state. A keyboard action on a surface that doesn't show the toggled state must announce itself. (4) reused the j/k test's exact shape (active-gating + typing-guard) — pressing `w` over the market calls the toggle, `w` in the filter input does not, `w` with `active=false` does not. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 237.

## Phase 17b — Fix Dragon Slayer to need an actual dragon kill (2026-06-12)

263. **"Jesse-gated" deserves a second look — the save-compat analysis turned a flagged design call into a safe bug-fix.** 17a discovered `dragon-slayer` fires off `ledger.itemsMinted['superior_dragon_bones'] > 0` (a producer mints those bones within ticks, so the deed auto-completes with no kill) and I logged it as Jesse-gated ("changes when a deed fires / save-compat"). Re-examined: `checkMilestones` only evaluates UNLATCHED deeds, so every save that already has dragon-slayer KEEPS it — the new criterion only affects games that DON'T yet have it, which is strictly more correct. Deeds are UI-only (`game.milestones`, not WorldState) → no verified-score impact, no redeploy. Lessons: (1) **"is this safe to change?" is answerable by tracing the data flow, not by gut caution** — the fear was "changing a deed's criteria breaks existing players," but latched-list semantics (you only ever ADD to `milestones`, and `achieved` gates only the add) means tightening a criterion can't un-earn anything. A precise mechanism check (who reads this, when, and can it regress?) is what distinguishes a real design gate from reflexive conservatism. (2) **the wrong proxy hid behind a plausible comment** — "Only dragons mint superior dragon bones — the kill is in the ledger" reads as authoritative but encodes an assumption (only dragon kills mint bones) the seeded producer economy violates; the deed had been auto-firing (and mis-toasting "Dragon Slayer!" seconds in) since it shipped. A comment asserting a fact is not the fact; the only check that caught it was forcing the away-deed feature (17a) through real ticked state. (3) **re-key on the EVENT, not a downstream artifact** — the deed means "you slew a dragon," so it should read your dragon KILLS (`killsByMonster` for `*_dragon` ids), not an item that happens to correlate (bones, which the economy also produces). When a deed/achievement keys on a side-effect instead of the act, any other producer of that side-effect false-triggers it. (4) `DRAGON_IDS = MONSTERS.filter(m => m.id.endsWith('_dragon'))` is self-maintaining (a future `red_dragon` counts automatically) — derive the set from the data, don't hardcode the list. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 236.

## Phase 17a — Deeds earned while away (away-bar) (2026-06-12)

262. **"Earned while away" must mean CROSSED during the gap, not merely achievable at boot — and proving the difference exposed a latent deed bug.** No `checkMilestones` runs during the offline gap, so a deed the clerk grew your worth past while away latched silently (the boot/catch-up `checkMilestones` returns were discarded). Surfacing them in the away-bar ("🏅 earned while away: …") seemed trivial — capture the return — but the first test exposed that a naive capture credits the away period with deeds that were ALREADY achievable before you left. Lessons: (1) **a "what changed while away" feature needs a BEFORE baseline, not just an AFTER read** — the fix is a pre-accrual `checkMilestones` (latch + discard) on the pre-gap state, THEN accrue, THEN capture; only deeds newly crossed by the accrual remain. The test that pins it hires the clerk BEFORE leaving and asserts `hired-help` does NOT appear (achievable pre-gap) while the clerk-rested `first-offer` DOES (crossed during the gap). Any "delta since X" surface needs the X snapshot taken before the change, or it reports the whole state as new. (2) **building the test surfaced a real pre-existing bug**: `dragon-slayer`'s `achieved` reads `ledger.itemsMinted['superior_dragon_bones'] > 0`, but a PRODUCER mints those bones within ticks of world creation (measured 0→64 by tick 100), so the deed fires off the ECONOMY, not a dragon kill — it already mis-toasts "Dragon Slayer!" in live play seconds in, with no kill. The comment ("Only dragons mint superior dragon bones — the kill is in the ledger") encodes an assumption the seeded producer economy violates. Logged, not fixed here (changing a deed's criteria is a deliberate balance/save-compat decision, not a side-effect of an unrelated brick) → NEXT_STEPS. The lesson: **a test that forces a feature through real engine state is also an audit of the assumptions that state encodes** — the away-deed test couldn't help but reveal dragon-slayer's mis-firing. (3) **the artificial test case ≠ the real flow**: a fresh tick-0 world + immediate offline DOES surface dragon-slayer (bones not yet minted at the pre-gap snapshot), but real flow can't reach it — a fresh game has no `lastSeenMs` until it's saved, and saving follows live ticks that already latch the economy deeds; so the realistic test warms the world + latches first. When a test fails on an edge the product can't actually hit, fix the TEST's realism rather than contorting the feature. (4) reused the `settleDuel` multi-site shape (16v): the value (worth/deeds) advances on the offline path, so the check must run at every offline site (boot, catch-up finalize) — and the away-bar, not a transient toast, is the right home (persistent until dismissed, already the "while you were away" surface). UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 235.

## Phase 16z — Apex Predator, the elite capstone (2026-06-12)

261. **A "first-kill" salute and a "killed them all" trophy are different progression beats — the rare-event arc needed both.** 16w fired a one-shot toast the FIRST time each named elite fell; 16z adds the collective goal (the "Apex Predator" deed for felling all four distinct elites) and a tracker that COLLECTS the four bosses — scattered one-per-deep-region, so the conquest rosters only ever showed each in isolation — into one "☠ Named Elites N/4" strip. Lessons: (1) **transient acknowledgement ≠ persistent goal**: a celebration marks a moment as it happens; a deed + a progress surface give the player something to AIM at over many sessions and see how close they are. The same underlying event (an elite kill) wants both an in-the-moment salute (16w) and a standing target (16z) — shipping only the toast left the long-game hunter without a scoreboard. (2) **a set scattered across the UI deserves a collected view** — the four elites each live in a different region's conquest roster, so "how many of the four have I felled?" required visiting four rows and mentally tallying; one strip that gathers the set answers it at a glance. When a meaningful group is distributed across per-container views by the data model, add the cross-container collection. (3) **`ELITES` (the 16w roster helper) became the single source for BOTH the deed and the tracker** — the capstone's `every(e => kills[e.id]>0)` and the strip's lit/dim glyphs read the same list, so they can't disagree on who the elites are; a roster helper extracted for one feature pays off as the shared definition for the next. (4) **a new featured element near indexed siblings must NOT borrow their selector** — the tracker reuses the conquest-head/conquestbar/conquest-roster INNER classes for visual consistency but is deliberately `.elitehunt`, NOT `.conquest-row`, because existing tests do `querySelectorAll('.conquest-row')[0]` to grab region 0; a shared outer class would have shifted every region index by one. Reuse the inner presentation classes, give the new container its own identity, and pin a test that the old selector's count is unchanged. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 234.

## Phase 16y — Sortable swing column (market-wide volatility) (2026-06-12)

260. **A per-item read becomes a portfolio tool when it's the same metric across every row, sortable.** 14p put a recent-volatility read ("🟢 steady / 🟡 choppy / 🔴 wild") on the SELECTED item's ticket; 16y lifts the same `priceSwing` to a sortable MarketTable column, so the trader can rank the WHOLE market by choppiness — ascending for the steadiest spreads (safe to flip: the price holds while both legs fill), descending for the wildest (volatility plays). Lessons: (1) **the sortable-column family is a pattern worth completing deliberately** — margin (14d, profit), band (15q, value), and now swing (risk) are three orthogonal axes a flipper weighs, each the column form of an existing per-item read. Once you have two members of a family, the third is both obvious and high-value; a trading screen wants its key decision metrics ALL sortable, not one-off. (2) **reuse the data window you already built** — the swing map draws from the exact `sparks` price window the row sparkline already accumulates from `trades`, so the column added a `Map` and a `priceSwing` call per item, not a new data path; when adding a derived column, check whether the underlying series is already in hand (it was). (3) **compute the derived metric ONCE per render, not inside the sort comparator** — `numOf` is called O(n log n) times during sort, so I built `swings: Map<id, PriceSwing>` once and had both the comparator and the cell read it, rather than calling `priceSwing` inside `numOf` (which would recompute the window scan on every comparison). A sort key that's expensive to derive should be memoized before the sort, not derived in the `compare`. (4) **null sort-keys sink consistently** — untraded items (absent from `sparks`) map to `null`, and the existing `numOf` null-handling floats them to the bottom in either direction, so the steadiest/wildest sorts both keep real data on top without a special case. Reusing the established null-sink contract meant the new column inherited correct empty-handling for free. UI-only, no engine change, no new CSS → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 233.

## Phase 16x — Dive readiness advisor (2026-06-12)

259. **Unlocking a region and being strong enough to FARM it are different facts — the UI conflated them.** You unlock a region by clearing the one before it (3 kills), but foe power jumps each tier, so a freshly-unlocked deep region can flatten you — and the embark screen, which lets you SELECT any unlocked region, gave no global "how deep can I actually go?" read; you had to click each region to scan its forecast. `diveReadiness(you, frontier)` scans the unlocked regions and returns the DEEPEST one you're favored against vs its TYPICAL foe, surfaced as one line ("✓ ready / 📍 your safe depth is {region} / ⚠ outmatched"). Lessons: (1) **a per-item read doesn't add up to a portfolio read** — the per-region forecast (favored/risky for the SELECTED region) is necessary but not sufficient; the player's actual question is "where, across ALL my options, should I go?", which needs a helper that scans the set and returns the best, not N independent reads the user mentally maxes. When you have a per-X detail view, ask whether the across-all-X summary is the real decision. (2) **pick the right foe for the right question**: the existing forecast reads the region's HARDEST foe (worst-case survival for the selected region); readiness reads the TYPICAL foe (`regionTypical`, pool average) — because "can I reliably FARM here?" is about what you usually meet, not the rare elite. Same `combatForecast`, different foe input → complementary reads, not a duplicate. Naming which foe each read answers for kept them from collapsing into one. (3) **monotonic-by-design lets "deepest favored" BE the answer** — regions get harder with depth, so the deepest region where you're favored is a clean ready-depth (everything shallower is also favored); I scan and take the max favored index rather than assuming, but the design guarantee is what makes a single index meaningful. (4) **adding a line that names domain entities (region names) to an all-rooms-mounted panel re-triggers the #147/#150 collision class — twice in one brick**: the readiness line says "favored" (collided with a forecast test's `getByText(/favored|risky/)`) AND "clear {frontierName}" (collided with a region-picker test's `getByText('Lumbridge Plains')`). Fixes tighten the OTHER tests to their specific elements (scope `favored|risky` to the `forecast:` line's textContent; scope the region name to `selector: '.maplabel'`), never weaken the feature — any new text in a mounted panel means auditing loose matchers for BOTH the adjectives AND the proper nouns it introduces. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 232.

## Phase 16w — Elite first-kill celebration (2026-06-12)

258. **The rarest events deserved the loudest beat and had the quietest — the deep-tier bosses fell in silence.** The four named elites (Skarn/Vorkanth/Zukrath/Vessith) are the rarest kills in the game (deep-region-gated, ~10% spawn, jackpot loot), yet felling one for the FIRST time passed unmarked; the Bestiary recorded it but nothing celebrated the moment. 16w fires "☠ {elite} falls!" once per boss, reusing the level-up celebration's exact machinery: `newElites(slain, kills)` (pure — the elites with a kill not yet in the baseline Set) + an `eliteFired` Set guarded by an `eliteBaselineGame` game-identity ref, baselined on boot/swap to the elites already in `killsByMonster`. Lessons: (1) **audit your event surface against the RARITY of what it celebrates — saturation hits the common events first, leaving the rare-and-special ones uncovered.** Eight bricks of social/duel polish had piled celebration onto frequent moments (fills, alerts, levels) while the single most impressive kill in the game went unmarked. When picking the next feature at deep saturation, ask "what's the most special thing that STILL has no acknowledgement?" — rarity is an under-served axis. (2) **the offline-silent rule is a PATTERN, not a per-feature choice** — baseline-on-boot to current kills means an elite felled in a prior session (or by a sellsword while away) is adopted silently, exactly like the level/region baselines; only a kill landed live this session fires. The duel (16v) fires offline *because it's a meta-goal*, but a kill is an in-world event and follows the in-world celebrations' rule. Knowing which bucket a new event falls in (in-world → boot-silent, meta-goal → settle-offline) is a reusable decision, not a fresh judgement each time. (3) **the right test trigger was the existing fast-forward, not fake timers** — `+1k` runs `refreshProgress`, so a render test baselines on the first click, injects a kill into the shared game object, clicks again, and asserts the salute — no `vi.useFakeTimers` (which no test in the suite uses and which fights React effect scheduling). The pure `newElites` carries the logic (4 units); the render test only proves the wiring fires and the boot baseline suppresses an adopted kill. (4) **checked the clobber surface before placing the toast** — celebrations are last-writer-wins, so I verified no deed reads a single elite's `killsByMonster` (the elite deed reads the separate `eliteSlain` aggregate; the bestiary/conquest deeds need full rosters) before ordering elite below level/region but above deed/duel; a coincident deed or duel-win, both rarer/meta, still win the slot. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 231.

## Phase 16v — Win the duel offline too (settle at every worth-update) (2026-06-12)

257. **In an idle game, a "did my number cross the threshold?" check has to run wherever the NUMBER changes — and worth changes OFFLINE.** 16t fired the duel win only in `refreshProgress` (live ticks), but the auto-flipping clerk grows your worth during offline catch-up, so beating a duel while the tab was closed was silent and left a stale "beat {worth}" banner on a target you'd already passed. `settleDuel(g)` now runs at every worth-update site: live, boot-after-`beginOffline`, and the chunked catch-up finalize. Lessons: (1) **the idle loop is a second clock — any event keyed on a value that the offline path advances must also be checked on the offline path.** I'd put the trigger where the value changes ON-SCREEN (the tick handler) and forgotten the value also changes OFF-screen (catch-up). For an idle game, "on every tick" is NOT "on every change"; enumerate the worth-update sites (live, boot, catch-up) and settle at each. (2) **idempotent-by-construction is what makes "call it at N sites" safe** — `settleDuel` deletes the target and saves, so the target is GONE after the first call; the other sites then no-op. Designing the settle to be self-clearing (not guarded by an external fired-flag) means scattering the call can't double-fire — the idempotency lives in the action, not in remembering whether it ran. (3) **a `const` arrow referenced inside an effect defined EARLIER in the body is fine** — the effect's callback closes over the variable and runs post-render, by which point the `const` is assigned; tsc accepts it (no TDZ at call time). Source order constrains top-level use, not closure capture — so a shared handler can live near its primary caller and still be used by earlier effects. (4) tested the exact gap: a save whose starting worth already clears a tiny target (standing in for an offline-grown run) fires the win on BOOT and drops the banner — the boot path is the one 16t missed, so that's the one to pin. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 230.

## Phase 16t — The duel, raced (accept → beat → celebrate) (2026-06-12)

256. **A target shown once isn't a duel — it has to RIDE the run and pay off when you beat it.** 16s showed the challenge target in the bar, but the bar vanishes on accept, so the duel was invisible during play and beating it was silent. 16t persists the target on the run (`Game.duelTarget`), shows it in a banner ("⚔ dueling {handle} — beat {worth}, you: {now}") while live, and fires "🏆 You beat {handle}!" when your worth clears it. Lessons: (1) **a goal needs three beats to be a goal: SET it, SHOW it, and ACKNOWLEDGE clearing it** — 16s only set it; without the persistent show (a banner you race) and the payoff (a celebration), the mechanic was a number on a transient bar, not a duel. When you add a "target" feature, check that all three beats exist or it's inert. (2) **`Game.duelTarget` is the right home: a UI field on the wrapper, not WorldState** — it persists (survives reload, resume the duel) and is cloud-synced, but it's NOT in the engine state, so it's replay-NEUTRAL (no redeploy) — the same category as `ghost`/`milestones`. The line is: anything that changes the SIMULATION goes in WorldState (replay-affecting); anything that's the player's META-progress/UI rides `Game`. (3) **fire-once = `delete` + save, so a tick AND a reload can't re-fire** — clearing the ref-on-the-game (not a separate fired-Set) means the persisted save already reflects "won," so reopening doesn't re-celebrate; persisting the cleared state is what makes the one-shot survive a reload, where a transient `useRef` fired-flag would not. (4) `duelWon` is a one-line `worth >= target.worth` but named + tested (tie counts as a win, undefined = never) so the celebration trigger has a single pinned definition — the same thin-but-named discipline as `alertHit`/`bandAlertHit`. Completes the duel mechanic 16s opened: a shared link is now a goal you can SEE yourself racing and a moment when you WIN it. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 228.

## Phase 16s — "Beat my score" challenge links (2026-06-12)

255. **A shared link that says "play this world" is half a challenge — encoding the sharer's score turns it into a DUEL.** The challenge link (and the brag's link) carried only `#seed=N`; adding `&w=worth&by=handle` lets the recipient see "⚔ {handle} dares you to beat {worth} on seed N," closing the brag→duel→brag-back loop that is the actual competitive-viral mechanic. Lessons: (1) **the data to make a share competitive was already at the share site** — the player's worth and handle are right there when they hit "share"; encoding them costs one URL param each and converts a passive "here's a world" into an active "here's a target." When a share could carry a CHALLENGE, the challenge is usually free (the sharer's own stats). (2) **a tolerant parser is a prerequisite for evolving a URL format** — `parseChallengeSeed`'s `$`-anchored regex (`^#seed=(\d+)$`) would have REJECTED the new `&w=…` links, silently breaking them; loosening it to `(?:&|$)` first (and keeping the old bare-link tests green) is what let the format grow without breaking existing links. Anchored-to-end patterns are brittle against future params — anchor to a boundary, not the end. (3) **claimed vs verified is a deliberate honesty line**: the link's `w=` is a CLAIM ("I got 12k, beat me"), NOT proof — the replay-verified leaderboard remains the truth. Naming that in the type (`ChallengeTarget` = "the CLAIMED figure") keeps the social-bragging feature from masquerading as a verification one; a friendly duel and an anti-cheat score are different things and shouldn't share a trust level. (4) round-tripped through the parsers in one test (`challengeLink` → `parseChallengeSeed` + `parseChallengeTarget`) including the `encodeURIComponent`/decode of a handle with a space — a build-then-parse round-trip is the right test for a serialization format, catching encoding bugs a one-way assertion misses. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 227.

## Phase 16r — Tab activity badges (cross-room awareness) (2026-06-12)

254. **A transient toast and a persistent dot are complements, not duplicates — one says it NOW, the other says it's STILL there to see.** Toasts are last-writer-wins and 4s; heads-down on the Adventure tab you can miss "your order filled" (Exchange) or "a deed earned" (Hall). A tab dot, set when an event fires off-tab and cleared on visiting, is the cue that waits for you. Lessons: (1) **don't conflate "notified" with "noticed"** — a notification system that only fires transiently assumes the user is looking; for events on a tab they're NOT on, you also need a state that persists until acknowledged. The pair (toast + badge) covers both "tell me" and "remind me," and the badge's clear-on-visit is the acknowledgement. (2) **extract the decision, push the wiring thin**: `markRooms(prev, fired, active)` is a pure 6-line helper (flag a room iff its event fired, it's not active, and it's not already flagged) tested with 5 cases including the same-ref-when-unchanged contract; the App side is just setting two booleans where toasts already fire and one `setUnseen` at the end. The stateful, hard-to-test part (which-room-is-active across a tick) is reduced to passing `roomRef.current` into a pure function. (3) **return the SAME reference when nothing changes so a per-tick caller is free** — `markRooms` is invoked inside `refreshProgress` (every live tick), but returns `prev` unchanged on a quiet tick, so React bails the re-render; a "compute the next state every tick" pattern is safe ONLY if the no-change path is referentially stable. (4) **a stale-closure-safe `roomRef`** reads the CURRENT active room inside `refreshProgress` (which closes over an older render's `room`) — the same ref-mirror pattern the alert/event detection already uses; any per-tick function that needs "what's true NOW" must read a ref, not a captured value. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 226.

## Phase 16q — Export the Run Card (self-contained SVG → share/download) (2026-06-12)

253. **The deferred "untestable" feature became testable by changing the FORMAT, not the ambition — SVG export sidesteps the canvas that made PNG export jsdom-opaque.** 16o shipped the Run Card screenshot-only because the obvious export (PNG) needs `canvas` (`Image.onload` → `drawImage` → `toBlob`), which jsdom can't run, breaking the every-brick-tested discipline. Exporting the SVG itself needs NO canvas — `XMLSerializer` → an `image/svg+xml` Blob → `navigator.share({files})`/download — and SVG is a real, scalable image. Lessons: (1) **when a feature is blocked by an untestable dependency, look for a different IMPLEMENTATION of the same outcome before deferring it** — "shareable image file" had two paths (PNG-via-canvas, SVG-via-serialize); the second delivers the same user value (a saveable image) through a testable API. The deferral in 16o wasn't "this can't ship," it was "this specific path can't ship cleanly" — and naming that distinction is what surfaced the alternative. (2) **a shareable artifact must be SELF-CONTAINED — its styles have to travel with it.** On-page the card rode the page's `:root` CSS variables and web fonts; a standalone `.svg` has neither, so the export would have been an unstyled mess. The fix was to embed the `bc-*` rules in an SVG `<style>` with LITERAL hex colours (#e6d8b8…) and font stacks whose FALLBACKS (Georgia/Consolas) render without the web fonts (Cinzel/IBM Plex Mono). The rule generalizes: anything you serialize-and-send must carry its own presentation, because the receiving context is bare. (3) **split the export at the testability seam**: `shareOrDownload(blob, filename)` (the share-vs-download decision) is unit-tested by mocking `navigator.canShare`/`share`; the irreducibly-browser bit (the `<a download>` click / `URL.createObjectURL`) is a small guarded fallback that jsdom skips without throwing. Push the untestable surface down to the smallest possible guarded shell, and test everything above it. (4) the embedded `<style>` is the single source for the card's look (on-page AND exported), so `styles.css` keeps only the container sizing — moving presentation INTO the artifact removed a page-CSS dependency rather than duplicating it. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Completes the social arc: link (challenge) → text (brag) → native share → visual card → **shareable image file**. Brick 225.

## Phase 16p — Personalize the Run Card with your handle (2026-06-12)

252. **A shareable artifact wants the sharer's NAME on it — "{handle}'s run" beats "a run."** The 16o Run Card was a strong visual but anonymous; adding the leaderboard handle (`ew-handle`, already set for competition) as a byline ties the social artifact to the player's competitive identity with zero new input. Lessons: (1) **reuse the identity the user already gave you** — the handle existed (the leaderboard collects it); a second "what's your name?" prompt for the card would be friction for data you hold. When personalizing, look for an identity already in the app before asking for one. (2) the omit-when-empty discipline extends to LAYOUT, not just content: a set handle shifts the title up and the divider down to make room for the byline (`y={who ? 38 : 42}` / `y={who ? 64 : 58}`), so a handle-less card reads EXACTLY as 16o — conditional content that also conditionally reflows keeps both states clean rather than leaving a gap where the optional element would be. (3) trimmed the handle (`(handle ?? '').trim()`) so a whitespace-only value counts as none — the same "a blank isn't a value" guard as the brag's zero-omission; whitespace is the empty-string's disguise. (4) a near-trivial brick at deep saturation, but genuine: personalization is a real shareability multiplier, fully testable, and free given the existing handle — the honest small win when the big wins are spent. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 224.

## Phase 16o — Run Card (a shareable visual of your run) (2026-06-12)

251. **The social arc needed the PICTURE to go with the link — a run card is the image a feed actually stops on.** 16k/16l gave the text brag + the native share (the LINK); the Run Card is the visual: a self-contained SVG trophy (combat level · net worth hero figures, best-haul/survival/deeds chips, the seed/site) in the game's stone-and-gold livery, placed in the Hall as an identity/trophy surface and screenshot-able to share. Lessons: (1) **an inline SVG is a dependency-free, fully-TESTABLE shareable image** — no canvas (jsdom-opaque), no chart lib; the card is just `<text>`/`<rect>`/gradient nodes, so its content is asserted by DOM queries (wordmark, "combat level", "seed 42") exactly like any component, and it scales crisply. When you want a styled visual artifact that tests want to see INSIDE, SVG beats canvas. (2) **the same omit-zeros rule from the text brag (16k) applies to the visual** — empty achievement chips would read as a weak run, so the chip row drops best-haul/survival/deeds when absent; a fresh run shows just the two hero figures, a deep run fills the row. Visual brags and text brags share the "only show what flatters" discipline. (3) **a new always-mounted surface is a locator-collision risk — and it bit immediately**: the card's "seed 42 · …" footer lives in the tab-hidden-but-MOUNTED Hall, so a pre-existing challenge-link test's loose `getByText(/seed 42/)` went ambiguous (the recurring #147/#150 lesson). The fix is to tighten the OTHER test to its distinctive text (the toast's "seed 42 — same world"), not to weaken the card — adding content to an all-rooms-mounted app means auditing loose text matchers across the WHOLE suite, because your new text is in the DOM even on other tabs. (4) auto PNG-export / share-as-file deferred (documented) — the card + a screenshot + the 16k/16l link-share is a complete-enough v1; the export's canvas/file machinery is partly jsdom-untestable, so shipping the testable visual now and the export later is the clean split. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 223.

## Phase 16m — Engine core review: SOUND + deriveStats tie-break hardening (2026-06-12)

250. **A third adversarial review — on the deterministic ENGINE core — came back SOUND, and the certification is the deliverable: the foundation the whole game + a replay-verified leaderboard stand on is now traced, not assumed.** The review followed every value path against the ledger (GE tax balances; combat/loot mints, death-forfeit + escrow + event + bailout burns all booked; `checkInvariants` covers items incl. the non-obvious pack + worn paths) and every randomness/iteration path against determinism (single rng cursor; bounty/expedition streams consume zero world draws; no unsorted-Record decision feeds a hashed path; integer gp; no JSON-dropping `undefined`) and matching (price-time priority, self-trade skip, `TRADE_WINDOW` display-only). Zero Critical/High. Lessons: (1) **certifying the negative space of a critical core is worth a brick even when nothing's broken** — "the conservation ledger is complete and determinism holds" is now a traced, citable fact rather than a hope; on the foundation everything depends on, the absence of a bug verified is as valuable as a bug found. (2) the one MEDIUM was a FRAGILITY, not a bug — `deriveStats` picked equal-score pack gear by strict `>` (insertion-order-dependent) while `equipBest`/`worn` already tie-broke by `itemId <`; **inconsistency across paths that implement the same rule is itself the smell**, even when each is individually correct, because the day the shared assumption (here: tied gear is stat-identical) breaks, the divergent one fails silently. (3) **a determinism trap is the worst class of latent bug — silent and leaderboard-corrupting — so it's worth closing PROACTIVELY even when inert**: added the `itemId <` tie-break to `deriveStats`, making the rule uniform. (4) **proved the engine change byte-identical THREE ways before trusting it**: the full 513-test suite passes unchanged, a new order-independence test pins it (tied legs in both key orders → equal stats), and the sim seed-42/10k-tick hash stayed `fe75df57` — so it's replay-NEUTRAL (no verify-score redeploy, batch unchanged). `deriveStats` returns `{atk,def}` (stats, no identity), and tied pieces have equal stats, so the summed output couldn't change; the tie-break only future-proofs WHICH identical-stat piece is chosen. When you touch a hashed path, the hash is the proof — re-run it. UI/byte-identical engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 221.

## Phase 16l — Native Share for the Brag (Web Share API) (2026-06-12)

249. **Copy is the desktop share; the native sheet is the mobile one — route the same payload to whichever exists.** 16k made the brag copyable, but on mobile (where casual sharing actually happens) `navigator.share` is one tap to any app — the real viral mechanism. `shareBrag` prefers `navigator.share({ text })`, falling back to the 16k clipboard path. Lessons: (1) **progressive enhancement is the right shape for a capability that's present on some clients and not others** — don't pick one channel; prefer the best available and degrade. The same `bragText` (16k) flows to the native sheet on mobile, the clipboard on desktop, a `window.prompt` on ancient browsers — three tiers, one payload, each the best the client can do. (2) **prefer the durable platform API over a per-platform intent URL** — a `twitter.com/intent` link rots when the platform renames/changes; the Web Share API lets the OS route to whatever the user has, and survives the platform churn. When a standard capability exists, it beats hardcoding a specific destination. (3) **a "no feedback" branch is correct when the system provides its own** — the native share sheet IS the confirmation, so the share path shows no toast (a toast would double up); only the clipboard fallback toasts, because a silent copy gives no signal. Match your feedback to whether the channel already has its own. (4) both branches are testable by toggling the capability: jsdom lacks `navigator.share` so the default run exercises the copy fallback, and mocking `navigator.share` (then deleting it in `finally`) exercises the preferred path — a clean way to test capability-gated code without a real browser. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 220.

## Phase 16k — Shareable Run Brag (2026-06-12)

248. **A leaderboard without a SHARE button is a growth loop with the last step missing — the game had the score and the challenge link but no way to brag them together.** `bragText(game, worth, origin, pathname)` formats a run into a paste-anywhere summary (combat level · net worth · best haul · M-dive streak · K deeds) + a `#seed=S` challenge link, copied by a "📋 brag" chip beside the existing "challenge link" one. Lessons: (1) **the social loop for a competitive game is "what I did" + "the exact world to beat me on" — and both halves already existed separately** (`copyChallenge` shared the seed; the stat helpers `diveStreak`/`diveRecords`/`combatLevel` computed the achievements); the brag is just JOINING them into one shareable string. Look for features that are a composition of two things you already ship — they're the cheapest high-leverage adds. (2) **a brag must omit its zeros** — "0 deeds · 0-dive streak" reads as a bad run, the opposite of a brag; `bragText` drops empty stats and keeps only the always-true title/level/worth/link, so even a fresh run reads cleanly and a deep run reads impressively. Conditional inclusion is what separates a brag from a stat dump. (3) reused `copyChallenge`'s exact clipboard pattern (navigator.clipboard → `window.prompt` fallback → confirm toast, jsdom-safe) — a proven UX shape applied to a second payload costs nothing and stays consistent (two share affordances that behave identically). (4) **a deliberate non-band, non-trading, forward-looking pick** after a long band/adventure/save-flow run — at saturation, a genuinely fresh CATEGORY (social/viral) is worth more than a 10th lens on an existing one; the value-band pull was strong (9 bricks) and resisting it for a new axis is the right call. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 219.

## Phase 16j — Save-flow review fixes: catch-up swap-guard + newGame parity (2026-06-12)

247. **A second adversarial review, this time on the SAVE path, found a real data-corruption bug that no green test would ever catch — because the bug only fires on an event ordering tests never simulate (a game swap mid-offline-catch-up).** The review (calibrated: it certified the negative space — quarantine loss-free, plain-JSON discipline holds, the up-front `lastSeenMs` restamp fails safe — and flagged its own #2 as model-dependent) found that the chunked offline catch-up driver reads `gameRef.current` fresh per chunk but runs `planRef.current` (the plan made for the game `beginOffline` saw); a cloud-adopt (small-debt sync path), import, or **restart (which never calls `beginOffline`)** swaps `gameRef` out from under an in-flight catch-up, so the old plan's ticks advance the NEW world and `finishOfflineProgress` finalizes a stale plan against it — over-advanced ticks (determinism/worth corruption) + garbage "while away" deltas. Lessons: (1) **review the SAVE/LOAD/migration path explicitly — it's the highest-severity surface and the least test-covered, because its bugs need a SEQUENCE of events (swap during async work) that unit tests don't stage.** Data-loss/corruption bugs hide in event-ordering, not in arithmetic; a path whose tests all pass can still corrupt on a swap-during-catch-up nobody scripted. (2) **the robust fix for "async work outlives its context" is to TAG the work with its context and re-check at each step**, not to remember-to-cancel at every call site — a `catchUpGame` ref + a driver guard (`catchUpGame.current !== gameRef.current → abort`) covers ALL swap sites (adopt/import/restart) at once, where chasing each swap site to add a cancel would have missed restart (which doesn't even go through `beginOffline`). When N entry points can invalidate in-flight async state, guard the CONSUMER once, don't patch the N producers. (3) **`newGame` and `normalizeGame` had drifted** — `normalizeGame` defaulted `delves` but `newGame` omitted it (the constructor and the migrator must agree on the shape, or "fresh" and "loaded-then-migrated" games differ); pinned `newGame().delves === []` so a fresh game equals a normalized one. Two functions that both produce the same type must stay in lockstep — a parity test is the cheapest guard. (4) **document, don't reflexively fix, a finding whose impact is bounded by an external decision** — the cross-device double-accrual (#2) inflates only DISPLAYED worth (the verified leaderboard score replays from `commandLog`, where idle offline ticks reproduce identically), and a real fix needs a multi-device-model call that's the owner's, not mine; the honest move is to record it precisely (impact + why deferred) rather than ship a semantics change. (5) **two reviews, two real bugs (16e refreshProgress, 16j save-flow), both in the accumulated-complexity hotspots green tests had grown around** — at feature-saturation, reviewing the load-bearing stateful machinery is where the genuine value is, not the 218th feature. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 218.

## Phase 16i — Band Meter (visualize the cost→value range) (2026-06-12)

246. **A category hides the magnitude it was bucketed from — nine band lenses said "rich" but none said "180 of a 100→200 range, 20 left".** The value-band is the game's central trading concept, surfaced as a 🟢/⚪/🟡 tag on six-plus surfaces, but the tag only ever gave the THIRD, never the concrete BOUNDS or the exact position. `BandMeter({def, lastPrice})` draws a floor─[marker]─ceiling bar (`bandPosition` for the marker, a green/gray/gold zone-gradient track, `baseCost`/`consumeValue` labelled) so "how much room is left before fair value" is one look. Lessons: (1) **the difference between polish and a feature is whether it surfaces NEW information** — I'd hesitated on this as "band brick #9 / just visual," but the meter shows the band's actual numeric range (floor/ceiling) and continuous position, which the category genuinely cannot express; a visual that reveals data the existing reads hid is a feature, not decoration. The honest test for "is this padding?" is "does it tell the user something they couldn't get from what's already there?" — here, yes (the bounds + the magnitude). (2) **this is the inverse of 16g's lesson** — 16g took a continuous metric (streak) and gated it to discrete milestones to avoid noise; 16i takes a discretized read (the cheap/fair/rich bucket) back to its continuous source to recover lost magnitude. Bucketing and de-bucketing are both tools; which you want depends on whether the consumer needs a trigger (discretize) or a sense of scale (keep continuous). (3) reused the existing `bandPosition`/`valueBand` (15q/15f) with zero new logic — the meter is a thin VIEW over the numbers two earlier bricks already computed, which is why a "9th band brick" cost almost nothing: the hard part (the math, the single source) was long done; only the rendering was new. (4) a zone-gradient CSS track (green 0-34% / gray 34-67% / gold 67-100%) + an absolute-positioned marker is a dependency-free way to draw a labelled range meter — no chart lib, testable by asserting `marker.style.left`. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 217.

## Phase 16g — Survival-Streak Milestone Celebration (2026-06-12)

245. **A monotonic +1 counter makes "celebrate a new record" noisy — milestone-gating is what turns a streak into a moment.** 16f's streak grows by exactly 1 per survived dive, so a naive "celebrate every time you beat your best" would toast EVERY dive once you're past your old record (5→6→7… all new bests). Gating to a sparse set (`STREAK_MILESTONES = [5,10,25,50,100]`) with exact-equality membership fires at most once per milestone-reaching dive — a real "you've done something hard" beat, not spam. Lessons: (1) **the celebration cadence must match how the underlying metric moves** — for a metric that jumps unpredictably (loot haul), "beat your best" is naturally rare and worth celebrating each time (15e); for a metric that increments by 1 deterministically (a streak), "beat your best" is EVERY step, so you celebrate milestones instead. Same "mark an achievement" goal, opposite gating, dictated by the metric's step distribution. (2) **exact-equality on a +1 counter is a clean once-each trigger with no dedup state** — because the streak passes through each milestone value exactly once on the way up, `current === milestone` fires once per milestone with no "already celebrated" ref needed; the monotonic-by-1 property IS the dedup. (A death resets to 0, which is never a member, so deaths self-exclude — no `!died` guard needed either.) (3) reused the 15e onDelveEnd append-point detection (compute the streak AFTER the record is appended, fire if it's a milestone) — no baseline ref, no swap-guard, because `onDelveEnd` only fires on a genuine dive-end, not every tick; choosing the detection SITE by when the data arrives (append point vs every-tick refreshProgress) keeps the swap-guard burden off features whose trigger is already a discrete event. (4) ordered last in the handler so the rarer milestone wins the toast slot over a co-occurring best-haul — the 16e last-writer-wins discipline applied to a second handler. Completes the streak feature (readout 16f ↔ celebration 16g). UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 215.

## Phase 16f — Dive Survival Streak (2026-06-12)
