244. **A streak gives an existing skill a number to PROTECT — the careful-extraction the game already rewarded was a series of one-offs until you could break a run.** The adventure loop had risk instrumentation (push-read at current hp 13p, at-stake loot on the extract button 13o, extract recap 15n) but no carry-over reward for playing the odds well dive after dive. `diveStreak(delves)` → `{current, best}` (trailing consecutive survivals + longest run) surfaced as "🔥 N clean (best M)" turns "I extracted in time" into a thing you're building, and a death doesn't just forfeit one dive's loot — it breaks the streak, the exact extra tension that sharpens the bank/push call. Lessons: (1) **streaks are a near-free retention multiplier on an existing decision** — the data (`Game.delves` with `died` flags) was already latched (12i); a one-pass fold (`run = died ? 0 : run+1; best = max(best, run)`) over it manufactures stakes with no new mechanic, no engine change. When a game already records per-attempt success/failure, a current+best streak is almost always a cheap layer of meaning on top. (2) the single-pass insight: the trailing `run` at loop-end IS `current` (it resets on every death and only the trailing survivals survive to the end), so `current` and `best` fall out of ONE pass — no separate backward scan needed; the same value that tracks the running max also IS the final answer. (3) pinned the reset semantics as numbers (death-last → `current 0, best 2`; rebuild → `current 1, best 2`; early-death-then-long-run → `current 4, best 4`) so "a death resets current but not best" can't silently regress. (4) the adventure analog of the daily login streak (10s) — the same "don't break it" psychology, applied to risk management instead of attendance; reusing a proven retention shape in a fresh context is breadth without novelty risk. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 214.

## Phase 16e — refreshProgress toast-priority + swap-symmetry (review-driven) (2026-06-12)

243. **The overdue Review step earned its keep: an adversarial pass found two real bugs that 361 green tests couldn't, in the exact place complexity had silently accumulated.** After 15 rapid Draft-Merge bricks I ran an adversarial review on `refreshProgress` (now ~7 stateful detection loops). It self-corrected its own first "HIGH" finding to "sound" on re-inspection (good calibration — no fabricated flaws), confirmed the bulk correct (re-arm complete on all four one-shot loops, null guards clean, baseline swap-guards correct), and surfaced two genuine low-severity issues. Lessons: (1) **last-writer-wins toast priority is INVERTED from reading order, and append-only growth quietly demotes the oldest (highest-value) toast** — celebrations (level-up/region-unlock/deed) were written FIRST, so every alert and the event-recap appended after them over successive bricks silently OUTRANKED them; a level-up could be clobbered by a co-occurring "X is cheap" alert. The fix is just MOVE the celebrations last, but the bug is invisible without asking "what's the call ORDER vs the intended priority?" — and it only appeared because features kept being appended to the function's tail. When a shared sink is last-writer-wins, every append silently reprioritizes; audit the order whenever you add to such a function. (2) **swap-guard symmetry: when ONE class of cross-tick ref gets a game-identity guard, audit ALL of them** — the level/progress/event baselines re-baselined on swap but the four alert `*Fired` Sets didn't (the only un-guarded cross-tick state). The right re-baseline DIFFERS by semantics: celebrations SUPPRESS the adopted state (don't fire "you leveled up" for a save you adopted), but informational alerts fire FRESH ("your watched X is cheap" on load is accurate, not a false achievement) — so the fix was `clear()` not "mark-as-fired." Same swap trigger, opposite re-baseline, because one is an achievement and the other is a status. (3) **a behaviour-preserving reorder is provable by a green suite** — moving the celebration block past the alert loops changed only collision-priority, so all 361 per-detection tests staying green IS the proof of no regression; the one thing they DON'T cover (the collision itself) is brittle to test, so an explicit "Celebrations LAST" intent comment guards the ordering against a future re-sort. (4) the review's biggest value was the NEGATIVE space it certified — re-arm completeness, null-guard coverage, baseline correctness — confidence that's worth as much as the two fixes, and unobtainable from "tests pass." UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 213.

## Phase 16d — Value-Band "Take Profit" Alert (sell side) (2026-06-12)

242. **An asymmetric signal is a half-built one — 15s alerted you to BUY the dip but never to SELL the peak.** The band-alert system fired on the cheap third (buy) but had no rich-third (sell) counterpart, so a value investor got told when to accumulate but never when to take profit. `richAlertHit(def, last)` = `valueBand === 'rich'` (the exact sibling of `bandAlertHit`) + a 🟡 watchlist toggle completes the pair. Lessons: (1) **a notification system that fires on one edge of a band wants both edges** — cheap→buy and rich→sell are the two actionable extremes of the same fundamental signal; shipping only one leaves the user squinting at the passive readout for the other. When you build an alert for "X is low," the "X is high" alert is almost always the missing symmetric half, and it's nearly free (same pref shape, same one-shot/re-arm loop, same toggle — only the predicate flips). (2) **keep both halves of a pair on ONE surface** — I deliberated putting the sell alert on PositionsPanel (the "take profit on a HOLDING" framing is arguably more natural), but splitting band-alerts across two panels would fracture the alert hub; a user configures all alerts (price ≤/≥, band cheap/rich) in one place. Consistency of where-you-arm beats semantic purity of which-surface. (3) the implementation reused 15s wholesale — `richAlerts` pref, `richFired` re-arm ref, the post-cheap-loop check, the gated toggle — so the diff reads as "the same thing, sell side," which is exactly how a reviewer wants a symmetric feature to look. (4) the price-alert + band-alert system is now fully symmetric: absolute (buy-below ≤ / sell-above ≥) AND fundamental (cheap 🟢 / rich 🟡), entry and exit on both axes. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 212.

## Phase 16b — Live Provisional Board Standing (2026-06-12)

241. **A leaderboard is dead until your LIVE position is on it — 16a gave the gap to your submitted rank; this puts your current run on the ladder in real time.** Before the sprint mark, your current worth IS what your sprint score would be if it ended now, so `provisionalRank(rows, myWorth)` honestly answers "if the sprint ended this instant, you'd sit #N" — a live race position, gated to `tick < SPRINT_TICKS` and hidden after (where current worth includes post-sprint play and is no longer a sprint score). Lessons: (1) **the honesty of a derived stat lives in its GATE, not its formula** — comparing live worth to sprint-mark scores is meaningful only inside the sprint window; the same number after the mark would be a vanity lie. The one-line `tick < SPRINT_TICKS` gate is what makes the feature truthful, and finding the window where a comparison holds is the real design work, not the rank math. (2) **I wrote the doc intent right ("a tie sits below the incumbent") but coded the wrong operator (strict `>`), and only caught it writing the test** — strict `>` ties you AT the incumbent's rank (#2), not below (#3); "you haven't beaten an equal score" requires `>=`. The pinned test (`provisionalRank(board, 3000) === 3`) is what forced the contradiction into the open; a vaguer assertion would have shipped the optimistic-by-one bug. Write the boundary case as a NUMBER, not a description — "ties sit below" passed my eyes, `=== 3` did not lie. (3) `>=` also happens to match real submission tie-breaking (a fresh equal score lands after existing ones), so the honest-conservative choice and the accurate-to-the-backend choice coincided — when unsure on a tie-break, "you haven't earned it yet" is both the kinder-to-truth and usually the correct-to-system answer. (4) 16a (static gap to submitted rank) + 16b (live provisional rank) together make the board a two-tense surface — where you ARE (submitted) and where you're HEADED (live) — which is what turns a standings list into a race you feel mid-run. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 210.

## Phase 16a — Leaderboard Rank Gap (2026-06-12)

240. **A standings list shows WHERE you are; a competitive hook shows what it COSTS to climb — the board had the rank but not the gap.** The Sprint/Daily board displayed "you're #5 on this seed" and the rows, but "what would it take to move up one spot?" was mental arithmetic across two rows. `rankGap(rows, meRank)` → `{gap, rank, ahead}` turns it into a target: "🎯 {gap} gp behind #4 {handle}" — the exact fortune you'd need at the sprint mark to overtake the player above you. Lessons: (1) **rank is an ordinal; motivation is a cardinal** — knowing you're 5th doesn't tell you if 4th is one good trade away or hopeless; the GAP (a real gp number) is what makes the next position feel attainable and worth a re-run. When a surface shows a position in an ordering, the adjacent DISTANCE is usually the higher-value derivative. (2) the helper math is all off-by-one discipline: `meRank` is 1-based, so your row is `rows[meRank-1]` and the rank above is `rows[meRank-2]` — pinned both (rank 2→trails rank 1, rank 3→trails rank 2) so the indexing can't silently drift, plus the null guards (#1 → nothing to chase, unranked → nothing to measure). (3) **breadth pays: the leaderboard hadn't been touched since 11q's `myRank`**, and one fetched-but-underused data shape (board rows already carry `worth` + `handle`) yielded a whole competitive feature with no new cloud call — when feature-hunting in a saturated codebase, the least-recently-touched surface often has the cheapest wins precisely because it was left at MVP. (4) scoped to the NEXT rank, not the leader: the immediate-above gap is the actionable target; a gap-to-#1 would be motivating-or-crushing noise for a mid-board player — show the rung you can reach, not the summit. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Opens Phase 16. Brick 209.

## Phase 15z — Event-End Recap (close the event lifecycle) (2026-06-12)

239. **Every lifecycle with a START surface wants a CLOSE surface — events had actionable chips but expired silently, so you never learned if the craze paid off.** The events system showed active events as chips ("⚡ {item} {label} · N left", 15l) but an event just vanished when it ended; added a recap toast — "⚡ {item} {label} ended · settled {start}→{end} (±X%)" — that captures the item's price when the event begins and reports the move when it ends. This is the third lifecycle I've closed with the same instinct: dives (death recap ↔ extract recap, 15n), and now events (chip ↔ recap). Lessons: (1) **make the stateful detection a PURE reconcile and the ref-juggling thin** — `reconcileEvents(captured, activeNow, priceOf)` returns `{recaps, captured}`; it's a pure set-diff (newly-active → capture at price; was-captured-but-not-active → recap + drop) that unit-tests deterministically (capture at 100, end at 150 → +50% recap, then cleaned up), while the App just holds the map in a ref and calls it. Detection that *reads like* a transition (active→ended) is exactly where a pure "diff two snapshots" helper shines — push the bookkeeping into the function, keep the effect a one-liner. (2) **a cross-tick capture ref is a game-swap landmine — guard it the moment you add it** (`eventCaptureGame.current !== game` → reset), or a cloud-adopt/restart recaps the OLD game's events the instant their ids vanish from the new game's array (the 14e/14f false-celebration class). Any ref that remembers "what was true last tick" needs the identity guard; I now add it reflexively when introducing one. (3) the recap needed a price the event-start moment had but the engine doesn't store (`WorldEvent` has no start price) — so the UI captures it on first-seen-active, the same "snapshot a baseline when you first observe it" move as the offline `pricesBefore` (14y) and the level/daily baselines. When the data you want to diff against isn't persisted, capture it yourself at the observation edge. (4) firing for ALL ended events (not just held/watched) mirrors the chips showing all active ones — consistent scope across a lifecycle's two ends beats a clever asymmetry; if it's noisy, held-scoping is a one-line filter later. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 208.

## Phase 15y — Region Tint on the Embark Readout (2026-06-12)

238. **Finish the through-line: a palette that appears on two of three sibling surfaces reads as inconsistent, not restrained.** `arenaTheme` tinted the combat backdrop (14u) and the map node halos (15t), but the embark screen — the surface where you CHOOSE the region — stayed palette-neutral, so the green-plains→ember-Maw→void-Abyss identity dropped out at the exact moment of decision. Wrapping the danger/loot/forecast/wounded block in a `.region-readout` div with a faint per-region gradient closes the set: all three adventure surfaces now share one palette. Lessons: (1) **a transparent wrapper is the lowest-risk way to theme an existing block** — the wrap added a `<div>` around five existing reads without touching any of them; text queries pass through, the content is byte-identical, and only the backdrop changed. When the goal is purely visual, find the smallest container that holds the thing and tint THAT, rather than threading a colour through each child. (2) **hex8 low-alpha (`{from}33`/`{to}11`) keeps a tint subordinate to legibility** — the arena palette is already dark, and at ~20%/7% alpha over the dark panel the dim/small reads stay perfectly readable while the hue still shifts per region; a tint you can't read over isn't atmosphere, it's noise. (3) the test asserts the wrapper EXISTS with a gradient AND still contains the reads (`danger:`/`loot:` inside `.region-readout`) — proving the theming is present and the wrap is transparent in one check, the right invariant for a "decorate without disturbing" change. (4) completing a visual set is worth a brick on its own: consistency is a feature — a player who sees the region's colour in the fight and on the map expects it when planning, and its absence there was a small but real seam. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 207.

## Phase 15x — "Your Holding Moved" Away-Bar Line (2026-06-12)

237. **The same computation over a different subset turns an impersonal headline into a personal one — `heldMover` is `biggestMover` filtered to what you own.** The away-bar's `topMover` (14y) reports the market's biggest swing while away ("the world didn't sleep"), but it might be an item you neither hold nor watch; the question that actually matters on return is "what happened to MY money?" `heldMover(before, markets, inventory)` = `biggestMover(before, markets.filter(held))` answers it, deduped against `topMover` so the same item isn't reported twice. Lessons: (1) **personalization is often a filter, not a new feature** — the data (captured `pricesBefore` + the post-away `playerView`'s `markets` and `inventory`) was all already there; the value came from scoping an existing market-wide computation to the player's own positions. Before building a "personal X," check whether X already exists market-wide and just needs a `.filter(mine)`. (2) **a one-line wrapper still deserves to be a named, exported, unit-tested helper** — `heldMover` is literally one `biggestMover` call, but naming it (a) documents intent at the call site in `finishOfflineProgress`, (b) lets it be tested deterministically with synthetic data (held +10% beats unheld +100% → returns the held one) instead of only through a sim run, and (c) gives future callers a single source. The same justification as `bandAlertHit`/`alertHit`: thin ≠ not-worth-naming. (3) **dedupe overlapping readouts at the render, not the data** — `heldMover` and `topMover` can legitimately be the same item (your biggest holding IS the market's biggest mover); rather than complicate the helper, the away-bar just hides the held line when `itemId === topMover.itemId` — keep helpers pure and total, resolve presentation overlap in the view. (4) salvaged the price-only half of the away-feedback idea that 15q's away-FILLS investigation killed: fills are engine-gated (512-trade window evicts them), but price DELTAS on held items need no fills — separating the recoverable part of a blocked idea from the blocked part is its own win. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 206.

## Phase 15w — "Rest Before You Dive" Wounded Nudge (2026-06-12)

236. **A forecast computed at one state silently lies when you act from another — the embark "favored" verdict assumed full hp but you can embark wounded.** `combatForecast` on the embark screen runs at `trainedMax` (full hp) because it's a plan for a fresh dive; but the engine heals you over ticks only while NOT on expedition, so embarking straight out of a prior dive carries the wound in and the verdict you read is optimistic. Added a wounded nudge: when `agent.hp` is defined and below max, a "⚠ you're at {hp}/{max} hp — you'll dive hurt (forecast assumes full hp)" line + a one-tap "rest to full (≈N ticks)" using the existing rest infra. Lessons: (1) **when a readout is computed at an assumed state, the bug isn't the number — it's the unstated assumption; surface the assumption or peg the number to reality.** I chose to keep the forecast at full hp (it's the PLAN you'd execute after resting) and make the assumption LOUD ("assumes full hp") + actionable (rest now), rather than silently re-pegging to current hp (which would confuse a player who intends to heal first). Naming the assumption beats hiding it either way. (2) this is the EMBARK-side twin of the mid-dive push-read (13p, which already reads at CURRENT hp) — the same "is the forecast honest about my hp?" question, but the planning surface never got the answer the in-fight surface had. When you fix an honesty gap on one surface, check its siblings for the same gap. (3) **the engine's `delete agent.hp`-at-full convention IS the wounded predicate** — a defined `hp` means hurt, undefined means full; reusing that (as CharacterPanel's `restEta` does) means the UI and engine agree on "wounded" with zero new state. (4) the fix cost almost nothing because the rest plumbing (`onRest=fastForward`, `healEta`, `REST_REGEN_TICKS`) already existed from 13x/13y — threading one prop into a third consumer; a well-factored action (fast-forward N ticks) pays out every time a new surface needs it. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 205.

## Phase 15v — Item→Source Index ("where does this drop?") (2026-06-12)

235. **The same table, read in two directions, links two economies — `regionLoot` (region→items) and `itemSources` (item→region) are one drop table indexed both ways.** 15u answered "what does this region drop?" on the embark screen; this answers "where can I farm this item?" on the trade ticket — a "🗡 farm: {monster} {chance}% · {region}" line for any item that appears in a drop table. The same `MONSTERS[].drops` data backs both; the only difference is which key you index by. Lessons: (1) **a cross-reference is most valuable in BOTH directions, and the second direction is nearly free once the data's mapped** — having shipped region→loot, the inverse item→source is the same table with a reverse index + a region resolve (`region.monsters`/`elite` ∋ monsterId); building both completes a loop (price you see ⟷ dive that supplies it) that either half alone leaves open. (2) **gate a contextual line to its relevant subset so density stays earned** — the ticket is the densest surface in the app, so the farm line renders ONLY for drop-items (runes/bones/dragon gear), and `itemSources('shark') === []` keeps it off every staple's ticket; a line that shows for the minority that needs it costs nothing for the majority that doesn't. (3) **place reference info with context, not with the math** — the farm line sits by the wiki-snapshot (what this item IS), not among the blend/break-even/concentration block (what this TRADE does), because "you could hunt this" is a fact about the item, not the order. (4) the `as const` test-props gotcha resurfaced: `{...} as const` froze `recentPrices: []` to `readonly []`, which won't satisfy the component's `number[]` — spreading a shared props object into render() wants either no `as const` or an explicit `[] as number[]`; `exactOptionalPropertyTypes`-grade strictness extends to readonly-vs-mutable too. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 204.

## Phase 15u — Loot Upside on the Embark Decision (2026-06-12)

234. **A decision surface that shows only one side of a trade-off isn't a decision surface — the embark screen instrumented RISK to the teeth but hid REWARD.** The "plan a dive" flow had foe danger (colour-coded ⚔/🛡 vs your kit), leech, elite warnings, a rounds-to-kill/rounds-to-fall forecast, and prep nags — every signal answered "can I survive?" and none answered "is it worth it?". Added `regionLoot(roster)` → the gp-per-kill range + the region's distinct drops (each at its best chance, likeliest first), rendered as a "loot: ≈lo–hi gp/kill · drops: …" line right after the danger read. Lessons: (1) **audit decision surfaces for missing SIDES, not missing data** — the loot data was in the engine the whole time (`MONSTERS[].gp/.drops`); what was missing was the REWARD half of a risk⟷reward call. The same shape recurred in 15n (death recap existed, success recap didn't): when one arm of a trade-off is richly narrated, look for the silent opposite arm — it's usually the higher-value add. (2) **a forward aggregator complements a backward one** — `raidTotalsByRegion` (15c) tells you what a region HAS paid (history); `regionLoot` tells you what it CAN pay (the table), and the embark decision needs the forward view because you're choosing before you have history. Same domain, opposite time-direction, both worth having. (3) scoped the reward read to an honest RANGE + a drop LIST, deliberately NOT an expected-gp/hr (chance × wiki-price) number — a speculative EV would over-promise on a stochastic drop and invite "the game lied" when variance bites; a range you can see the bounds of is the honest instrument (the same ≈/estimate discipline as the combat forecast in #148). (4) roster ids come from `region.monsters` (engine-internal, always valid), so `monsterById` is safe HERE even though it throws on stale PERSISTED ids (the 15t finding) — the safety of a lookup depends on where the id came from, not the lookup itself. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 203.

## Phase 15t — Region-Identity Tint on the Map (2026-06-12)

233. **Tint additively, never by override — a separate halo element preserves the state colour-coding a fill would have clobbered.** 14u themed the combat backdrop per region; this carried the same palette to the RegionMap so the descent (green plains → ember Maw → void Abyss) is felt on the map, not just labelled. The node circles are state-coloured by CSS (`.mapnode.cleared/frontier/locked circle`), so a `fill={regionColour}` on the circle would have destroyed the cleared/frontier/locked distinction. The fix was to add the tint as a SEPARATE element — a `<circle className="maphalo">` rendered FIRST in each node `<g>`, behind the r=9 state circle, showing as a coloured rim. Lessons: (1) **when a property is already doing semantic work, layer the new signal beside it, don't overwrite it** — fill encodes node STATE; region IDENTITY is an orthogonal signal, so it gets its own element at its own z-order. Two signals, two elements, zero conflict — the alternative (one fill carrying both) can't express both at once. (2) reuse the palette, don't invent one: importing `arenaTheme` (a component-file export) into RegionMap means the map and the fight are guaranteed to agree on what "the Abyss" looks like; a second hardcoded palette would have drifted. (3) the test asserts the INVARIANT, not the hex — "8 halos, first ≠ last" proves a per-region tint without coupling the test to the exact colours, so a palette re-tune later won't break it. (4) **a deliberate atmosphere brick is a legitimate breadth pivot** after a long functional run (six value-band trading bricks) — game-feel is a feature, and recolouring the descent costs almost nothing while reinforcing the core progression fantasy.

**Discovered (logged to NEXT_STEPS, not fixed — needs engine work):** `monsterById(id)` THROWS on an unknown id (`quest.ts:374`). Persisted state — a saved active combat's `exp.combat.monsterId`, or a bounty's `monsterId` — could carry an id that a later engine version renamed/removed, and the engine TICK calls `monsterById` during combat resolution too, so a UI render-guard alone can't save it. A clean fix is load-boundary quarantine (drop/settle a stale persisted combat in `normalizeGame`), which touches engine semantics → Jesse-gated. Realized probability is currently ~nil (content changes have been additive, not removals), but it's a real latent crash vector as the bestiary evolves. UI-only this brick, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 202.

## Phase 15s — Value-Band "Buy the Dip" Alert (2026-06-12)

232. **The most useful alert is the one with no number to maintain — a value-band trigger self-adjusts to fundamentals where an absolute price threshold goes stale.** The alert system had buy-below (≤ X) and sell-above (≥ X), both demanding the player pick AND maintain a price; but a value investor's real question is "tell me when this is cheap *relative to what it's worth*", which `valueBand` already answers. Added a third alert type that arms on a banded watch row (🟢 toggle) and fires a one-shot "🟢 {item} is cheap" toast when last enters the cheap third — re-arming when it leaves. Lessons: (1) **a band/ratio trigger dominates a fixed threshold for "buy the dip"** — set a buy-below at 110 and it never fires again once the item's floor drifts to 130; "cheap relative to its band" keeps working as the fundamentals move, with nothing to re-tune. The threshold-free alert is strictly less maintenance for the player and strictly more robust. (2) **a new alert TYPE is cheap when you copy the established shape exactly** — `bandAlerts: Record<string,boolean>` + a `bandFired` re-arm Set + the same one-shot/clear-on-exit loop the price alerts use; the only novelty is the predicate (`bandAlertHit` = `valueBand === 'cheap'`), everything else is the proven pattern. Reuse the control structure, vary only the trigger — the result reads as "obviously correct" next to its two siblings. (3) **gate the affordance to where it's meaningful** — the toggle only renders on rows that HAVE a band (`bandPosition !== null`); a cheap-alert on a bandless item (consumeValue ≤ baseCost) could never fire, so offering it would be a lie. (4) the thin `bandAlertHit` wrapper (one line over `valueBand`) earns its place the same way `alertHit` does: a named, unit-tested single source means App and the watchlist can't drift on the definition of "cheap", and the predicate gets pinned independently of the notification plumbing. Completes the value-band arc as an ACTIVE signal (market track 15f → mood 15g → watch readout 15i → positions 15o → sortable column 15q → now an alert). UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 201.

## Phase 15r — Pre-Buy Concentration Preview (2026-06-12)

231. **A risk shown only after the fact isn't risk management — moving the concentration read from PositionsPanel (post-hoc) to the buy ticket (pre-commit) is where the decision actually is.** The ticket was densely pre-instrumented on the buy side (avg-cost blend 12d, gear delta, break-even 12h) but the one mistake that ruins a flipper — dumping too much of your worth into a single illiquid item — was only visible *after* the fill, on the allocation bar. Added `buyConcentration(view, itemId, addCost)`: a "after fill: {item} ≈ X% of holdings ⚠" line next to the cost, reusing the PositionsPanel risk thresholds (>50% red, ≥34% dim). Lessons: (1) **the honest denominator for "what % will I be exposed?" is liquid worth, which a buy does NOT change** — a buy swaps cash→goods, so worth (cash + held goods at last price) is identical before and after; the item's share rises purely because the gp moved INTO it. Getting this right meant the preview is exact, not an approximation. (2) **my first unit test encoded the wrong mental model and the test caught ME, not the code** — I wrote "commit 1000 of 1000 worth → 50%", reasoning as if the commit ADDED to the denominator (1000/2000). It's 100%: worth stays 1000, the item becomes all of it. The helper was correct; the test expectation was the bug. This is the ideal failure: a pinned numeric test refuted a plausible-but-wrong intuition at authoring time, not in production. When a test fails, *first* re-derive whether the code or the expectation is wrong — here the "obvious" 50% was the error. (3) the feature needed NO new prop — `PlayerView` already carries `gp` + `inventory` + `markets.lastPrice`, so liquid worth is computable inside the ticket from data it already holds; check what the existing view exposes before threading new props (the 14r/App-baseline reflex). (4) the brick was chosen after inspection killed three richer-sounding candidates: away-fills (engine's 512-trade window evicts them, 15q), a 100k determinism gate (longrun.test.ts already does 50k snapshot-resume), and empty-panel onboarding hints (MoversPanel/ProfitPanel/TopFlips already have them) — verifying-before-building turned a feature hunt into three fast "already covered / not cleanly possible" calls and one real gap. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 200.

## Phase 15q — Sortable Value-Band Column (2026-06-12)

230. **A category is a glance; a number is a ranking — extracting the numeric basis under a categorical helper turns 5 per-item glances into a whole-market sort, for free.** `valueBand` (15f) classified one price as cheap/fair/rich and was surfaced on 5 read-surfaces, but always as a per-item read — the market table could sort by bid/ask/last/margin/vol yet not by *fundamental value position*. The fix wasn't new logic: `valueBand` already computed a clamped 0..1 position internally and then threw it away by returning only the bucket. Extracting that as `bandPosition(def, last): number | null` and having `valueBand` categorize *its* result (thresholds now live in exactly one place) gave a sortable "band" column at near-zero cost — sort ascending for the market's best accumulation candidates, descending for offload candidates, which subsumes BOTH the queued one-sided "rich track" and "value-band as a sortable column" in one move. Lessons: (1) **when a helper returns a category, ask what continuous quantity it bucketed** — that hidden number is usually the more reusable primitive (it sorts, thresholds, and interpolates; the bucket only labels). Expose the number, derive the category from it, and the callers that wanted a glance are unchanged while new callers get a ranking. (2) the refactor was *behaviour-preserving by construction*: `valueBand`'s output bucket is a pure function of `bandPosition`'s output, so the 5 existing callers cannot regress — the existing `valueBand` test stayed green as the proof. Pin the new numeric directly too (`bandPosition` clamps to [0,1], nulls a bandless item) so the single-source contract is locked from both ends. (3) **a sort beats a filter when you want both ends of a spectrum** — the queued "rich track" would have been a second filter mirroring the "cheap track"; a sortable column reveals cheapest→richest in one control, so I shipped the more general thing and retired the narrower queued idea. (4) the brick was *chosen* by ruling out a richer-sounding one: "fills while away" died on inspection because the engine's 512-trade rolling window (`exchange.ts:6`, ~41 agents trading) evicts a player's away-fills long before the post-catch-up `recordFills` — doing it right needs an engine-side per-player fill accumulator (replay-affecting → Jesse-gated). Verify the data-availability mechanism before designing the UI; a feature that renders empty 90% of the time is worse than not shipping it. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 199.

## Phase 15p — Citation-Drift Audit (2026-06-12)

229. **The one source-comment citation in the whole codebase was the load-bearing one — and it had drifted.** After ~29 UI bricks, a citation audit found the source tree carries exactly ONE `file:line` pointer comment: `game.ts:342` → "mirrors the engine's `damage()` MEAN (quest.ts:394)". That comment is the load-bearing one because `expectedHit` *duplicates* the engine's local, unexported, rng-shaped `damage()` across the module boundary (a debt logged at FINDINGS #148) — the pointer is the only thing telling the next dev where the source of truth lives. It had drifted: `damage()` moved to **quest.ts:407** (line 394 now holds `outcome: 'fighting'`), and the active NEXT_STEPS note (line 12) carried the same stale 394. Both fixed → 407. Lessons: (1) **a duplicated formula's pointer comment is higher-risk than the formula itself** — the formula breaks loudly (a pinned test fails), but a drifted *pointer* fails silently: it sends the next reader to an unrelated line, and the "keep in sync" contract rots without any test catching it. Pointer comments need the same audit cadence as the code they point at. (2) **the audit's first job is the real-bug check, not the line number** — before touching the citation I re-derived the equality: `damage()` rolls uniform in [max(1,ceil(atk/3))..max(2,atk)] then subtracts floor(def/4); `expectedHit` returns (lo+hi)/2 − floor(def/4); the mean of a uniform [lo..hi] *is* (lo+hi)/2, so the mirror is still exact. Drift in the *citation* (cosmetic) vs drift in the *logic* (a lying forecast) are different severities — check the dangerous one first, then fix the cosmetic one. (3) **don't rewrite historical records to "fix" their citations** — FINDINGS #148 (brick 114) and #207 (brick 176) cite quest.ts:394 / App.tsx:900, which were correct *when written*; they're append-only point-in-time narrative anchored to brick numbers. Bumping their line numbers would falsify history. The fix belongs only in citations a reader *follows today* (the live source comment + the active NEXT_STEPS note), not in the ledger of what was once true. (4) a maintenance brick is the right pivot when feature value thins: it found a real (if cosmetic) rot AND re-certified a duplication debt is still sound — both beat shipping a 30th feature nobody asked for. UI/doc-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 198.

## Phase 15o — Value-Band on Open Positions (+ toast-system review) (2026-06-12)

228. **The same fundamental signal answers the BUY question on the market and the SELL question on your holdings — value-band on the Positions panel turns "where does this sit in its band?" into "which of my holdings is ripe to offload?".** P&L (vs your cost) and value-band (vs fundamental floor/ceiling) are orthogonal sell signals: a holding can be in profit yet cheap (hold for more) or in profit and rich (near its ceiling — sell). Adding the 🟡/⚪/🟢 tag to each position completes `valueBand`'s reach across every actionable surface (market browse 15f, mood 15g, watchlist 15i, and now holdings). Lessons: (1) a single pure signal earns its keep by appearing on every surface where its question is asked — the SAME `valueBand` reframes from "is this cheap to buy?" (market) to "is this rich to sell?" (positions) with zero new logic; build the primitive once, then place it wherever a decision needs it. (2) the toast-system audit this turn paid off as a NEGATIVE result worth recording: `setToast` is a plain `useState` (last-writer-wins, "priority" = call order within `refreshProgress`), and the session's cross-effect toasts (best-haul, extract-recap, streak, region-unlock) are temporally separated (combat vs extract vs daily-load fire on different ticks) and the one real collision (record extract) was ordered correctly in 15n — so no clobbering bug exists; confirming soundness and writing down WHY is as valuable as a fix, and prevents re-litigating it. (3) after a long build run, a deliberate review pass is the honest move when feature value is thinning — it either finds a bug (high value) or certifies the work (confidence), both better than churning a feature nobody needed. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 197.

## Phase 15n — Extract Recap (2026-06-12)

227. **A branch that handled the bad outcome but not the good one left the SUCCESS path silent — death toasted a recap, extract toasted nothing, so an ordinary good dive ended with no acknowledgement.** The push-your-luck loop's whole tension is extract-vs-push, and the payoff for extracting (you made it out with the haul) had no beat; adding a "🎒 Returned from {region}: banked N · M cleared" toast closes it. Lessons: (1) when you find an `if (badOutcome) { feedback }` with no `else`, ask whether the GOOD outcome deserves feedback too — symmetry of acknowledgement matters; a system that only narrates failure (you died, you lost) and stays mute on success trains the player to dread the loop instead of enjoying the win. (2) toast ORDERING is a real constraint when two handlers can fire on one event — the local extract toast and App's record "best-haul" toast (inside `onDelveEnd`) compete for the single slot, so firing the local toast FIRST and `onDelveEnd` LAST lets the rarer, bigger celebration win on top; with last-writer-wins, you sequence by importance (common recap first, special celebration last). (3) gate the recap on a non-empty result (`packGp > 0 || cleared > 0`) so an immediate bail-out doesn't toast a hollow "banked 0 · 0 cleared" — the earn-its-appearance rule again: a recap of nothing is noise. (4) reordering a side-effecting call needs a regression check on the OTHER path — moving `onDelveEnd` after the death toast could have changed death behavior, so the existing death test passing is the proof the reorder was safe both ways, not just for the new branch. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 196.

## Phase 15m — Daily-Streak Celebration (2026-06-12)

226. **A loop with only its STICK and no CARROT is half-built — the daily streak had the FOMO nudge ("keep your streak or lose it") but never the reward for keeping it, so the one moment that should feel good (you came back, it ticked up) passed silently.** Adding a "🔥 N-day streak!" toast on every continuation supplies the positive-reinforcement half. Lessons: (1) audit retention loops for BOTH halves — loss-aversion (the at-risk nudge) and reward (the celebration); a streak that only warns of breaking trains anxiety, while one that also cheers continuing trains a habit, and the codebase had built the warning (`streakAtRisk`) without its twin. (2) the celebration was a 3-line addition because the detection point already existed — `bumpStreak` was already computed in an effect that fires once per day (idempotent), so a pure `streakCelebration(prev, next)` slots in beside the existing `setStreak`; the cheapest place to add a beat is wherever the state ALREADY transitions. (3) gate the toast on a GENUINE event — `next.count > prev.count && >= 2` fires only on a real continuation, not the first day (a hollow "1-day streak!") nor a reset-to-1 after a break (which would cruelly "celebrate" losing a 30-streak); the predicate encodes "this is worth cheering," same earn-its-appearance discipline as every prior celebration. (4) this completes the progression-celebration set — level-up (14e), best-haul (15e), region-unlock (15h), and now the daily streak (15m): every threshold the player crosses, in combat, raiding, exploring, AND showing up, now has its moment. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 195.

## Phase 15l — Tradeable Event Chips (2026-06-12)

225. **An informational readout that names an OPPORTUNITY should be the doorway to acting on it — the active-event strip told you a craze was live on an item, while the whole reward (the Storm-Trader play) is trading that item, yet the chip just sat there.** Turning the chip into a button that loads the item in the ticket makes "see the event → trade it" one click. Lessons: (1) the readout→action test ("can the player act on this without leaving it?") keeps paying out brick after brick — cut-losers (14s), best-buy (14z), and now event chips were all a readout that implied an action the UI made the user perform manually; the fix is always the same thin callback over primitives that already exist (`setSelected` + `pickRoom`, the exact pair the Delve-Log jump and best-buy use). (2) the icon work created the SECOND reason to touch this chip — auditing for "show the real icon" (15j/15k) surfaced the bare-name event chip, and once you're editing it for the icon, making it actionable is nearly free; unrelated passes often converge on the same neglected spot, and batching them is cheaper than two visits. (3) a `<span>`→`<button>` swap needs a CSS reset (`font-family/color: inherit; cursor: pointer`) or the browser's button defaults leak through the chip styling — the cheapest accessibility upgrade (real button = keyboard + focus + role) costs three CSS lines. (4) test the OUTCOME the user cares about, not the mechanism — assert the ticket header shows the event item after the click (the player's actual goal), not that an internal setState fired; the test reads like the feature's promise. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 194.

## Phase 15k — Item Icons Across the Cockpit (2026-06-12)

224. **A polish fix has a blast radius — Jesse pointed at equipped items, but the same gap (item shown as bare text) lived in five more panels, so "where we can" meant finishing the sweep, not patching the one example.** 15j iconned equipment/inventory/pack; 15k carried `ItemIcon` to the trading cockpit (top flips, positions, the trade tape ×3, realized profit, movers) where items were still plain names. Lessons: (1) when a user reports a representational gap, treat the report as a SAMPLE, not the spec — grep for the same shape (`<span>{names.get(id)}</span>` with no icon) everywhere and fix the class, or the same complaint recurs panel by panel; one user-caught instance usually has siblings. (2) reuse from the prior brick made this a near-mechanical sweep — `ItemIcon` already existed (15j), so each of five panels was the identical three-edit pattern (import, `wikiOf`, icon-in-span); the first brick's extraction is what makes the second a sweep instead of five inventions. (3) de-risk a repetitive multi-file change on ONE instance first — I did PositionsPanel and ran its tests before the other four, confirming the `getByText` concern was moot (panel tests use real `DEFAULT_ITEMS` → wikiId present → PNG renders → an `<img>` adds no text → the name span's text is unchanged); proving the pattern safe once beats discovering breakage after editing all five. (4) an `<img>`-based icon is test-transparent by nature — unlike a glyph-emoji, it contributes no text content, so dropping it INTO a name span leaves `getByText(name)` working; choosing the representation that doesn't perturb the DOM text is what let the icon go where it visually belongs (left of the name) without a single assertion change. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 193.

## Phase 15j — Real Item Icons for Equipment/Inventory (Jesse-requested) (2026-06-12)

223. **An asset already in the build was being shown in only ONE of the places it belonged — the wikiId item PNGs (121 of them in public/icons) rendered in the MarketTable but every other item surface fell back to a generic ⚔/🛡 glyph, so a player's rune 2h sword looked like a category, not an item.** Jesse caught it ("equipped is a generic icon when it could be the actual item icon"). The fix is one reusable `ItemIcon` (PNG when the catalog has a wikiId, glyph otherwise) swapped in at every equipped/inventory/pack site. Lessons: (1) when an asset/pattern lives inline in ONE component, it's a candidate to extract and spread — the MarketTable had quietly solved "show the real icon" for itself, and the rest of the app never inherited it; a good audit question is "what nice thing does ONE screen do that the others should too?" (the same extract-and-scale as 15f/15i, here for an image not a formula). (2) a fallback chain makes a swap test-SAFE by construction — `ItemIcon` returns the glyph when `wikiId` is absent, and tests pass MINIMAL ItemDefs (no wikiId), so all 335 prior tests stayed green on the glyph path while production (real catalog) lights up the PNG; design the new thing to degrade to the old thing and the migration is free. (3) the glyph fallback is NOT always an emoji — `Icon` renders the SVG asset when one exists (skill-attack.svg does), so a test asserting the literal '⚔' was wrong; assert the CONTRACT (no wikiId → not the item PNG) not an incidental representation, the same robust-invariant discipline as 15d. (4) a user catching a polish gap is high-signal — the feature WORKED, it just looked generic, which is exactly the kind of thing that's invisible in tests (glyphs and PNGs both "render fine") and visible to a human looking at the screen; ship-and-look beats assume-it's-right. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 192.

## Phase 15i — Watchlist Decision Board (2026-06-12)

222. **A curated list the player already maintains is the cheapest place to add decision power — the watchlist held the items you care about but only showed price+trend+alerts, so judging "act or wait?" still meant loading each into the ticket.** Adding the two decision metrics it lacked (flip margin = is it flippable now, value-band = cheap/rich) turns the watchlist into a self-contained board. Lessons: (1) when the player has hand-picked a SET (watchlist, positions, loadout), that set is a high-value surface to enrich — the curation already happened, so every metric you add there pays off on exactly the items they've signalled they care about; enriching a curated list beats building a new selection mechanism. (2) extract-then-reuse retires duplication at the moment of the SECOND consumer — `flipMargin` was fine as a MarketTable local until the watchlist needed it; lifting it to game.ts (exported) and pointing BOTH at it means the market column and the watch row can never disagree on a margin, the same single-source move as 15f's `valueBand` (the pattern: the instant a second caller appears, extract rather than copy). (3) mind the orphaned import after an extraction — removing the local `flipMargin` left `GE_TAX_RATE` unused in MarketTable; `noUnusedLocals` would flag it, so the extraction's cleanup includes pruning the now-dead import (the donor shouldn't carry a dependency only the moved code used). (4) reuse the data already in props — WatchlistPanel already received `items` + `view`, so the value-band (needs defs) and margin (needs the book) computed with no new plumbing; a curated-set panel usually already holds everything its richer metrics need. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 191.

## Phase 15h — "New Frontier Unlocked!" Celebration (2026-06-12)

221. **The celebration's detection site is dictated by WHEN the state updates — and `questProgress` (unlike a delve record) updates synchronously inside the dive-clearing command, so the level-up baseline-in-refreshProgress is exactly the right home, the mirror-image of 15e's lesson.** 15e moved best-haul detection OUT of refreshProgress because the delve was appended LATER (by onDelveEnd); 15h keeps region-unlock detection IN refreshProgress because the frontier index climbs inside the command, visible the same tick. Same question ("where does this state land relative to the refresh?"), opposite answer — and getting it right means the celebration fires reliably. Lessons: (1) two superficially-identical features (both "celebrate a progression event") can need OPPOSITE detection sites purely because of data-arrival timing; the discipline is to trace each event's update path, not to pattern-match "celebrations go in refreshProgress." (2) reuse the swap-guard that's already there: region-unlock re-baselines on the SAME `levelBaselineGame.current !== game` check as the level-up, folded into the same if/else, so a deeper cloud-adopted save can't false-fire and there's one game-swap guard, not two that could drift. (3) every progression THRESHOLD the player crosses deserves a beat — leveling (14e), a best haul (15e), and now opening a new region; the map silently changing was a missed moment, and a per-unlock toast (distinct from the once-at-a-depth "Frontier Pioneer" milestone) marks each push deeper. (4) test-assertion scoping: the region name appears in BOTH the toast flavor and the RegionMap label, so `getByText(/name/)` is ambiguous — scope the assertion to the toast element (`.closest('.toast')`) and check its `textContent`, asserting the celebration's content without colliding with the same string elsewhere on screen. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 190.

## Phase 15g — Market Mood / Breadth Read (2026-06-12)

220. **Every market signal had been per-item — the macro lens (how's the WHOLE market doing?) was the obvious missing altitude, and it's just a count over the signals that already exist.** Movers name the top few leaders; value-band tags one item; but nothing said "40 up / 30 down, broadly cheap" — the breadth read a trader uses to time the environment (broad rally vs selloff). `marketMood` aggregates `view.markets` into up/down/cheap/rich counts. Lessons: (1) when a system has many PER-ITEM signals, the fresh feature is often the AGGREGATE — zoom out, not in; "summarize the whole set" is a different altitude than "rank the items," and breadth (advance/decline) is a classic macro read that no amount of per-item detail substitutes for. (2) the aggregate reuses the per-item primitives, inventing nothing — `up`/`down` is last-vs-EMA (the movers' own comparison) counted, `cheap`/`rich` is `valueBand` (15f) counted; a macro lens built from the established micro signals is automatically consistent with them and free of new formulas. (3) exclude the meaningless from the count — breadth only counts items with `volume > 0`, because an untraded item's "last vs EMA" is noise (both seeded equal); a breadth number that included never-traded items would overstate participation. (4) the aggregate needs no new data or plumbing — `MarketTable` already had `view.markets` + `items`, so the mood line is a pure function of what's on screen, dropped above the table; the highest-leverage features are often a new VIEW of data already in hand (the recurring theme: 14y's free snapshot, 15c's groupBy, 15f's extract-and-scale). UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 189.

## Phase 15f — "Cheap" Value-Band Market Track (2026-06-12)

219. **A signal already computed for ONE item is usually worth computing for ALL of them — the ticket told you whether the SELECTED item was cheap/fair/rich in its fundamental band, and the same one-line formula, applied across the market, becomes a whole accumulation shortlist.** Prices here anchor in `[baseCost..consumeValue]`, so "where in its band?" is a real value-investing lens orthogonal to flip-margin (spread) and movers (EMA) — and it was sitting unused at scale because it lived inline in the TradeTicket for a single selection. Lessons: (1) when a per-item readout encodes a genuine signal, ask whether the MARKET-WIDE version (a filter, a sort, a marker) is a higher-leverage surface — a thing you compute for the one item you clicked is often more valuable as "show me every item like this," which is a filter over the same formula. (2) extract-then-reuse is the right shape, not copy: I lifted the ticket's inline cheap/fair/rich into a pure `valueBand(def, lastPrice)` and made the ticket CALL it, so the per-item tag and the new market filter are literally the same code — they can't drift, and tuning the 0.34/0.67 thresholds moves both at once (the recurring single-source discipline, now retiring a soon-to-be-duplicated formula instead of adding one). (3) a new analytical lens earns its place by being ORTHOGONAL to the existing ones: 'flippable' filters by live spread, 'cheap' by fundamental position — a player can now ask "what's cheap AND flippable?" by reading one track then sorting, a query neither alone expresses; added lenses are cheap when they answer a genuinely different question. (4) widening a predicate's input is the small enabling step — `inTrack` only declared `{itemId,bestBid,bestAsk}` but was always called with the full row, so adding `lastPrice` to its type unlocked the value-band check with no call-site change. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 188.

## Phase 15e — "New Best Haul!" Celebration (2026-06-12)

218. **WHERE you detect an event is dictated by WHEN its data lands — the level-up celebration lives in `refreshProgress` because levels update synchronously on the command, but a delve is latched LATER (by `onDelveEnd`, after refreshProgress), so the same baseline-ref pattern would miss the very dive it's meant to celebrate.** A new-best-haul toast keyed off a refreshProgress baseline would compare against a `game.delves` that doesn't yet contain the just-finished dive. The fix is to detect at the APPEND point — `onDelveEnd` already has the new record and the prior log — which is also SIMPLER: no identity-keyed baseline, no swap-guard (onDelveEnd fires only on a real dive-end, never on a game swap, so the 14e/14f swap-bug class can't apply). Lessons: (1) before copying a celebration pattern, trace the data's arrival time — "diff a baseline in the per-tick refresh" only works for state that updates IN that refresh; for state appended by a later callback, detect IN that callback, comparing new-vs-prior at the moment of the append. The right detection site eliminates the timing race AND the baseline machinery. (2) a callback that fires only on the real event needs no swap-guard — the swap-bug class (a mount-baseline that wrongly persists/resets across a game swap) exists only for refs that live ACROSS events; an event-scoped handler that gets the prior+new in its arguments is immune by construction. (3) "first sets the bar silently, only a genuine beat celebrates" — gating on `prior !== null` means the first survived dive doesn't fire a hollow "new best!" on a 10-gp run; the celebration earns its appearance (the recurring don't-cry-wolf discipline: 14x/14y/15a/15c). (4) compute-before-mutate: read `isNewBestHaul(game.delves, record)` BEFORE `log.push(record)`, or the record would be compared against itself and never count as a beat. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 187.

## Phase 15d — Peak-Dive Records (2026-06-12)

217. **Aggregates and peaks are different progression stories, and a records panel full of totals was quietly missing the "your best ever" half.** The Adventurer's Record had lifetime SUMS (monsters slain, deaths, contracts) but no MAX — no "biggest single haul," the RPG counterpart to the trading side's daily-best and net-worth peak. Adding two peaks (`diveRecords`: best banked haul + most cleared in one dive) over the existing Delve Log fills it. Lessons: (1) when a stats surface is all aggregates, audit for the missing PEAK — "how much total" and "what's your best single" are distinct satisfactions, and games lean on peaks for the brag-worthy memory ("250k from the Abyss in one run"); a panel of sums alone undersells the highlight reel. (2) the qualifier encodes the rule: best HAUL counts only SURVIVED dives because a death forfeits the loot (it was never banked — not a haul), while most-CLEARED counts any dive because you cleared those foes even if you fell after; one helper, two correctly-different filters over the same records. (3) keep the existing helper's contract — `recordRows(st)` stayed stats-only (its signature and tests unchanged) and the delve-derived peaks were appended in the COMPONENT, so a new data source (game.delves) didn't force a breaking change on a pure function consumed elsewhere. (4) test against the robust invariant, not the brittle format: `fmtCompact(1500)` isn't compacted, so the render test asserts the haul row NAMES its region (always true) rather than a specific "1.5K" string (threshold-dependent) — assert what the feature guarantees, not an incidental formatting detail. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 186.

## Phase 15c — Per-Region Raid Stats (2026-06-12)

216. **The same persisted log answers a richer question when you slice it differently — the Delve Log already held every dive's region/loot/outcome, so "which region is my best farm?" was a `groupBy` away, not a new data pipeline.** The panel showed dives chronologically + one all-dives total; grouping by `regionId` surfaces the farm-optimization lens ("the Abyss: 5 runs, 3 deaths, −80k net; Brimhaven: +180k") with zero new state. Lessons: (1) a flat aggregate (`raidTotals` over everything) and a grouped one (`raidTotalsByRegion`) are different analytical lenses on identical data — when you have a log, ask what DIMENSIONS it could be cut along (by region, by outcome, by time) before assuming a new feature needs new tracking; the second lens is usually pure aggregation. (2) gate the grouped view on when the comparison is MEANINGFUL — with one region raided, the per-region row just restates the header total, so showing the breakdown only at ≥2 regions keeps it from being redundant noise (the same earn-its-appearance discipline as 14x/15a). (3) the scout-with-existence-checks paid off after the 15b duplicate: it VERIFIED no `raidTotalsByRegion` existed and correctly flagged that ConquestPanel's per-region view is a DIFFERENT axis (monster mastery, not raid risk/reward) — distinguishing "same surface, different metric" from "already built" is exactly the check that 15b skipped. (4) honest deferral: the scout's higher-ranked Gap #1 (per-dive item loot) looked UI-only but `ExpeditionState` merges drops into `pack` with no per-dive loot-items accumulator, so doing it RIGHT needs a replay-affecting engine field — deferred rather than hacked with fragile pack-diffing; picking the clean adjacent gap beats forcing the headline one. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 185.

## Phase 15b — Deep-Link the Onboarding Steps (2026-06-12)

215. **GREP BEFORE YOU BUILD — I designed and half-wrote a whole new onboarding "first steps" checklist (helper + latching component + placement) before discovering `FirstSteps`/`firstSteps` had shipped in brick 11v, fully wired and tested.** The Write tool refusing to overwrite the "new" file is what surfaced the duplicate; one `grep FirstSteps` up front would have saved the round-trip. The recovery turned the wasted scoping into a real brick: ENHANCE the existing checklist (make its static steps deep-link to the right tab) instead of replacing it. Lessons: (1) the CLAUDE.md rule "search for existing patterns before implementing" isn't bureaucracy — in a 180-brick codebase the odds that a "fresh idea" already exists are high, and the cost of checking (one grep) is trivial against the cost of building a duplicate; do it as the FIRST step of any feature, not after designing. (2) discovering a feature exists reframes the work from "build it" to "what's the next increment ON it" — the existing checklist NAMED the fundamentals but didn't take you there, so the readout→action pattern (14s/14z) had an obvious home: an unfinished step becomes a button that calls `pickRoom`. The duplicate-discovery handed me a better, smaller brick than the one I'd planned. (3) reuse the navigation primitive verbatim — `onGo={pickRoom}` is the SAME room-switch the tabs fire, so a step-link behaves identically to clicking the tab; an action assembled from an existing verb needs no new logic and can't surprise. (4) gate the affordance on actionability: only an UNFINISHED step with a `goto` links (a done step is a record, not a to-do; the time-gate step has nowhere to go) — the link appears exactly where it helps. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 184.

## Phase 15a — Typical-Foe Push Read (2026-06-12)

214. **A worst-case readout protects but also over-scares — pairing it with the EXPECTED case, shown only when they disagree, turns "always be afraid of the rare terror" into a real risk/reward read.** The push read judged survival vs the region's HARDEST foe (correct for "can I survive the worst?"), but in an elite region that's a rare named terror, so "risky" fired even when the common encounter was a walkover. Adding a TYPICAL-foe verdict (the regular pool's average, elite excluded) and surfacing it ONLY when it differs from the hardest gives "risky vs the worst, but usually favored." Lessons: (1) a conservative metric (worst case) and an expected metric (typical case) answer different questions — survival-floor vs likely-outcome — and a push-your-luck decision wants both; showing only the floor makes every elite region read as a wall. (2) gate the second opinion on DISAGREEMENT — when typical and hardest give the same verdict the tag adds nothing, so rendering it only on `ft.favored !== f.favored` keeps a dense line clean and makes the tag's appearance itself meaningful ("the common case is better than the worst" / "even the common case is dicey"), the same earn-its-appearance discipline as 14x's gated warning and 14y's news floor. (3) "average ≤ max" is a free, robust test property: the pool average can never exceed the roster's hardest, and strictly under-shoots it wherever an elite lives — so the helper's correctness is checkable against `regionDanger` without hardcoding any monster's stats, which would rot when the catalog is tuned. (4) the positive render path follows from the negative by symmetry: the tag's trigger is a single `!==` boolean, so a negative-render test (verdicts agree → no tag) plus the helper unit covers the branch without brittle stat-tuning to force a disagreement on screen (honest coverage accounting). UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 183.

## Phase 14z — Click-to-Buy the Upgrade Advisor (2026-06-12)

213. **A recommendation that names the right move but can't perform it is half a feature — the same readout→action gap that 14s closed for "underwater position → sell it" applies to "best upgrade → buy it," and the fix is the identical shape: make the advice a button.** 14v computed the best affordable upgrade; acting on it still meant switching tabs and hunting the item among 128. A "→ buy" chip that does `setSelected(id) + pickRoom('exchange')` collapses that into one gesture. Lessons: (1) when you ship a recommendation, immediately ask "can the player ACT on this without leaving the recommendation?" — naming the move and performing it are two features, and the second is usually a thin callback over plumbing that already exists (select + room-switch were both already there); the recommendation is the expensive part, the action is nearly free. (2) the action reuses primitives, invents nothing — `setSelected`/`pickRoom` are the same calls the tabs and Delve-Log jump use, so "go buy this" behaves identically to every other navigation; an action built from existing verbs is predictable. (3) respect-the-player on the destructive/committal edge: the chip LOADS the ticket, it doesn't auto-buy — same stance as 14s's two-tap and 11u's prefill, the player always places the final order; convenience shouldn't become "spent your gp for you." (4) `exactOptionalPropertyTypes` gotcha when THREADING an optional callback through a layer: a prop typed `onBuy?: (id)=>void` won't accept a value of type `((id)=>void) | undefined`, so a forwarded optional must be typed `?: ((id)=>void) | undefined` — the standard fix, cleaner than conditionally spreading the prop in JSX. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 182.

## Phase 14y — "Biggest Mover While Away" (2026-06-12)

212. **The data for an idle game's signature payoff was already being computed and thrown away — `planOfflineProgress` built a full `playerView` snapshot before fast-forwarding and used it only for a null-check, so "what did the market do while you were gone?" cost one `Object.fromEntries`, not a new capture system.** The away-bar told you what you EARNED but not what the WORLD did; surfacing the biggest price mover ("Rune plate +23% while away") is the "the world didn't sleep" moment idle games live on. Lessons: (1) before building a capture mechanism, check what the code already computes-and-discards — a snapshot taken for one purpose (the null-check view) is often exactly the snapshot another feature needs; the cheapest new data is data you're already holding. (2) a "headline" readout needs a NEWS FLOOR or it lies by precision — reporting the max mover unconditionally would surface a 0.3% wiggle as if it mattered, so the 3% floor (return null below it) is what makes the line meaningful WHEN it appears, the same don't-cry-wolf discipline as 14x's gated warning. (3) skip-invalid is load-bearing in a market diff: items with a zero or missing start/end price (never traded, newly listed) would produce Infinity/NaN %s — guarding `start > 0 && end > 0` keeps a divide-by-zero from becoming the "biggest mover." (4) testing a data-dependent integration deterministically: rather than run 5000 sim ticks and hope a price clears 3% (brittle), the plumbing test calls `finishOfflineProgress` with NO ticks (after == before → null, proving the flat path) and with a hand-halved `pricesBefore` (→ +100% mover, proving the diff path) — exercising both branches of the real wiring without depending on the sim's emergent prices. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 181.

## Phase 14x — New-Game Abandon Warning (2026-06-12)

211. **A destructive action deserves a warning PROPORTIONAL to what's actually lost — and "actually lost" is computable, so the honest guard quantifies it and stays silent when there's nothing at stake.** `restart()` `clearSave()`s the run irrecoverably; the challenge path warned, the manual new-game path didn't (the same protect-here-but-not-its-sibling gap as 14w). The fix isn't a reflexive "are you sure?" — it's "⚠ wipes this run — 250K net · 7 deeds", shown only once you've earned a deed or grown past your stake. Lessons: (1) a confirmation that fires every time trains the user to dismiss it reflexively — gating the warning on real progress (`deeds > 0 || worth > startGp`) means its APPEARANCE carries information ("you have something to lose now"), so it isn't wallpaper; a guard you always see is a guard you stop reading. (2) quantify the stakes instead of asking a yes/no — "what will I lose?" is the actual question behind a restart prompt, and answering it concretely (net worth + deed count) lets the player decide, where a generic modal just adds a click. (3) match the guard to the irreversibility: `clearSave()` is unrecoverable (only a ghost survives), which is exactly when a warning is worth the friction — and exactly when crying wolf on a fresh run would be most corrosive to trust. (4) the consistency-gap heuristic keeps paying out: "this sibling surface lacks the protection that one has" (14w keyboard, 14x warning) is a reliable generator of real, scoped work — audit for affordances/guards present in one place and absent in its twin. (5) pure helper + thin render: `restartStakes(game, worth)` holds the gating logic (unit-tested in isolation) while App just renders it — the stakes computation can't hide a bug in JSX. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 180.

## Phase 14w — Keyboard Embark Navigation (2026-06-12)

210. **A consistency gap is its own to-do list — half the game (the trade loop) was keyboard-drivable and half (the adventure embark) wasn't, and that asymmetry is a clearer signal of what to build next than any feature wish.** The trade cockpit reached j/k → b/s → Enter; the embark stayed mouse-only. Closing the gap (←/→ region, Enter embark) made the whole game reachable without the mouse. Lessons: (1) when a UX affordance exists in ONE place, the absence of it in a sibling surface is a bug-shaped feature — "this flow can't do what that flow can" is a sharper spec than "add keyboard support," and the existing surface even hands you the pattern (the 12g `active`-gated window listener) to copy. (2) gate a global `keydown` on BOTH the active surface AND the event target — `active && tagName !== INPUT/TEXTAREA/SELECT` — or the listener hijacks typing elsewhere or fires on the wrong tab; a window-level handler is powerful precisely because it's global, so its guards are the whole correctness story. (3) one action, two triggers must share ONE function: extracting `doEmbark()` so the button's onClick and the Enter key call the identical path (same locked-guard, same draft→pack) means they can never drift — the recurring single-source discipline, now applied to an action rather than a number. (4) hooks-rule placement: the keydown `useEffect` had to go BEFORE the in-dive `return null`, because a component that conditionally returns can't have a hook after the branch; the effect's own `active && !expedition` guard does the "should I act?" work that the early return does for rendering. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 179.

## Phase 14v — Best-Affordable-Upgrade Advisor (2026-06-12)

209. **N per-item readouts don't add up to one recommendation — the game could tell you whether ANY given piece was an upgrade (gearDelta, badges, the buy preview), but never which of the 128 items was the best NEXT buy, and the synthesis is a different, higher-value question than the lookup.** Every prior gear aid answered "is THIS good?"; the advisor answers "what should I do?" by scanning the whole catalog against your purse and levels and naming one move. Lessons: (1) once you have a good per-item scoring primitive (`gearDelta`), the high-leverage follow-up is often the ARGMAX over it — a recommendation is just "run the scorer across the whole set and surface the winner," and it's disproportionately valuable because it collapses a 128-item search into a sentence; build the primitive first, then the synthesis falls out almost free. (2) a recommendation earns trust by its EXCLUSIONS — afford (a live ask ≤ gp, not a theoretical price), use (level-met), and not-already-owned (owning it means equip, a different action) are each a way the naive "max delta" answer would have lied; the honest filters are what make "best buy" actually buyable-and-useful rather than aspirational. (3) reuse the exact scorer the per-item badges use (`gearDelta`) so the advisor's "+45" and the item's own badge can never disagree — one source of truth across the lookup and the synthesis (the recurring single-formula discipline). (4) deterministic tie-breaks matter even in advice: iterate sorted ids and break ties by lower price then id, so the "best buy" is stable run-to-run and doesn't flicker between equal-delta pieces as the book churns. (5) the data the recommendation needs (markets + gp) lived one prop away (`view`); adding it OPTIONAL kept every existing GearManager render site unchanged while unlocking the feature — opt-in capability, not a forced dependency. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 178.

## Phase 14u — Region-Themed Combat Atmosphere (2026-06-12)

208. **Distinctness in DATA isn't distinctness in FEEL — the eight regions had eight danger profiles but one identical stage, and a system can be mechanically rich while emotionally flat.** Every fight rendered on the same transparent arena regardless of whether you were in the soft Lumbridge plains or the light-eating Abyss; the descent was labelled (danger reads, names) but never SEEN. A muted per-region gradient backdrop makes the journey's escalation visible at the moment it lands hardest — mid-fight. Lessons: (1) audit for places where the model differentiates but the presentation doesn't — wherever you have N variants that all look the same, there's cheap immersion to be had by letting the variant show; the eight regions were the obvious candidate hiding in plain sight after a long run of purely-functional bricks. (2) atmosphere must not cost legibility — the palettes are deliberately MUTED and dark so the hp bars, figures, and hit-splats (the information) stay readable on top; mood is a layer UNDER the readout, never a competitor to it. The instinct from the frontend-design guidance ("create atmosphere rather than defaulting to solid colours") is right, but in a data-dense game it's bounded by "the numbers must still win the contrast fight." (3) a presentational lookup keyed by id stays robust with a default branch — `arenaTheme(unknown) → neutral stone` means a renamed/new region degrades to a sane stage instead of crashing or rendering `undefined`, the same defensive-default discipline the gameplay helpers use. (4) the prop was added OPTIONAL with a fallback, so the one render site opts in (`exp.regionId`) while every existing/test render of CombatScene is unchanged — additive capability, not a breaking signature. (5) clip discipline: a full-bleed SVG `<rect>` backdrop needs `overflow:hidden` on a border-radiused container or its square corners poke past the rounded frame — a backdrop must respect the box it fills. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 177.

## Phase 14t — RegionMap Selection Flash + a 14r correction (2026-06-12)

207. **Verify mount behavior before you reason from it — the same one-grep check ("how do tabs render?") both ENABLED this brick and EXPOSED that the previous brick's finding was wrong.** 14t needed to know whether RegionMap stays mounted when you jump to it from another tab (so a `useEffect([selected])` would fire) — grepping App found all rooms render unconditionally and hide via `.tabhidden` ("display rules beat `hidden`," App.tsx:900). That confirmed the flash works on a live `selected` change AND revealed that 14r's FINDINGS #205 claim — "WealthPanel unmounts on tab switch" — was false; tabs never unmount on a switch, so a panel-local baseline would have worked too (#205 now carries the correction). Lessons: (1) a claim about component lifetime is a FACTUAL claim about the render tree, checkable in one grep (`room === 'x' && <…>` unmounts; a `.hidden`/`.tabhidden` class does not) — I pattern-matched "tabs ⇒ unmount" without checking and shipped a wrong rationale; the fix's code was still right, but right-code-for-a-wrong-reason is a latent trap for the next reader who trusts the reason. (2) the durable principle survives the correction: a captured baseline's HOLDER must outlive the window it measures — App-level is still the better home (centralizes session state, keeps the panel dumb, and is the only choice that survives IF rooms ever go conditional), just not because of an unmount that doesn't happen. (3) self-correct in the permanent record the moment you find a past error, in-place, rather than letting a tidy-but-false lesson stand — the methodology's value is the findings being TRUE, and a visible "Correction:" is worth more than a clean-looking wrong one. (4) the motion itself is a skip-the-mount effect: a `mounted` ref gates the first run so the static starting region doesn't pulse — only a genuine selection CHANGE flashes, which is the difference between "draw the eye to the new pick" and "strobe on every tab open." UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 176.

## Phase 14s — "Cut a Loser" One-Tap Market Sell (2026-06-12)

206. **A pile of readouts eventually needs a verb — the game could SHOW you the underwater position six ways (13n's callout, red rows, paper P&L) but couldn't ACT on it without a five-step trip to the ticket, and at some point the missing thing isn't more information, it's a button that does the obvious thing.** "Cut losers" turns the 13n diagnosis into a one-gesture exit: sell the whole losing position at the best bid, straight from the portfolio. Lessons: (1) when an analysis surface keeps growing, audit it for the ACTION its own data implies — the underwater highlight is a question ("do something?") the UI had been leaving the player to answer manually; a panel that diagnoses a problem is a natural home for the button that fixes it. (2) a destructive, irreversible action in a no-undo game earns a guard PROPORTIONAL to its cost — not a modal (too heavy for a game loop), but a two-tap arm/confirm on the same chip, which is exactly how the existing "sell whole position" (11u) hedged with prefill-then-submit; match the safety pattern the codebase already chose for the same risk class. (3) reuse the command path, don't invent a "market order" — selling at `bestBid` via the existing `place` command takes the top of book and rests any remainder, which IS a market sell in a limit-only book; the honest semantics fell out of the primitive already there, no engine change. (4) an additive action stays backward-safe by gating on its handler: the cut chip renders only when `onCommand` is passed, so every existing `onSelect`-only render site (and its tests) is byte-unchanged — opt-in capability, not a forced prop. (5) `stopPropagation` is load-bearing when an interactive control lives inside a click-through row — without it the cut would also fire the row's load-in-ticket select; a button inside a clickable parent must claim its own clicks. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 175.

## Phase 14r — Session-Scoped P&L (2026-06-12)

205. **A "since you sat down" baseline must live in a holder whose lifetime spans the whole sitting, and you must VERIFY that lifetime rather than assume it — keying it to game identity in App is correct, but the reason matters.** The baseline lives in App (the root), keyed to game identity so it survives ticks (engine mutates `game.world` in place) and resets only on a real swap (cloud-adopt/restart = a new session). **Correction (verified 14t, App.tsx:900):** I first justified App-over-panel by claiming WealthPanel "unmounts on tab switch" — that is FALSE. All three rooms render unconditionally and hide via `.tabhidden` ("display rules beat `hidden`"), so WealthPanel never unmounts on a tab switch; a panel-local ref keyed to game identity would have worked just as well. App-level remains the better call — it centralizes session state, keeps the panel dumb/prop-driven/testable, and is robust IF rooms ever become conditionally-rendered — but it was a clarity/robustness choice, not a fix for a real reset bug. Lessons: (1) the durable principle still holds: a baseline's HOLDER must outlive the window it measures — but you must CHECK the actual mount behavior (grep how tabs render: conditional `&&` unmounts; a `.hidden`/`.tabhidden` class does not), not pattern-match "tabs ⇒ unmount."
