I asserted the unmount without checking and the claim was wrong; the next refactor that flips rooms to conditional rendering is where a panel-local ref WOULD break, which is the real argument for App-level. (2) keep the panel DUMB — WealthPanel takes `sessionStartWorth` as a prop and just renders; App owns the stateful capture, so the panel is trivially testable and can't carry a lifetime bug. (3) two worth deltas with different horizons (lifetime-vs-stake, session-vs-load) are shown as SEPARATE lines, never merged — same shape (`StakeReturn`), different question, different place. (4) honest coverage accounting: helper + panel render are unit-tested; the App-internal ref capture reuses the 14e-proven `!== game` idiom rather than re-testing it in isolation. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 174.

## Phase 14q — "Realm Conquered" Milestone (2026-06-12)

204. **A completion system needs a TERMINAL reward or it quietly tops out into nothing — the ConquestPanel counted fully-mastered regions but earning all 8 paid out only a panel that read "8/8", with no deed to mark the accomplishment.** 14n made per-region progress legible; 14q gives finishing it a capstone in the Hall of Deeds. The whole brick was reuse: `regionMastery` (the panel's own done-logic, already pure) dropped straight into a milestone `achieved`/`progress` pair. Lessons: (1) when you build a progress TRACKER (14n's glyphs) without an achievement at 100%, you've built a treadmill with no finish line — the grind reads as pointless past the visible bar; pairing a tracker with a terminal deed is what turns "see your progress" into "chase a goal." (2) define the deed in terms of the SAME predicate the tracker uses (`regionMastery(...).done`), never a re-derived copy — a milestone that computed "mastered" independently could drift from the panel and award at a different moment than the 👑 appears, breaking the player's mental model; one source, two consumers. (3) "Monster Scholar" (every monster) and "Realm Conquered" (every region's full roster) look interchangeable but aren't — region rosters can repeat a monster and add an elite, so the bars cross different finish lines; reusing `regionMastery` (which dedups + includes the elite) keeps the deed honest to the conquest framing rather than the flat-bestiary one (the #193 surface-distinction lesson, now in the reward layer). (4) milestones living on `Game` (UI), latched by `checkMilestones`, are provably non-replay-affecting — `replay.ts`/`hashState` read only `WorldState` — so a satisfying new reward ships UI-only with no engine.js rebuild. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 173.

## Phase 14p — Realized Price-Swing Readout (2026-06-12)

203. **A static label and a live measurement of the same property are different facts, and the trading decision wants the live one — the item's volatility TIER (staple/exotic) is a fixed attribute, but "how wild is it trading right now" is what tells a flipper the spread might evaporate before both legs fill.** The ticket already showed the static `def.volatility` tier and a sparkline shape, but no NUMBER for realized recent movement — so a "staple" mid-spike looked as safe as a calm one. `priceSwing` reads the same recent-trade window the sparkline draws and reports lo/hi + a peak-to-trough swing% bucketed steady/choppy/wild. Lessons: (1) when a system has a STATIC classification of some property (volatility tier, danger level, difficulty), there's usually value in also surfacing the REALIZED/live version of the same property — the static one sets expectations, the live one catches the exception (a normally-calm item trading wild today); pairing them is more honest than either alone. (2) reuse the data a neighbor already consumes — the sparkline's `recentPrices` was right there, so the swing read added a quantification beside the picture with zero new plumbing; the chart shows the shape, the readout names the magnitude. (3) range (`(hi−lo)/lo`) is the honest, legible metric for this surface — std-dev/OHLC would be "more precise" but harder to read and overkill for a go/no-go flip glance; pick the simplest statistic that supports the decision, and defer the fancy one (noted as a follow-up) rather than gold-plate. (4) a sub-2-points → null guard means low-volume items (which have the thinnest, most spread-fragile books — exactly where you'd want the warning) simply show nothing rather than a fake "0% steady"; honest absence beats a confident wrong reading. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 172.

## Phase 14o — Heal-Aware Push Read (2026-06-12)

202. **A decision aid should show the threat AND your margin separately, not fold them into one verdict — basing favored/risky on hp-plus-food would make every well-stocked push look "favored" and hide the real danger, so the honest design keeps the verdict at RAW hp and adds the food cushion as its own clause.** The push read (13p) judged survival at current hp only, ignoring that a diver carrying 4 sharks has 80 hp of cushion. The naive fix — pass hp+heal into combatForecast and let it flip the verdict — is the WRONG fix: food is finite across a random multi-foe streak, so a single munged "favored" overstates safety and the player loses sight of the raw threat. Lessons: (1) when new information improves an estimate, ask whether it belongs IN the headline metric or BESIDE it — survival-with-food is real but conditional (you must keep eating, the next foe is random), so it's a margin to display ("🍖 food +≈N rounds"), not a fact to bake into the risk verdict; transparency beats a smarter-looking single number. (2) name your model's optimism in the code AND the tooltip — `healFromPack` sums raw heal and ignores overheal, which overestimates; that's acceptable BECAUSE the forecast already frames itself as "an estimate, not a promise," and the helper's doc + the clause's title say so, rather than pretending precision the model doesn't have. (3) the suppression of the "bank your haul?" nag uses the WITH-food forecast (`ff.favored`) while the verdict uses the raw one — different sub-decisions can correctly read different models: "should I warn you to bank?" accounts for the food you'd eat, "how dangerous is this raw?" does not. (4) a pure helper kept the whole brick UI-only and testable in isolation (`healFromPack({shark:2,...})===58`) — the same pure-function discipline that made `gearDelta`/`combatForecast` reusable. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 171.

## Phase 14n — Conquest Roster Glyphs (2026-06-12)

201. **A progress fraction names that work remains but not WHICH — "3/5 mastered" is a number you can't act on, while the roster strip turns the same data into a target by showing the two dim glyphs you still have to hunt.** The conquest codex (12f) had per-region slain/total bars, so a completionist knew a region wasn't done but had to cross-reference the flat Bestiary to find the specific foes left. Rendering the region's actual roster as lit/dim `MonsterGlyph`s makes the gap legible: the dim ones ARE the to-do list. Lessons: (1) a completion surface should show the ITEMS, not just the count — an aggregate metric measures progress but a visible roster directs it; the panel built to drive 100% conquest was the one place the foes themselves belonged (the #193 "same data, the job differs per surface" lesson, applied inside one subsystem: the Bestiary is the flat all-foes reference, the Conquest roster is the per-region which-is-left checklist). (2) The brick was pure composition because the pieces were already pure and shared — `MonsterGlyph` (deterministic per-id portrait, already used by combat + bestiary), `regionRoster` (deduped, order-stable), and `killsByMonster` (the lifetime tally) snapped together with zero new computation; reusing the SAME glyph means a foe looks identical wherever it appears, which is what makes the dim-vs-lit read instant. (3) Lit/dim is the whole UX — grayscale + 0.22 opacity on `.unmet` needs no legend because "faded = not done" is universally read, and the per-glyph title carries the kill count for the hover. (4) Restructuring a flex row into a stacked header+strip means overriding the shared `.rows li` flex (→ column) on just `.conquest-row` — a reminder that a base list style is a contract the row variant must explicitly opt out of, not silently inherit. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 170.

## Phase 14m — Move "Plan a Dive" to the Right Column (user request) (2026-06-12)

200. **A panel that grows past the fold is a layout smell the user feels before you do — the embark flow kept getting richer brick-by-brick (danger read 13z/14b, forecast, prep warnings, equip-best, loadouts, packer) until it buried the adventurer status at the bottom of a tall left column while the right side sat empty.** Jesse pointed at the PACK & EQUIP block: "should be on this right hand empty side instead." The fix is extraction, not reflow: `EmbarkPanel` becomes its own panel that owns the embark state (region/draft/loadouts, the regionPick pulse) and renders the whole plan-a-dive surface, leaving `ExpeditionPanel` to show only adventurer status + Bestiary. Lessons: (1) when a single panel accretes sub-features over many bricks, the right refactor is to split along the seam the user already perceives ("planning a dive" vs "your adventurer") — the layout complaint names the seam for you. (2) An extraction is a *pure move* only if the moved code's inputs are unchanged — the `deriveStats(view.inventory, lvls, agent?.worn)` calls and every command went across byte-identical, so the e2e "embark fists-first" passing against the new panel is proof the behavior survived the move. (3) Extraction always leaves a tail to sweep: the donor panel keeps imports that are now dead (`useState`/`usePref`/`embarkPrep`/`RegionMap`) — typecheck won't flag unused imports under this config, so grep each moved symbol's usages and remove what no longer resolves, or the donor carries phantom dependencies. (4) Selectors that assert WHICH panel owns a control (`.expedition` vs `.embark`) are load-bearing during an extraction: the 5 tests that broke are the suite confirming the controls actually changed homes, not friction to route around. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 169.

## Phase 14j — Net Worth Counts Equipped Gear (engine fix) (2026-06-12)

199. **Building the UI for a mechanic surfaces the engine bug the mechanic shipped with — making equipping easy (14h) is what made "equipping makes you poorer" visible.** `netWorth` valued gp + escrow + inventory holdings but not `agent.worn`, so since 13e added equipment, equipping a piece moved it out of the counted holdings and DROPPED your net worth (and leaderboard score) by its liquidation value. The bug shipped with 13e and sat latent for bricks because almost no one equipped valuable gear — until 14h made it a one-tap habit, at which point "I equipped my dragon sword and my worth fell 500k" becomes a question someone asks. Lessons: (1) a new convenience feature is a stress test of the mechanic underneath it — friction was hiding a wrong incentive (equipping penalized your score), and removing the friction exposes it. (2) When you add a state bucket (worn), grep every CONSUMER of the old buckets (inventory) — `checkInvariants` and the UI worth helpers were updated for worn in 13e, but `netWorth` (a third consumer, in report.ts) was missed; the bug is always in the consumer you didn't think of. (3) The fix is correct BECAUSE it mirrors an existing counted bucket: worn gear liquidates exactly like inventory (unequip→sell), so counting it the same way is consistency, not invention — and the benchmark never equips, so the golden sim hash is byte-identical, proving the change touches only the equip path. Replay-affecting (leaderboard worth) → engine.js rebuilt, joins the redeploy batch. Brick 166.

## Phase 14h — Adventure-Tab Equipment Manager (user request) (2026-06-12)

198. **The decision-support helpers built brick-by-brick became the spec for a feature the user asked for whole — `gearDelta` (13h) already computed "is this an improvement over what's equipped," so Jesse's request was 90% assembly, not invention.** The ask was "equipment manager on the Adventure tab showing each item's stats, requirements, and whether it's an upgrade." Every piece existed: `GEAR[id]` for stats/req, `gearDelta` for the upgrade comparison, the equip/unequip/equipBest commands (13e/13f), the worn state (13e). The brick was composing them into a richer view at the surface the player prefers for gear (the combat tab), not building new logic. Lessons: (1) incremental decision-aid helpers compound into feature-readiness — because gearDelta was a pure, reusable function (not inline in one panel), the user's request dropped onto a second surface with no new computation, which is the payoff of the "pure helper first" discipline running through the whole session. (2) "Also on tab X" is a legitimate, non-duplicative request when the framing differs: the Exchange Ledger is sell-focused inventory, the Adventure GearManager is equip-focused with stats/reqs spelled out — same data, different job per surface (the #193 lesson, now user-validated). (3) A present user's concrete request outranks the autonomous idea queue — pivoting immediately to it, finishing the in-flight brick first, is the right interrupt discipline. UI-only (the commands and the comparison already existed), no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 164.

## Phase 14g — "Flippable" Market Track (2026-06-12)

197. **Sort surfaces, filter declutters — they're different verbs on the same data, and a power tool wants both.** 14d's margin column lets you SORT the market by flippability (the positive ones float up); 14g's "flippable" track HIDES everything that can't clear the tax. Sorting answers "what's the best?"; filtering answers "show me only the actionable set so I can scan/sort IT by something else (volume, price)." Lessons: (1) a sortable column and a category filter compose — once margin is a column AND a track, the player can filter to flippable THEN sort that subset by volume to find liquid flips, a query neither feature alone expresses. (2) Generalizing `inTrack(id)` to `inTrack(m)` (the whole row) was the small enabling refactor — a filter predicate should receive enough context to express dynamic conditions (live margin), not just a static key; widening the input unlocked a category the id-only version couldn't compute. (3) This is the third reuse of the track-filter pattern (13j gear, 14g flippable) and the second of `flipMargin` (14d column, 14g filter) — established patterns make new lenses cheap, which is exactly when to add them. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 163.

## Phase 14f — Daily-Record Swap Guard (finish the class) (2026-06-12)

196. **Fix the CLASS, not the instance — 14e found one mount-lazy ref that false-fired on a save swap, and the right follow-through is auditing every sibling, not waiting for each to be reported.** The daily-record celebration had the same shape as the level-up: a baseline captured at mount, diffed forever after. It already reset on RESTART (someone hit this class before, partially) but NOT on cloud-save adoption — so the bug was half-fixed in the codebase, which is the most dangerous state: it looks handled. Verifying the sibling and closing the gap (mirror the restart reset at the cloud path) finishes the class. Lessons: (1) when you fix a bug, grep for its SHAPE (here: "ref set once at mount, never reset on a game swap") and fix every match in the same pass — a class half-fixed reads as fully fixed and lulls the next reader. (2) A partial pre-existing fix (restart reset, no cloud reset) is evidence the class is real AND that swap sites are easy to miss one-at-a-time — centralizing "what to reset on a game swap" would prevent the next omission, noted as the durable follow-up. (3) Honest test-coverage accounting: the cloud path can't be unit-tested in isolation (Supabase-gated), so the confidence is "2-line mirror of already-tested restart code + full-suite no-regression," stated plainly rather than implied — a fix you can't directly test should say so. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 162.

## Phase 14e — Level-Up Swap Guard (self-found) (2026-06-12)

195. **A "celebrate a change" effect has a hidden second trigger — the change you mean (a level-up) and the change you don't (the whole world being replaced) look identical to a diff-of-snapshots, and only IDENTITY tells them apart.** 13v/13w celebrate when levels rise between two snapshots, but a mount-lazy baseline ref doesn't reset on a game swap (cloud-save adoption / restart), so adopting a higher-level save would diff old-game-levels against new-game-levels and fire a false "Attack up!" on sign-in. The fix keys the baseline to the game/agent OBJECT: the engine mutates the agent IN PLACE on a real level-up (reference stable), but a swap hands you a new object (reference changes) — so `prevAgent.current !== agent` cleanly means "different save, re-baseline silently." Lessons: (1) any effect that fires on a delta must distinguish "the value changed" from "the SOURCE of the value changed" — identity (object reference, or an id) is the discriminator, and forgetting it makes every load/restart a phantom event. (2) The fix EXPOSED a lying test: the 13w flash test simulated leveling by passing a NEW agent object, which the real engine never does (it mutates in place) — the test passed for the wrong reason, and correcting it to mutate-in-place made it match reality AND made the swap case a distinct, testable scenario. A test that models the system wrong can hide the exact bug the fix addresses. (3) Self-review of the session's effects found this without an agent — pointing the same adversarial lens at one's own recent stateful code is the cheapest place to catch the diff-detection edges that #162/#186/#187 keep surfacing. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 161.

## Phase 14d — Market Flip-Margin Column (2026-06-12)

194. **A ranked top-N and a sortable column answer different questions — TopFlips says "here are the 4 best flips," the margin column says "let ME rank the whole market however I want," and a power user needs the second.** The cockpit had TopFlips (the best few flips) but the 128-row market table couldn't show or sort by per-row margin, so any flip outside the top 4 — an affordable one, one in a watched category, one you have a hunch about — was invisible as a margin. Adding margin as a sortable column hands the ranking control to the player. Lessons: (1) a curated shortlist and a sortable full view are complements, not substitutes — the shortlist is fast for the common case, the column is powerful for the exploratory one; shipping only the shortlist quietly caps the ceiling for engaged players. (2) Reuse the shortlist's exact math (`flipMargin` = `rankFlips`'s per-unit sum) so the column and the strip can never disagree about a flip's margin — one formula, two presentations (the recurring single-source discipline, #185). (3) Adding a 7th column to a dense table is a real estate decision with a mobile cost — gating the change on the phone-viewport e2e (which passed) is how you add density without trusting that it's fine. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 160.

## Phase 14c — Bestiary Combat Stats (2026-06-12)

193. **The same fact wants to live at every surface the player consults it — a foe's stats belong in the fight (13z), at embark (14b), AND in the reference you browse to plan (14c); a "know your enemy" system is only complete when all three agree.** The Bestiary was a kill-log (names, gp, drops, counts) but not a stat reference, so the one place built FOR browsing-to-plan couldn't answer "can I take the Abyss demon?" — the very question 13z/14a/14b answer at the moment of action. Adding the same atk/def/hp (+ leech) closes the loop: the player can now scout the threat in a calm reference before ever committing. Lessons: (1) classify each surface by WHEN it's consulted — in-the-moment (combat), at-the-gate (embark), or at-leisure (bestiary) — and make sure decision-critical data reaches all three; the leisure surface is where planning happens and is easy to leave as flavour. (2) Keep the reference NEUTRAL (no danger colouring vs current stats) where the in-the-moment view is contextual — same data, different framing per surface: the bestiary says what the monster IS, the combat line says what it is RELATIVE to you right now. (3) This caps a four-brick threat-surfacing arc (13z foe stats → 14a in-fight leech → 14b pre-embark leech → 14c bestiary stats) — a single conceptual gap ("the engine knows the threat, the player doesn't") worked across every surface it touched. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 159.

## Phase 14b — Pre-Embark Leech Warning (2026-06-12)

192. **A threat is most actionable at the EARLIEST point it's knowable — 14a told you mid-fight, 14b tells you before you commit, and the second is where the player can still change the outcome.** Leech drains loot per round; learning that during the fight means you've already bled some, but learning it at embark means you can bring more damage, plan to extract early, or skip the region entirely. Surfacing the same fact at both decision points (pre-embark danger read + in-fight foe line) covers the full decision timeline. Lessons: (1) when a cost is known from static data (a region's monster table), surface it at the FIRST decision the player makes about that data (embark), not just the last (the fight) — earlier warnings preserve more of the player's options. (2) Extending the existing derivation (`regionDanger` already scanned the region's foes for worst atk/def; adding the worst leech is one more reduction in the same loop) is cheaper and more consistent than a parallel computation — the danger read becomes the single place "how bad is this region?" is answered, now including the gp-drain axis. (3) Adding a field to a function with a strict `toEqual` test is a deliberate, caught break — the test failing is the type system and the suite agreeing the contract changed, which is the safety working, not friction. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 158.

## Phase 14a — Leech Gp-Race Warning (2026-06-12)

191. **A mechanic the engine enforces but the UI never names is a trap the player springs on themselves — the Abyss has drained loot per round since it shipped, visible only in a code comment.** `abyssal_leech`/`abyssal_demon`/`vessith` burn 40/80/200 loot gp every round a fight drags; that turns a winnable fight into a coin-bleed if you stall, and the optimal play (finish fast or extract) is the opposite of the usual "grind it down safely." The rule was fully implemented and fully invisible — the only mention was a comment in the forecast IIFE explaining why leech is irrelevant to the SURVIVAL race, which is exactly the framing that hid it from the player who cares about the GP race. Lessons: (1) every engine rule that changes optimal play deserves a UI tell at the decision point — a hidden cost doesn't just go unnoticed, it actively misleads, because the player applies their default strategy to a situation the rule has inverted. (2) The same surface (the combat foe line) now stacks three threat dimensions — hp (always), atk/def danger (13z), gp-drain (14a) — each a conditional marker; layering threat info on one line beats scattering it. (3) This is the #190 lesson one step further: 13z surfaced the foe's STATS, 14a surfaces the foe's special RULE — completeness of "know your enemy" means both what it is and what it does that's unusual. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 157.

## Phase 13z — Know-Your-Enemy Combat Stats (2026-06-12)

190. **Consistency is a feature with a long tail — the embark screen coloured foe stats red/green for bricks, but the place you actually FIGHT never did, and the eye notices the holdout.** The combat view had a rounds-forecast (the verdict) and the foe's HP, but not its atk/def — so the player saw "flee?" without the visible reason, and the in-fight read silently diverged from the embark danger read that trained their eye. Adding the same red/green atk-vs-your-def, def-vs-your-atk lens to the combat line closes the gap. Lessons: (1) when a visual lens (danger colouring) is established in one view, audit every sibling view that shows the same KIND of information — the forecast answered "should I flee?" but not "why?", and the why is what builds the player's intuition over time. (2) Hoist a value computed for one consumer when a second needs it (`youAtk`/`youDef` fed the forecast; the foe readout reuses them) rather than recomputing — one source, two readouts, no drift. (3) The most game-y surface (active combat) had the least decision instrumentation precisely because attention had flowed to the trading cockpit and the embark screen; breadth-first auditing finds these under-served cores. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 156.

## Phase 13y — "Rest to Full" One-Click (2026-06-12)

189. **A readout invites the action it names; building the action is the cheaper, more useful half — "≈30 ticks to heal" all but asks for a button that does exactly that.** 13x put a number on the rest cost; the manual way to act on it (tap +1k/+10k) overshoots and is fiddly, so the natural completion is a one-click that fast-forwards EXACTLY the ETA. The friction was architectural, not logical: resting is a UI time-advance (`runTicks`), not a player command, so it couldn't ride `onCommand` — it needed a fast-forward callback threaded App → ExpeditionPanel → CharacterPanel. Lessons: (1) when a panel needs to do something that ISN'T a domain mutation (advance time, copy a link), thread a purpose-named callback rather than forcing it through the command surface — keeping `applyCommand` for real mutations preserves the replay/authority boundary the project guards. (2) `exactOptionalPropertyTypes` bites when through-passing an optional callback (`onRest?: T` can't receive a `T | undefined` value) — type the receiving prop `T | undefined` so an optional can flow through layers; a small tax for the safety of not silently passing `undefined` where a value was required. (3) This closes the readout→action pattern that ran through the session (13i/13o/13q put info ON the control; 13x/13y put the action BESIDE the readout) — a decision aid is finished when the obvious next click is one click away. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 155.

## Phase 13x — Rest Heal-ETA (2026-06-12)

188. **A cost the engine imposes but the UI never names is a hidden tax on the player's decision — the wounds-mend-slowly rule existed in sim.ts for bricks, invisible until you put a number on it.** Wounds carry between dives and heal only out of the field at +1 hp per 3 ticks — a real constraint on how often you can dive — yet the UI showed only a static hp bar, so "rest first or embark hurt?" was decided blind. Naming the cost ("≈N ticks to heal") turns an invisible rule into a planned action. Lessons: (1) surface the ETA/cost of every engine rule the player must plan around — a mechanic the player can't SEE the cost of is one they'll mis-plan, and the fix is arithmetic the UI can do from constants it already has. (2) Gate the readout on the exact conditions the rule fires (`hp !== undefined` = wounded, `!expedition` = out of field) so it never lies — showing a heal ETA mid-dive (where regen is paused) would be worse than showing nothing. (3) Mirror the engine's formula exactly (`+1 / regenTicks`) rather than approximating, and source `REST_REGEN_TICKS` from the engine so the ETA tracks the rule if it's ever retuned — the same single-source discipline as the tax in #185. Completes the dive-decision triad: embark forecast (full hp) → mid-dive push read (13p, current hp) → rest ETA (13x, between dives). UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 154.

## Phase 13w — Skill-Cell Level-Up Flash (2026-06-12)

187. **Notify-and-confirm is two surfaces for one event — the toast says WHAT happened, the flash says WHERE, and a player believes the second more than the first.** 13v's toast announces "Attack up!"; 13w pops the Attack cell gold. Neither alone is as good as both: a toast is transient and can be missed or overridden (it's a single shared slot), while a flash on the actual skill is durable for its 1.2s and unambiguous about which stat moved. Placing the flash on the Adventure tab — where combat (and thus xp gain) happens — means it fires in the player's field of view, not on a panel they'd have to switch to. Lessons: (1) for an important event, pair an ephemeral global notification with a persistent local highlight on the thing that changed; the redundancy is the point, not waste. (2) Share the detection (`leveledUp`) between the toast and the flash so they can never disagree about what leveled — the same anti-divergence discipline as the gear tie-break (#182), applied to feedback rather than logic. (3) The lazy-init-baseline guard recurs a third time (#161, #186): any "celebrate a change" effect needs "first observation sets the baseline silently," or mounting the component re-fires every latent level. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 153.

## Phase 13v — Combat Level-Up Celebration (2026-06-12)

186. **The most basic feedback is the easiest to forget to build — the game celebrated deeds and daily records but never the thing an RPG is fundamentally about: leveling up.** Across 150+ bricks the progression loop had milestones, daily-record celebrations, and a live xp-to-next bar, yet the moment a skill actually ticked over fired nothing. It's the kind of gap that hides precisely because it's obvious: everyone assumes it's there. The fix reused the exact diff-detection-into-toast pattern already proven by death-capture and the daily-record (#161): a lazy-init ref of prior levels, checked in the same `refreshProgress` that runs after every command/tick/fast-forward. Lessons: (1) audit the CORE loop's feedback explicitly — the fancy celebrations (records, deeds) can all exist while the foundational one is missing, because attention flows to the novel, not the obvious. (2) Toast PRIORITY is a real design decision encoded by call ORDER on a single last-writer-wins slot: level-up fires before the milestone check so a deed earned the same tick outranks it — and the render test surfaced this by accident (a level-15 jump crossed a combat-level deed that ate the toast), which is exactly the override behaviour intended, so the test was tightened to a deed-free 1→2 jump rather than the priority "fixed". (3) Lazy null-init of the comparison ref is the recurring guard (#161, feed-glow): "first observation establishes the baseline, never celebrates it" is what stops a save-load from phantom-firing every latent level at once. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 152.

## Phase 13u — Recent Flips (per-round-trip journal) (2026-06-12)

185. **The book that aggregates can't un-aggregate — to show per-flip detail you re-run the FIFO and keep what the book throws away, rather than trying to recover it from the totals.** The lifetime tradeBook folds every sell into per-item `{profit, soldUnits}` aggregates (applyFillToBook), so the individual round-trips are gone the moment they're booked — you can't reconstruct "this specific Shark flip made +470" from the totals. The clean answer isn't new persisted state; it's a SECOND pass over the same fills with the same FIFO+tax arithmetic that keeps per-flip records instead of summing them. Lessons: (1) when a feature needs detail an existing aggregator discards, re-derive from the raw events with a parallel computation rather than enriching the aggregator (which would bloat the hot path and the saved state for a display concern) — the fills window is right there. (2) Parallel FIFO must match the book's tax/rounding EXACTLY (`price − floor(price·tax)` per unit) or the per-flip profits won't reconcile with the per-item realized totals — reusing the same formula, not re-deriving it, is what keeps "flips" and "Profit by Item" telling the same story. (3) A new MODE on an existing component (TradeFeed tape/mine/flips) beats a new panel when it shares the data source — tape=all trades, mine=raw fills, flips=matched round-trips are three lenses on one window, not three panels competing for screen. Honest scope note logged: the window is capped at 50, so "recent" is literal; full history would need a persisted log (engine/save change), which the rolling window deliberately avoids. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 151.

## Phase 13t — GE-Capped Flip Marker (review-driven) (2026-06-12)

184. **A SOUND review still pays — its one latent note became a real feature, because "this flag is loose but never rendered" is an invitation to render it correctly.** An adversarial pass over the session's four money helpers found no bugs (good — the math was right), but flagged `flipAffordability.limited` as semantically loose: it could read `true` for a zero buy limit (units 0), saved only by the caller never surfacing it outside the affordable branch. The lazy response is "latent, ignore it." The better one: tighten the flag (`units >= 1`) so it's correct unconditionally, THEN surface it — a "GE" tag on flips you can afford but the buy limit caps, which is a genuine scaling signal (you can't do more this window even with more gp). Lessons: (1) a latent-but-wrong flag is debt that the next person who renders it will trip on; fixing it when you find it (not when it breaks) is cheap insurance, and pairing the fix with a use makes the correctness load-bearing instead of theoretical. (2) "no bugs found" is the GOAL of a review, not a failure of it — confidence that the money helpers are correct is itself the deliverable, and the small hardening is a bonus. (3) The distinction the marker draws (gp-bound ×N vs limit-bound ×N-GE vs unaffordable ✕) completes the affordability lens with the one piece of information a capital-rich player actually needs: "more gp won't help here." UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 150.

## Phase 13s — Upgrade Affordability Hints (2026-06-12)

183. **A disabled button is a dead end; a disabled button with a distance is a goal — "5,000 gp (greyed out)" says no, "need +4,900" says not yet, keep flipping.** The shop greyed out upgrades you couldn't afford, which communicates the constraint but kills the motivation: the player learns they can't buy, not how to get there. Adding the shortfall turns each locked upgrade into a concrete saving target, and the spendable-purse header lets them compare at a glance. Same affordability lens as 13l (flips) and 13n (positions), now on the progression spend. Lessons: (1) when you block an action, show the GAP to unblocking it — a constraint with a distance is a quest; a constraint without one is a wall. (2) Show the RIGHT number for the decision: the shop header shows spendable gp (cash), not net worth, because upgrades can't be bought with held goods — putting net worth here would mislead (you'd think you could afford something you can't), so it's not a WealthPanel duplicate but a different, decision-correct figure. (3) One `need()` helper across four buttons keeps the four affordability hints identical by construction — the anti-divergence discipline (13r) applied preemptively, not retroactively. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 149.

## Phase 13r — Gear Tie-Break Consistency (review-driven) (2026-06-12)

182. **The adversarial review earned its keep on the comment, not the code — a tie-break divergence that's harmless in stats was paired with a comment that CONFIDENTLY lied about it, and the lie is the part that bites later.** After 13 un-reviewed feature bricks I pointed an adversarial review at the three independent "best gear per slot" pickers. It found exactly one real flaw: `deriveStats`, `equipBest`, and `equipped()` break ties by three different iteration orders, and the GEAR table has stat-identical ties — so the equip-best result can name a different itemId than the paperdoll preview. Blast radius is near-zero (tied pieces are stat-identical → combat, worth, and the golden sim hash are all invariant), but `equipBest`'s comment asserted "identical tie-break to deriveStats," which is false and would mislead the next person who trusts it. Lessons: (1) review yields most where there are MULTIPLE implementations of one concept — three pickers is three chances to diverge, and "they obviously agree" is exactly the assumption worth attacking. (2) A confident-but-wrong comment is a worse defect than the subtle behavioural divergence it describes, because the code can be re-derived but the comment is believed; fixing the lie mattered more than the itemId. (3) The fix made the rule ORDER-INDEPENDENT (explicit smaller-itemId tie-break) rather than "make everyone iterate the same collection" — encoding the invariant in the comparator beats encoding it in a fragile convention every call site must remember. (4) Left `deriveStats` untouched on purpose: its tie-break is unobservable (returns stats, ties are stat-identical), so changing ground-truth combat code for zero behavioural gain would be churn — the discipline is to fix what's observable, not everything the review touched. The review confirmed the other four claims (conservation, determinism, fallback, invariant counting) SOUND. Replay-affecting (equipBest item choice) → engine.js rebuilt, joins the redeploy batch; sim byte-identical. Brick 148.

## Phase 13q — Below-Break-Even Sell Tint (2026-06-12)

181. **A warning's effectiveness is set by its DISTANCE from the action it guards — the break-even readout existed below the field for bricks, but moving the same signal onto the input is what makes it catch the mistake.** The ticket already showed "break-even ≥ X · below break-even"; the data was right there. But it sat under the price field, so you'd type a loss-making price and only notice if you scanned down. Tinting the input itself puts the warning where the decision is entered — zero scan distance. Lessons: (1) co-locate the guard with the gesture: a validation rendered next to the control fires later (or never) than the same validation rendered ON the control; for loss-prevention, on-control wins. (2) This is the same "put it on the action" thread as 13o (payoff on the extract button) and 13i (delta on the buy ticket) — three bricks, one principle: information attached to the control beats information attached to a nearby readout. (3) Reusing the existing pure helper (`breakEvenSell`) meant the input tint and the readout can never disagree about what "below break-even" means — one source, two surfaces. Honest framing: it's advisory (the engine stays the rejection authority), tinting only what the math can prove is a post-tax loss vs your average cost. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 147.

## Phase 13p — Mid-Dive Push Read (2026-06-12)

180. **A forecast computed once at the start goes stale exactly when the stakes peak — the embark read was at full hp, but the dangerous decision is always made wounded, deep, with loot on the line.** The embark screen forecasts the region at full hp; that's the safe moment. The decision that actually matters — push deeper or bank — happens mid-dive after you've taken damage, and there the UI offered only a blind "venture deeper" button. The same `combatForecast` re-run at CURRENT hp turns a one-time pre-flight check into a live risk read that sharpens precisely as it gets dangerous. Lessons: (1) a decision aid must be evaluated at the moment of the decision, not the moment of setup — a static forecast is a different (weaker) feature than a live one, even with identical math, because hp moved. (2) Two halves of one decision belong together: 13o put the LOOT stake on the extract button, 13p puts the RISK read beside it, and the "— bank your haul?" nudge only fires when both conditions meet (risky AND loot held) — composing the two readouts into a single recommendation neither could give alone. (3) Reusing the engine-mirroring forecast (with the same dragonfire bonus the live combat read uses) keeps the embark, mid-dive, and in-combat reads consistent — one formula, three moments, no drift. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 146.

## Phase 13o — Extract Payoff on the Action (2026-06-12)

179. **Put the consequence on the verb — a button labelled with its payoff ("bank 5,000 gp") drives a decision that the same number sitting in a stats line never will.** The dive already showed loot as a neutral stat and offered a generic "extract (keep everything)" button. Both facts were present; the DECISION (lock in the haul vs push and risk it) wasn't framed where it's made. Moving the at-risk amount onto the action — "extract · bank N gp", green — turns a label into a prompt, the same move as 13i (gear delta on the buy ticket) and 13h↔13i (the same datum belongs at every point the decision is taken). Lessons: (1) status and action are different surfaces: a number in a readout answers "what is true", a number on a button answers "what happens if I press this" — push-your-luck games live or die on the second. (2) Showing the same value in two framings isn't the duplication the #169/#170/#173 discipline warns against — that rule is about COMPETING displays that can disagree; a value can't contradict itself, so status-vs-action framing is complementary, not redundant. (3) Rotating to the least-touched surface (the active dive) found a high-leverage one-line change precisely because it had had no attention — breadth-first rotation surfaces cheap wins that depth-first mining misses. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 145.

## Phase 13n — Underwater Positions Glance (2026-06-12)

178. **"Coloured red" is not "surfaced" — the loss was already visible per-row and still invisible in practice, because best-first sorting buried it below the fold.** PositionsPanel had tinted each position's paper P&L green/red since 12c, so one might call underwater-visibility "done." But the list sorts best-first and caps at 8, so the bleeding positions are exactly the ones that scroll off — the data was present and the signal was absent. The fix isn't more colour on the row, it's an AGGREGATE that doesn't depend on scroll position ("⚠ N underwater −X paper") plus a row tint strong enough to spot when a loser does appear. Lessons: (1) per-item styling and at-a-glance risk are different jobs — a property rendered on every row still fails if the rows you care about are the ones sorted away; surface the summary separately from the list. (2) Respect the data's honesty: only MARKED positions (those with a live price) count as underwater — an unmarked position has unknown P&L, not a loss, and conflating them would cry wolf. (3) This completed the trading RISK lens (holdings) as the counterpart to 13k/13l's ACQUISITION lens (what to buy) — the same system seen from "how am I doing?" vs "what do I do next?", and a cockpit needs both. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 144.

## Phase 13m — One-Tap "Prep Me" (2026-06-12)

177. **A "do everything" button is a dedupe problem wearing an automation costume — the work isn't applying the fixes, it's knowing that two warnings can share one fix.** 12q gave each embark warning its own "+pack" chip; the obvious "prep me" is just a loop over those chips. But the antifire and food warnings can resolve to the SAME item (super-antifire heals AND blocks breath), so a naive loop would pack two when one suffices, and worse, the button would offer itself for a single underlying fix that already has a chip. Deduping the fix items (`new Set`) and gating on `≥2 distinct` is what makes the button honest: it appears only when it genuinely saves taps, and packs exactly what's needed. Lessons: (1) a batch action over N items isn't N× the single action — the interesting logic is the cross-item relationships (shared fixes, conflicts, ordering) that don't exist at N=1. (2) Reuse the per-item resolver (`fixFor`) rather than re-deriving the fixes — the batch and the singles can never disagree about what clears a warning, the same discipline as #170's "reuse the consumer's own selection logic." (3) Pivoting systems for breadth paid the methodology dividend NEXT_STEPS predicted: after eight trading/equipment bricks, a 20-minute RPG-side brick closed a queued follow-up and kept the surface evenly developed. The test also pinned an emergent fact (dragons_maw reads "risky" for a fresh fighter, so the food warning fires) — a render test doubling as a forecast-calibration guard. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 143.

## Phase 13l — Flip Affordability Lens (2026-06-12)

176. **The honest version of a previously-DECLINED feature is often a different, shippable feature — "realizable profit" was rightly refused (needs order-flow depth the UI can't see), but "affordability" is the same intuition stripped to what the data CAN prove.** NEXT_STEPS recorded a deliberate decline: don't rank flips by realizable profit, because you can't know how much rests at the bid/ask. The temptation is to treat the whole area as off-limits. But the player's real question — "which of these can I actually act on right now?" — splits into a knowable part (gp/buy, capped by the GE buy limit = how many you can OPEN) and an unknowable part (how many will FILL = depth). `flipAffordability` answers only the knowable part, and the framing is everything: a `×N` badge labelled "units your gp can buy now", never "profit you'll make". Lessons: (1) when a feature is declined for an epistemic reason (the data can't support the claim), look for the adjacent claim the data DOES support — often 80% of the value survives once you drop the part you can't honestly assert. (2) Make the scope visible in the words: "fits purse" and "×N units" promise capital-coverage, not fills, so the UI never over-claims. (3) A filter that re-ranks deeper before slicing (limit 100 → filter affordable → take 4) surfaces the best AFFORDABLE option without abandoning the margin order — the constraint (your gp) is applied as a filter on an honest ranking, not baked into a fabricated score. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 142.

## Phase 13k — Return on Stake (2026-06-12)

175. **A cockpit can be richly instrumented and still miss the simplest question — six analytics panels measured spreads and positions, but none answered "am I up?"; the most-glanced number was the one not shown.** Branching off the equipment arc to the trading side, an Explore map found TopFlips/Movers/Depth/ProfitPanel/PositionsPanel all present — sophisticated decision aids — yet no single readout for net worth vs where you began. The Fortune chart plots worth over time but not the delta against the stake; realized/unrealized P&L is per-item, never a headline. The fix was one pure helper over a value (`game.startGp`) that already existed for exactly this purpose (it gates the double-your-stake deed) but was never surfaced as a number. Lessons: (1) audit for the OBVIOUS missing glance, not just sophisticated gaps — the more advanced the instrumentation, the easier it is to skip the trivial top-line everyone actually checks first. (2) When a baseline value already exists in the model (startGp), prefer surfacing it to inventing a new one — but VERIFY its real semantics first: I almost shipped this as "session P&L" until the grep showed startGp is HUMAN_START_GP preserved across reloads, i.e. lifetime, not session; labelling it honestly (lifetime vs stake) avoided a number that lies about its own scope. (3) Integrate into the panel that already owns the concept (WealthPanel) rather than adding a competing card — the #169/#170 anti-duplication discipline applies to additive features too, not just bug fixes. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 141.

## Phase 13j — Gear Findability on the Market (2026-06-12)

174. **A buy-decision aid is worthless if the player can't reach the buy — six bricks made gear safe, equippable, previewable, and priced, but gear was still un-findable among 128 commodities, so the whole arc hinged on a marker I hadn't built.** 13d–13i is a complete buy/equip/compare pipeline, yet every step assumed the player had already SELECTED a gear item. Acquisition is on the ordinary order book, and the market table's only categorisation (all/staples/exotics) splits by volatility — useless for finding the ~19 equippable items. So the arc had a hole at its very entrance: you can preview "+35 Attack before you buy" (13i) only once you've found the gear, and nothing helped you find it. The fix is small (a `gear` track + a per-row ⚔/🛡 marker) but it's the keystone — without it the upstream work is reachable only by knowing item names in advance. Lessons: (1) trace a feature arc from the user's FIRST action, not the one you happened to build last — the entrance (find the gear) is as load-bearing as the exit (equip it), and it's easy to polish the exit while the entrance stays locked. (2) Markers earn double duty: the ⚔/🛡 glyph both identifies gear and, via its title, answers "what level does it need?" — one affordance, two questions. (3) Reuse the discriminator the domain already has (`GEAR[id]`) rather than inventing a category flag on items — the engine's gear map IS the gear taxonomy, so the UI filter and the equip logic can never disagree about what "gear" means. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 140.

## Phase 13i — Pre-Buy Gear Delta (2026-06-12)

173. **A decision readout belongs wherever the decision is MADE, and the same decision is often made in two places — the satchel (13h) answers "should I equip this?", the trade ticket answers "should I buy this?", and they want the same number in both.** 13h surfaced the upgrade delta on owned gear, but an Explore pass confirmed gear is acquired on the ordinary order book (gear pieces are just tradeable catalog items — no special shop), so the BUY decision lives at the trade ticket, one step earlier than equipping. Putting the same `gearDelta` there means the player sees "+35 Attack over your Adamant dart" before committing gp, not only after the piece is sitting unequipped in the satchel. The reuse was nearly free because the helper was already pure and decoupled — the only friction was data: PlayerView deliberately omits combat levels, so the levels had to be threaded from App as a prop (made optional so isolated render tests don't need to fake an agent; absent levels just drops the "needs Atk N" note, never the delta). Lessons: (1) when you add a decision aid, ask "where ELSE is this same decision made?" — the buy and the equip are the same upgrade judgment at two moments, and covering only one leaves the gap where the gp is actually spent. (2) A pure helper with a small, explicit signature (id, worn, lvls) pays off precisely here — it dropped into a second component with no refactor, which a helper that read component state or context could not have. (3) Keep an optional prop's ABSENCE graceful (skip the level-gated note, keep the core readout) so the component stays testable in isolation without constructing the whole world. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 139.

## Phase 13h — Gear Upgrade Delta (2026-06-12)

172. **Close the loop back to the user's ORIGINAL words — five bricks of equipment plumbing (safe/equip/auto-equip/coherent display) all served a complaint that began "I'm trying to buy upgrade gear," and the buy DECISION was still the one thing left blind.** 13d–13g made gear safe from the clerk, equippable, one-click auto-equippable, and made the character sheet tell the truth. But the satchel still showed only an "equip" chip — nothing told you whether a piece you just bought actually BEATS what you wear. The decision the user actually described (is this an upgrade worth buying/equipping?) had no readout. `gearDelta` adds it: the governing-stat change vs the worn piece (weapon→Attack, armor→Defence), green for upgrade, red for downgrade, 🔒 when under-level, "no gain" for a sidegrade. Lessons: (1) when a feature arc grows long, re-read the user's FIRST sentence — the plumbing is necessary but the payoff is the specific decision they named, and it's easy to build everything around it without ever building it. (2) A read-only `WorldState` access is the right tool when the view projection lacks what display needs (combat levels aren't in `PlayerView`) — the UI rule forbids MUTATING outside applyCommand, not reading for display, so threading levels from `game` beats bloating the engine's view contract for a cosmetic. (3) Reuse the codebase's established colour semantics (`.up`=green/good, `.down`=red/bad, already set by the danger readout) so a new badge reads correctly on first sight without inventing a palette. UI-only (pure `gearDelta` helper + a badge), no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 138.

## Phase 13g — Paperdoll ↔ Worn Coherence (2026-06-12)

171. **Adding a new source of truth (persistent `worn`) silently invalidates every OLD display that derived the same concept a different way — the bug isn't in the new feature, it's in the stale readers you forgot were computing the answer themselves.** 13e/13f gave the player real persistent equipment (`worn`), and both new surfaces (PlayerPanel's "Equipped" list, the combat math) read it. But the Adventure-tab character sheet predated persistent equipment and still computed its paperdoll from `equipped(inventory, lvls)` — "best gear you could pack" — a PREVIEW that made sense only when packing was the only way to wear gear. Once equipping MOVES gear out of inventory into `worn`, that preview reads the wrong bucket: equip your best weapon and the figure shows the weapon slot DOWNGRADED (next-best still in the satchel) or empty, the instant you equipped your best. Same root for the "in battle: ⚔X 🛡Y" line, which called `deriveStats` without the `worn` arg and so ignored everything equipped. The Explore-agent map made the duplication explicit: two "equipment" displays in two tabs, derived two ways, free to disagree. Three durable lessons: (1) when you introduce a canonical store for a concept, GREP for every place that re-derives that concept and point them at the store — a derived preview is correct only until a real source exists, then it's a liar. (2) The fix needs no new plumbing when the component already holds the source — CharacterPanel already received the whole `agent`, so `agent.worn` was in hand; the work was realizing the read was stale, not wiring data. (3) Truthful beats flattering: if the player equipped a WORSE piece than they hold, the figure now shows the worse piece, because that's what fights — and `equipBest`/equip one-click the fix; a display that flatters ("you'd wield the dragon") while combat uses the adamant dart is worse than an honest downgrade. Mirrored `deriveStats`'s exact override rule so the figure, the effective stats, and the combat all agree by construction. UI-only (CharacterPanel reads `view`/`agent` already), no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 137.

## Phase 13f — Auto-Equip Best (2026-06-12)

170. **A one-click "do the optimal thing" action belongs in the engine, not the UI, the moment the optimum depends on state the UI can only approximate — `equipBest` had to know combat levels, so a UI loop would silently equip inert gear or skip real upgrades.** The follow-up looked like pure UI sugar: a button that fires N `equip` commands. But "best usable per slot" is gated by the player's Attack/Defence levels (a req-14 weapon is inert under level 14), and the canonical level→gear mapping lives in `deriveStats`/`levelsOf` in the engine. A UI-side loop would have had to RE-DERIVE the level gate from the view — duplicating a rule that already exists, and drifting from it the day the xp curve or a `req` moves. Putting `equipBest` in the engine let it reuse the EXACT same best-per-slot scan and first-seen tie-break that `deriveStats` uses, so what the button equips is provably what the combat math will fight with — no second source of truth. Three lessons: (1) the test for "UI convenience vs engine command" isn't how visible the action is, it's whether computing the action's RESULT requires a rule the engine owns — if it does, the convenience is an engine command with a thin button, not UI logic. (2) Reusing the consumer's own selection logic (same scan, same tie-break) is what makes a bulk action consistent with the per-item path — had I written a fresh "best" heuristic I'd have risked equipBest picking a different piece than deriveStats rewards. (3) The additive-by-override discipline from #169 paid off again for free: because the benchmark never issues `equipBest`, `worn` stays empty for it and all three sanity hashes are byte-identical — a new command + handler + UI button cost zero determinism risk because its identity case (never called) IS the existing behaviour. Replay-affecting (new command) → engine.js rebuilt, redeploy batches with 12b/13d/13e. Jesse-greenlit follow-up to the 13e equipment manager. Brick 136.

## Phase 13e — Equipment Manager (2026-06-12)

169. **An additive feature that LAYERS over a deterministic core can be added to a finished system at near-zero risk — `worn` overrides the pack, and "no equipment" is byte-for-byte the old behaviour.** The user wanted a real OSRS equip system on top of a combat/economy engine with golden-hash gates. The temptation was to REPLACE the pack-based gear model with persistent equipment; that would have touched `deriveStats`, every expedition test, death handling, and the loadout UI — a migration with real regression surface. Instead I made `worn` a per-slot OVERRIDE inside `deriveStats(pack, lvls, worn?)`: when `worn` is absent or empty, the function returns exactly what it did before, so every existing test and — critically — the benchmark are untouched. The proof is the sim hash being **byte-identical across all three sanity seeds** after a feature that added two commands, an agent field, a conservation rule, and a UI panel: because the benchmark never equips, `worn` stays empty for it, and "empty worn" is the identity case. Three lessons: (1) the cheapest way to extend a frozen system is to find the function where the new behaviour is a STRICT SUPERSET of the old, gated by a value that defaults to the old behaviour — additive-by-override beats replace-and-migrate. (2) An unchanged golden hash after a feature is the strongest possible proof of non-interference; design the feature so its identity case IS the existing behaviour and the hash will prove it for free. (3) The same `basis`/ownership thread from 13d recurs: equipped gear is "safe on death" because it lives in `worn`, OUTSIDE the at-risk pack — where state lives decides what happens to it, so put player-protected things in a player-protected bucket. Replay-affecting (new commands + deriveStats) → engine.js rebuilt, redeploy batches with 12b/13d. Jesse-present, user-requested. Brick 135.

## Phase 13d — Clerk Gear-Safety (User Bug Fix) (2026-06-12)

168. **The fix that preserves the invariant is the one that uses a distinction the system ALREADY draws — `basis` separated clerk-stock from player-goods without inventing any new state.** A user reported the autoFlip clerk selling gear they bought to equip (and the satchel "always empty"). The blunt fix — blocklist GEAR from the clerk — immediately failed the balance gate, because gear-flipping was real clerk profit (tier-3 seed-99 went −4436); the game's own economy test rejected the naive fix, which is exactly what a sanity gate is FOR. The clean fix came from noticing the clerk already records a cost `basis` for everything it buys: a held item with NO basis was bought by the human, not the clerk. Gating both sell and buy on basis-presence protects the player's goods AND is a provable no-op for the benchmark (which only ever holds its own buys) — confirmed by the sim hash being **byte-identical** (`fe75df57`) after the change. Three durable lessons: (1) when shared state has two owners and no ownership tag, look for an existing field that CORRELATES with ownership (here, basis = "I bought this") before adding a tag — it's cheaper and can't drift from reality. (2) A behaviour-change fix that leaves your golden hash unchanged is the strongest possible evidence it's surgical — if the benchmark's deterministic trace is identical, you've changed only the path you meant to. (3) The bug was the durable face (#162/#163 lineage) of the SAME slot-sharing class — the clerk, like the sellsword, shares state with the player, and "who owns this?" is the recurring question; answer it with data the system already has. This was a Jesse-present, user-reported engine fix — replay-affecting, so engine.js is rebuilt and the verify-score redeploy is flagged to batch with 12b. Brick 134.

## Phase 13c — Named Fill Toast (2026-06-12)

167. **The common case deserves the richer treatment; "name it when you can, count it when you can't" beats always-aggregate.** 13b's fill toast aggregated ("bought N · sold M") to handle the messy case of many items filling at once. But the MODAL case is one resting order clearing — all one item, one side — where naming it ("bought 50 Shark") is both trivially detectable and far more useful. `fillToastFlavor` detects the homogeneous burst and names it, falling back to the aggregate only when the fills are genuinely mixed. The lesson: an aggregate built to survive the worst case often under-serves the typical case — branch on homogeneity and you get specificity for free where it's available, generality where it's needed. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 133.

## Phase 13b — Resting-Fill Notification (2026-06-12)

166. **A notification's hard part isn't firing it — it's NOT firing it; the brick was 80% spam-suppression and 20% toast.** Surfacing "your resting order filled" is one `setToast`. Making it not a nuisance took three separate guards, each closing a way it would have flooded: gate to 1× (so fast-forward's hundreds of per-tick fills stay silent — they're summarized in the away-bar), skip the command path (instant fills are already narrated by the ticket, so notifying there is double-speak), and fire it FIRST in the refresh so the milestone/alert toasts that follow OVERRIDE it on a busy tick (a priority ordering via last-writer-wins on a single toast slot). The general lesson: any event-driven notification has an implicit frequency distribution, and the design work is matching the surfacing to the distribution's TAIL, not its typical case — the typical resting fill at 1× deserves a toast, the same event at 20× does not, and the same logical event arriving via a command deserves a different surface entirely. Reusing the existing `recordFills` latch (just returning its fresh fills instead of void) kept this from being a new diff-detection effect — important right after #162/#163 showed how those effects misbehave; the dedupe that already made "apply each fill once" hold doubles as the "notify each fill once" guarantee. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 132.

## Phase 13a — Help Overlay Keyboard Docs (2026-06-12)

165. **A feature and its documentation are one deliverable shipped in two files, and skipping the second leaves the first undiscoverable — the keyboard trade flow existed for five bricks before the help mentioned it.** I'd shipped j/k market nav (12g) and b/s side toggle (12t) with discoverability hints only at the point of use (a table footer, a button title), but the How-to-Play overlay — the one place a new player goes to learn what's possible — still listed only 1/2/3/p/?. The shortcuts worked perfectly and almost no one would ever find them. The lesson is a discipline one: when a feature is ONLY reachable via a non-obvious affordance (a hotkey, a long-press, a hidden gesture), its help entry isn't optional polish — it's part of the feature's definition of done, because without it the feature's adoption is ~zero. I caught it the same way as #156 and #162 — by reading the code I was about to extend (the help component) rather than only the code I was writing. Bonus: the help hardcoded the starting gp while sourcing regions/sprint-ticks from constants, a latent drift; switching it to `HUMAN_START_GP` makes the doc track the source. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 131.

## Phase 12z — Next-Deed Progress Bars (2026-06-12)

164. **Visual consistency is a feature you finish, not start — the deeds list was the last progression surface still showing progress as text while four siblings showed bars.** Across the build I'd grown a quiet convention: a thin inline progress bar for any "how close am I" quantity (bounty kills 11z, region conquest 12f, portfolio allocation 12k). The MilestonesPanel's next-deeds list predated that convention and still rendered "62%" as bare text — a small inconsistency that reads as "unfinished" once the eye is trained on the bars elsewhere. The brick is trivial (one `.deedbar` reusing the `.conquestbar` shape), but the lesson is about WHEN to spend a brick on it: a lone text-percentage among bars isn't worth fixing on day one, but once the bar pattern is the house style, the holdout becomes visual debt — the cost of inconsistency rises as the pattern spreads, so the right time to unify is after the pattern has proven itself, not before. A feature-complete game earns these consistency passes; they're cheap, they're the kind of polish players feel without naming, and they close the gap between "works" and "considered". UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 130.

## Phase 12y — Sellsword Dive Visibility (2026-06-12)

163. **A shared-slot bug usually has two faces — the data face and the render face — and fixing one surfaces the other.** 12x fixed the DATA side: the sellsword's dives (sharing `agent.expedition`) no longer write the player's Delve Log or fire false death toasts. But the same slot-sharing means the RENDER side was also wrong: while the sellsword hunts mid-dive, the expedition panel shows the full interactive dive view — combat scene, fight/extract buttons — for the sellsword's dive, so the player can mistake it for their own and even fight its rounds. The two are the same root cause (one slot, two owners) seen through two different consumers (the capture effect vs the render). The fix is the same `bySellsword` heuristic applied to a clarity banner. The durable lesson: when you fix a mis-attribution in one consumer of shared state, audit EVERY consumer of that state — the bug isn't "the Delve Log is wrong", it's "ownership is ambiguous", and ambiguity leaks into every reader. Honest scoping note recorded in the phase doc: the banner CLARIFIES but doesn't DISABLE the player controls during a sellsword dive — truly preventing interference wants an engine-side ownership flag (a Jesse-present change), so the UI-only banner is the right stopping point, not a half-measure pretending to be complete. Also folded the sellsword's lifetime haul onto its toggle (it was buried in the Hall) — control and consequence in one place. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 129.

## Phase 12x — Sellsword Expedition Mis-Attribution (2026-06-12)

162. **The review that found nothing (pure helpers, 12u) and the review that found a real bug (effects, 12x) differ by exactly where bugs live — shared mutable state and lifecycle, not arithmetic.** After 12u's pure-helper review came back SOUND, I pointed a second adversarial review at the session's EFFECTS and listeners, and it immediately surfaced a HIGH bug: the sellsword autopilot shares `agent.expedition` with the player (it calls `beginExpedition` inside `tickWorld`), so my 12i Delve-Log capture — and a pre-existing "You died" toast — fired for the SELLSWORD's dives, attributing its deaths to the player. Three lessons worth keeping. (1) **Aim reviews at the failure class, not the codebase uniformly**: pure functions over a clean domain are where review yields least; shared-slot state machines and effect lifecycles are where it yields most — so the second review was worth more than the first precisely because it targeted the riskier layer. (2) **A new feature can reanimate a latent bug in code it merely extends**: the toast mis-attribution existed before 12i, but was invisible (a transient toast); attaching a PERSISTENT Delve Log to the same effect made it a durable, visible artifact — extending shared code inherits its latent defects. (3) **Reference-verify the reviewer too**: the claim "sellsword shares the expedition slot" was checked against `commands.ts:406`/`sim.ts:166` before fixing (the Kronos rule cuts both ways). The fix stamps `bySellsword` ownership into the snapshot as the dive is observed and gates the player-facing narration on it — UI-only, since `agent.sellsword` is already readable. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 128.

## Phase 12w — New-Daily-Record Celebration (2026-06-12)

161. **To celebrate beating a record you must snapshot the record BEFORE the thing that updates it runs — a live-maxed "best" can't also be the mark you're measuring against.** The daily-best pref (12e) maxes itself every tick, so "did worth pass my best?" is unanswerable from the live pref: by the time worth is a new high, the pref already equals it (they move together). The celebration needed the record as it was at session START — captured once in a ref at first render, before this session's play moves it. The general pattern: when a feature reacts to a value CROSSING a threshold, and the threshold is itself derived from the same value, you have to freeze the threshold at a well-defined earlier moment, or the crossing never registers (the threshold chases the value). The same null-as-"no real record" trick from `dailyBestView` (#143) does double duty here: a null incoming best both means "nothing carried in" and filters the trivial "beat your own starting stake" non-event, so the celebration only fires for a genuine prior-session record. A latch ref makes it once-only. This was a deliberate JUICE brick — after a long run of readouts and decision-instruments, the feature-complete game wanted a *moment*, not another number; positive reinforcement on beating your own record is among the stickiest solo hooks. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 127.

## Phase 12v — Scroll Selected Market Row Into View (2026-06-12)

160. **A keyboard nav that can move the selection off-screen is only half-built — the cursor and the viewport have to move together.** 12g let j/k walk the market selection, but on a long/filtered list the selected row could scroll out of view and you'd lose your place. The fix is one effect (`scrollIntoView({block: 'nearest'})` keyed on `selected`), but two small judgments made it clean: `'nearest'` only scrolls when the row ISN'T already visible, so a normal click never yanks the viewport — the scroll is invisible until it's needed; and a `typeof row.scrollIntoView === 'function'` guard keeps jsdom (which has no layout engine) from throwing, the same defensive shape as #150's offsetParent lesson (don't assume browser-only DOM APIs exist in the test environment). The durable point: input affordances ship in pairs — a way to MOVE a selection implies a way to KEEP it visible, and forgetting the second half makes the first feel broken on exactly the large datasets where keyboard nav matters most. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 126.

## Phase 12u — Adversarial-Review Hardening (2026-06-12)

159. **After 20 fast feature bricks, the highest-value next move wasn't a 21st feature — it was stopping to let a skeptic attack the work, and the best possible outcome was "nothing's broken."** A calibrated adversarial reviewer hunted the session's ~25 pure helpers for divide-by-zero, NaN, non-terminating loops, and bad edges. Verdict: SOUND. The valuable part isn't that it found a critical bug (it didn't) — it's that the *absence* of bugs is now EVIDENCE rather than hope: the reviewer proved `expectedHit` can't return 0 (so the combat forecast can't divide by zero), that `breakEvenSell`'s tighten loops terminate for every taxRate including the impossible ≥1, and that every aggregate guards its empty case. The one MEDIUM note — `worthBreakdown`'s `Math.max(0, …)` clamp could *hide* an inconsistency — turned out non-firing: `view` and `playerWorth` share one engine snapshot and a buy order's `escrowGp == remaining*price` exactly, so the residual IS the (≥0) bid-walk holdings and the clamp never fires. I addressed it with a comment proving the no-op rather than a code change, because "fixing" an impossible case risked rendering a negative for it. Then I pinned the probed edges as regression tests, converting the review's one-time confidence into a permanent guard. Two durable lessons: (1) a review that ends in "sound" is not a wasted review — it upgrades correctness from assumed to demonstrated, and the demonstration can be frozen into tests; (2) when a reviewer flags a defensive guard as "hiding" something, check whether the something is *reachable* before removing the guard — a clamp over a provably-non-negative quantity is documentation owed, not a bug. No runtime change — the app is byte-identical, now with an adversarial bill of health. Brick 125.

## Phase 12t — Ticket Side Hotkeys (2026-06-12)

158. **The second use of a pattern is where it earns its keep — 12g's gated-window-listener shape dropped straight into the ticket for b/s, and the cost was near-zero because the hard parts were already solved.** Adding `b`/`s` side hotkeys to the trade ticket reused 12g's MarketTable recipe verbatim: an `active` prop gating a once-bound window listener, the same input-focus guard, the same `activeRef` to read current state without rebinding. The first time I wrote that (12g) I had to discover the two traps — all-rooms-stay-mounted (so a hidden component's listener mustn't fire) and stale closures (so funnel current state through a ref). The second time, those were settled facts, and the brick was mostly deciding WHICH keys and leaving Enter-to-submit out (it already submits when focused, and a global Enter would mint accidental orders — the guardrail-on-the-wide-gesture instinct from #156). The lesson is a quiet one about compounding: a well-factored interaction pattern isn't just reusable code, it's reusable *decisions* — the second implementation skips the design spelunking the first paid for. Worth noticing when a brick feels suspiciously easy: often it's because an earlier brick did the thinking. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 124.

## Phase 12s — Raid Risk/Reward Totals (2026-06-12)

157. **The honest aggregate is the one summed from data you already trust, not the one reconstructed from a model — raid P&L came straight from the Delve Log's own loot figures, sidestepping the cross-loop attribution trap.** The adventure loop had per-raid history (12i) but no bottom line, while trading had a realized total — an asymmetry worth closing. The tempting version computes "earnings by loop" by splitting the net-worth delta into trading vs raiding, but that's fragile: worth-delta includes unrealized holdings swings and market moves, and raid item-drops get SOLD (so they'd double-count into trading realized). Instead `raidTotals` sums the Delve Log's OWN `lootGp` by outcome — banked = survived loot, lost = death loot — an accurate, self-consistent figure that needs no model of how value flows between loops. The lesson: when you want an aggregate, prefer summing a ledger you already maintain over deriving it from a downstream quantity that mixes in other effects; the ledger sum is exact and explainable, the derivation is an estimate dressed as a fact. (Same family as #151's derive-by-subtraction, opposite move: there the total was trustworthy and a part was expensive, so subtract; here the parts are trustworthy and the "total split" would be lossy, so sum the parts.) UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 123.

## Phase 12r — "Sell the Spoils" Keeps Your Gear (2026-06-12)

156. **A convenience button that's slightly too eager is a footgun wearing a helpful smile — "sell the spoils" dumped the player's gear along with the loot.** Gear lives in the inventory between raids (the expedition pack auto-equips the best you own), so a bulk "sell everything sellable" button would, with one click, liquidate the rune/dragon kit a player spent the whole game assembling. Nobody asked for that and the loss is expensive and irreversible. The fix is a default-safety change, not a feature: the bulk sell now excludes GEAR (`lootSpoils`), while the per-item "sell @ bid" still sells anything because that's a deliberate, targeted choice. The lesson is about where to put the guardrail: a BULK action over a heterogeneous set should default to the safe subset and make the dangerous superset opt-in, whereas a SINGLE-item action can trust the click — the blast radius of a mistake scales with how many things one gesture touches, so the protection belongs on the wide gesture, not the narrow one. Found this not by a bug report but by auditing an existing component while looking for the next brick — proof that "read the code you're about to extend" surfaces latent footguns, not just integration points. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 122.

## Phase 12q — Actionable Preparedness Fixes (2026-06-12)

155. **An informative warning is half a feature; the fix is the other half — and the cheapest way to test the hard-to-reach fix was a mechanism built two bricks earlier.** 12p told the player "pack antifire"; 12q gives them a one-click chip that does it. The product lesson is small and durable: when you surface a problem the user can act on, surface the action next to it — the distance between "you forgot X" and "X packed" should be one click, not a hunt through the loadout. The implementation lesson is the better one. Fire regions are locked for a fresh player, so the antifire warning is genuinely hard to reach in a test (you'd need to construct questProgress and click an SVG region node). But 12l's `regionPick` jump — "click a Delve Log row to raid that region again" — sets the embark screen's region via a nonce pulse with NO lock check. So the test seeds one Delve Log row for `dragons_maw`, clicks "raid here again", and lands on the fire region's embark screen ready to assert the warning and click the fix. A feature shipped for player convenience became the test fixture that made a LATER feature cleanly testable — the kind of compounding you only get when each brick leaves a real, reusable seam rather than a one-off. Generalises: reach for an existing user-facing path to set up a hard-to-reach state before constructing artificial fixtures; the real path exercises more of the system and stays honest. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 121.

## Phase 12p — Pre-Embark Preparedness Check (2026-06-12)
