154. **A loop can be richly instrumented for OUTCOMES and still silent on PREPARATION — and preparation is where the avoidable deaths live.** Six bricks of adventure instrumentation (embark forecast, live combat read, danger readout, conquest, delve log) all answered "how will this go / how did it go?" None answered "did you bring the right kit?" — yet the single biggest un-enforced failure is embarking a fire region with no antifire packed (the engine warns in flavor text and then just lets you burn). `embarkPrep` closes that: it inspects what you've PACKED (the draft), not what you OWN, against the region's hazards, so it's a live "you forgot X" nudge that clears as you add it. Two design notes: (1) the warning keys on the DRAFT, not inventory — owning antifire you didn't pack is exactly the mistake to catch, so checking the pack is the point, not a limitation; (2) the food warning is gated on `riskyFight = !forecast.favored` rather than always-on, because a persistent "no food!" nag on the trivial starting region trains players to ignore warnings — a warning that cries wolf is worse than no warning, so it only fires when a hard fight is actually plausible. The broader lesson: a mature decision surface should instrument the INPUTS to a choice (am I equipped?) as much as its projected outputs (will I win?) — the forecast tells you the odds, the prep check tells you whether you've stacked them. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 120.

## Phase 12o — Live In-Combat Forecast (2026-06-12)

153. **The same estimate can be "good enough" in one context and dangerously wrong in another — a flee-decision aid has to count the damage a pre-fight estimate can gloss.** 12j's embark forecast ignores dragonfire: it's a rough "should I go here?" read at full hp, and being a bit optimistic is harmless. But reusing that same forecast LIVE, mid-fight, to answer "push or flee?" raises the stakes — under-estimating incoming damage in a dragon fight is precisely the failure that gets a player killed, in exactly the fights where the aid matters most. So the live read folds in dragonfire's `+ceil(atk/2)/round` (when no antifire), via an optional `foeHitBonus` param on `combatForecast` — default 0 so the embark caller is untouched, single source of truth for both. The general lesson: when you REUSE an approximation in a higher-stakes context, re-audit which simplifications are still acceptable — accuracy tolerances aren't a property of the formula, they're a property of the DECISION the formula informs. Two omissions I kept, each justified rather than overlooked: leech drains loot not hp (irrelevant to the survival race), and roll variance (the mean is the right central estimate, and it's labelled ≈). Stats mirror the engine exactly (`deriveStats + exp.boost`, antifire from `exp.combat.antifire`) so the live numbers match what the fight actually rolls against. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 119.

## Phase 12n — Flip Consistency + the Engine-Content Blast-Radius Check (2026-06-12)

152. **Before reaching for engine content to escape thinning UI ideas, MEASURE the test blast radius — a one-line data change can cascade-break dozens of deterministic tests, and that's the wrong risk to take unsupervised.** Fourteen UI-only bricks deep, I evaluated adding named elites to the shallow regions (0–3) for gameplay depth. It looks trivial — four monster entries + four `region.elite` assignments, no new code, the elite spawn/loot/conservation all handled by existing generic paths. But adding `elite` to a region inserts an `rng.chance(ELITE_CHANCE)` call into `rollEncounter`, and `lumbridge_plains` (region 0) is the *workhorse* of `expedition.test.ts` — dozens of fixed-seed `advance`/encounter assertions run there. One extra RNG draw shifts the cursor and changes every subsequent deterministic outcome in those tests. The 12b content dodged this by putting its elite in region 4 (barely tested); region 0 is the opposite. **The lesson: in a deterministic engine, "additive" content is rarely additive to the RNG stream — anything that consumes a random draw reorders everything downstream, so the real cost of new content is proportional to how heavily the touched seed-path is asserted, NOT to the size of the diff.** Measured the radius, judged it too high for an unsupervised loop, parked engine content for a Jesse-present session, and shipped a clean UI brick instead: `tradeRecord` — flip consistency (hit-rate: items closed in profit vs loss) + your worst item, the dimension the magnitude-focused realized-profit panel never showed. The meta-lesson: knowing when NOT to do the flashier thing is part of the methodology; a measured "no" logged with its reasoning is a real deliverable. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 118.

## Phase 12m — Wealth Composition (2026-06-12)

151. **The number you can't easily reproduce is the one to DERIVE by subtraction — holdings = worth − cash − offers, so the split sums exactly without re-implementing the engine's valuation.** A net-worth breakdown (cash vs buy-order escrow vs goods) tempted a re-derivation of the engine's `netWorth` valuation in the UI, which values held goods by walking the resting bids (a bid-walk I'd have to mirror, with all the drift risk of #148's combat formula). The cleaner move: take the two components I CAN read exactly off the view — cash (`view.gp`) and buy escrow (`Σ remaining×price`) — and compute holdings as the **residual** `total − cash − buyOrders`. The split then sums to `playerWorth` by construction, with zero duplication of the hard part. Generalisable: when decomposing a total whose pieces have unequal computability, derive the EXPENSIVE piece by subtraction from the known total and the cheap pieces — you get exactness for free and dodge the duplication. (Contrast #148, where no total was available to subtract from, so the formula HAD to be mirrored.) Bonus honesty: `netWorth` excludes in-progress expedition loot, so I surfaced that as a separate "at risk in the wild" note rather than fudging it into the total — the breakdown tells the truth about what's banked vs what's still on the table. This was also a deliberate breadth choice: a different LENS (liquidity, whole-worth) over a different SCOPE than 12k's by-item holdings concentration. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 117.

## Phase 12l — Delve Log "Raid Here Again" (2026-06-12)

150. **Two ways to connect a child component to a parent action — lift its state, or pulse it a nonce — and the nonce wins when the child's state is load-bearing for a flow you don't want to destabilise.** Making Delve Log rows jump to their region needed App to drive ExpeditionPanel's region selection. The "proper" move is lifting `regionId` to App and making it a controlled prop — but that turns a working, e2e-covered expedition flow's region selector into controlled state, and every existing setter (RegionMap clicks, etc.) has to be rewired through the parent. Instead I reused the ticket-prefill pattern already in the codebase: App sends a `{regionId, n}` PULSE, ExpeditionPanel keeps its own `useState` and applies the pulse via a nonce-guarded effect. The nonce is what makes it idempotent-yet-repeatable: the same region can be re-picked because `n` changes each click, and a stale pulse never re-applies because the effect checks `n !== appliedRef.current`. The cost: the child still owns its state (no single source of truth) — acceptable here because nothing else needs to READ the selection. **Testing footgun worth recording: region NAMES appear in at least four places app-wide (DelvePanel, RegionMap SVG, ConquestPanel, the embark selector), so `getByText(regionName)` cross-matches and throws — I targeted the click via the row's UNIQUE title ("click to raid here again") instead.** Same lesson as #147: in an all-mounted multi-room app, text is rarely a unique handle; reach for a title/role/test-id when surfaces share vocabulary. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 116.

## Phase 12k — Portfolio Concentration (2026-06-12)

149. **Ten bricks of trading UI all measured one axis — return — and never the orthogonal one: risk.** Margins, ROI, realized/paper P&L, cost basis, break-even, average-down: every trading surface answered "how much will/did I make?" None answered "how exposed am I?" A portfolio can be richly profitable and dangerously all-in on one item, and nothing surfaced that. `positionConcentration` reuses the per-item marked values the Open Positions panel (12c) already had and turns them into weights + a single `topPct` (biggest exposure), rendered as a stacked allocation bar + a red/amber/green "top X% in <item>" caption. The lesson is about feature-space coverage, not code: when a system accretes many surfaces along one dimension, the highest-value next brick is often a *different dimension over the same data*, not a deeper cut of the same one — the data was already there (held values), only the lens (share-of-total vs profit-vs-cost) was new. Deliberately marked to **value, not cost**: concentration risk is about what you'd lose at today's prices, not what you paid. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 115.

## Phase 12j — Embark Combat Forecast (2026-06-12)

148. **When the engine's number is a random roll, the UI's honest forecast is its MEAN — and "estimate" labelling is what makes a duplicated formula acceptable.** The adventure side showed raw danger stats but left "can I win here?" as mental math, while the trading side is densely decision-instrumented. Added an embark forecast: ≈rounds to down the worst foe vs ≈rounds for it to down you, favored/risky. Combat damage is RNG (`max(1, roll[ceil(atk/3)..atk] − floor(def/4))`, quest.ts:394), so there's no single number to read — but its MEAN is the right central estimate for a go/no-go call, and `expectedHit` mirrors that mean. The player-strikes-first rule made the verdict a clean inequality: a tie (kill on the very round you'd fall) is a win, so `favored = roundsToKill <= roundsToFall`. The uncomfortable part worth recording: `expectedHit` **duplicates** engine logic in the UI, because the engine's `damage()` is local, unexported, and rng-shaped — there's no mean to import without an engine change (which I'm avoiding to keep bricks redeploy-free). I made the duplication SAFE-ish three ways: (1) a "keep in sync with quest.ts:394" comment pointing at the source of truth; (2) tests that pin `expectedHit` against hand-computed means, so the mirror is at least self-consistent; (3) labelling the whole feature an estimate (≈, "rounds roll with variance, not a promise") so a small drift degrades gracefully rather than lying. The general principle: duplicating a formula across a module boundary is a debt, not a sin — pay it down with a pointer comment + pinned tests + honest UX framing, and flag it for the day the source moves. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 114.

## Phase 12i — Persistent Delve Log (2026-06-12)

147. **The end-transition you already detect for one purpose is the hook for another — the Delve Log rode the death-detection effect that was already watching for `exp` to vanish.** The adventure loop had no per-expedition history (only aggregate stats + transient toasts), because the engine's expedition state is gone the instant you extract or die. Rather than build new end-detection, I extended the existing death-detection effect: it already snapshots `exp` each render and fires on the present→absent transition for the death toast. Adding a `DelveRecord` on that SAME transition — for extract as well as death — was one branch, not a new machine. The outcome classification fell out for free from a signal already in hand: `prev.inCombat`. You can only extract OUT of combat, so vanishing mid-combat is death and vanishing otherwise is a clean extract — no new state needed to tell them apart. **The e2e caught a real regression that the 318 unit tests couldn't: the always-mounted Hall means a new panel's empty-state copy ("…embark from the Adventure tab…") collided with the expedition e2e's loose `getByText('embark')`, a strict-mode violation.** Two lessons: (1) every tabhidden room is still in the DOM, so panel text competes with every loose text locator app-wide — keep copy distinctive or locators will cross-match; (2) the failure was *useful* — it proved the DelvePanel actually mounts and renders, which the green unit tests had only asserted in isolation. Fixed by rewording the copy (avoid clobbering the e2e's intent), not by tightening the locator. `Game.delves` was made OPTIONAL (per the 11s lesson) so the new field didn't force-edit every test Game literal. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 113.

## Phase 12h — Break-Even Sell Hint (2026-06-12)

146. **`avgCost / (1 - taxRate)` is the right intuition and the wrong number — integer floor-tax means the clean formula overshoots, so the break-even floor has to be searched, not divided.** Completing the ticket's pre-trade instrumentation (12d gave the buy side an average-down preview; the sell side had nothing), I added a break-even floor: the least sell price that recovers your cost after the 2% tax. The naive `avgCost / 0.98` gives 103 for an avg of 100 — but the GE tax is `floor(price * 0.02)`, so 102 already clears (102 − floor(2.04)=2 → 100 = cost exactly). `breakEvenSell` uses the ceil of the clean formula as an UPPER bound, then tightens DOWN to the true least integer where `price − floor(price*taxRate) >= avgCost`. The lesson generalises: whenever a domain rounds with `floor`/`ceil` at integer granularity (gp, ticks, shares), the continuous closed-form is only an estimate — the exact answer lives within ±1 and has to be nailed by a tiny bounded search. I pinned it with a **property test** rather than just example values: for several costs, assert the returned price recovers cost AND one gp under does not — which catches an off-by-one in either tightening loop that hand-picked examples might miss. Players sell just above their average and lose money to the tax constantly; this line makes the real "sell above this to profit" explicit. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 112.

## Phase 12g — Market Keyboard Navigation (2026-06-12)

145. **The cleanest new feature is often the one that needs no new state — j/k market nav rode entirely on the `selected`/`onSelect` the table already had.** Adding keyboard navigation looked like it needed a "cursor" concept (a highlighted row index, its own class, sync with sort). But the market table already had a `selected` prop that highlights the row AND loads the ticket through `onSelect`. So j/k just move `selected` through the displayed `sorted` array and the highlight + ticket follow for free — zero new state, zero new CSS. The whole feature is one pure clamp (`nextRowIndex`) plus a keydown listener. Two traps the build surfaced and how they were dodged: (1) **all three room boards stay mounted** (`board tabhidden`, not conditional render), so MarketTable's window listener is live even on the Adventure/Hall tabs — j/k would silently move the hidden market. Fixed with an `active={room==='exchange'}` prop gating the handler. I first reached for an `offsetParent === null` visibility check instead, then realised jsdom does no layout so it'd mis-fire in every test — an explicit prop beats inferring visibility, and it's testable. (2) **Stale-closure** on a once-bound listener: rather than re-binding the listener every render (churn) or listing deps, I funnelled current `{sorted, selected, onSelect, active}` through a single `navRef` updated each render — bind once, always read fresh. The nav deliberately reads the *displayed* (post sort+filter) order, not the raw item list, so the cursor always matches what's on screen — the one thing that would feel broken if gotten wrong. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 111.

## Phase 12f — Region Conquest Codex (2026-06-12)

144. **The data for a feature can be fully present for bricks and still hide the feature — `killsByMonster` had everything Region Conquest needed; what was missing was the *lens*, not the data.** The engine tracked per-monster lifetime kills and the bestiary showed a flat per-monster count, but nobody had grouped those kills BY REGION to ask "have you slain everything that lives here?" — the classic 100%-completion hook. Added `regionRoster(region)` (pool + elite, deduped) and `regionMastery(region, kills)` → `{slain, total, done}`, surfaced as a Hall panel that crowns (👑) each region once every native foe falls. Three notes worth keeping: (1) **mastery `done` requires the elite too**, a strictly deeper bar than the existing region-*unlock* state (`RegionMap`'s ✓ means "you advanced past here", which you can do without ever killing the elite) — so I used 👑, not ✓, to avoid conflating two different "done"s in the same UI. (2) **Spoiler-safety was free**: I started to gate locked regions behind `questProgress`, then checked `RegionMap` and found it already renders every region's name (and even names the elites) with just a 🔒 on locked nodes — so listing all 8 regions reveals nothing the map doesn't, and the "0/3 in a locked region" rows actually *advertise* content ahead rather than spoil it. Verifying the sibling component's existing treatment killed a half-built gating branch. (3) `done` guards `total > 0`, which doubles as the divide-by-zero guard for the bar width — one invariant, two payoffs. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 110.

## Phase 12e — Daily Personal Best (2026-06-11)

143. **A shared leaderboard answers "did I beat others"; a personal best answers "did I beat *me*" — and the second is the cheaper, stickier hook because it needs no population.** After 5 trading bricks I deliberately pivoted to the retention system for breadth. The daily had a streak ember and a shared board but no personal record, the most proven solo retention mechanic. Added `recordDailyBest(prev, today, worth)` and `dailyBestView(best, today, worth, startGp)`, both pure, surfaced as a masthead 🏁 tag (green ▲ when you're at a new peak right now). Two design choices worth recording: (1) `recordDailyBest` copies `bumpStreak`'s **same-reference-when-unchanged** contract — net worth changes almost every tick but a *new high* is rare, so returning the identical object lets the effect skip the localStorage write on the common case; without this the pref would thrash on every tick. The effect is keyed on `game.world.tick` (so it sees each worth change) but writes only on a real record — the cheap max+compare runs hot, the expensive persist runs cold. (2) The **show/hide + at-peak** decision went into its own pure `dailyBestView` rather than a chain of JSX `&&`s, so "hidden off the record's day / before any progress past the start" and "atPeak ⇔ worth ≥ best" are truth-tabled, not eyeballed. The day key is just `dailySeed()` (the existing YYYYMMDD value) — reusing the one day-definition the streak/ember already share means the best, the streak, and the board can never disagree about when "today" is. Hidden until `best > startGp` keeps the baseline quiet (no "🏁 50k" the instant you load a fresh daily). UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 109.

## Phase 12d — Average-Down Buy Preview (2026-06-11)

142. **A position panel tells you where you ARE; the next decision tool tells you where a buy TAKES you — "averaging down or chasing?" is mental math the ticket can just do.** 12c surfaced every open position's cost basis; the natural follow-on is the *buy* that reshapes one. The ticket showed order cost but not the resulting blended average, so adding to a holding was a blind reshape. Added `blendBuy(open, addQty, addPrice)` → new units + quantity-weighted avg + a signed `delta` (avgCost − prevAvg), rendered under the order summary as "after this buy: N @ avg Y (was X) ↓ averaging down / ↑ averaging up / · unchanged". Two correctness notes worth keeping: (1) buys carry **no GE tax** (tax is sell-side only), so the blend is raw cost / raw units — matching the FIFO book's lot prices exactly; getting this wrong would drift the preview from the position panel it sits next to. (2) `blendBuy` returns **null when there's no position to blend with** — a fresh buy has no average to move, so the preview correctly stays hidden and only appears for the average-down/up case it's built for (gating the UI on a null-returning pure function keeps the "when to show" logic in one tested place, not scattered in JSX conditionals). The signed `delta` (vs a `lower: boolean`) was the right shape because it expresses three states — down, up, *and flat* — where a boolean would have mislabelled a flat add as "up". UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 108.

## Phase 12c — Open Positions Panel (2026-06-11)

141. **Cost basis you can only see one item at a time is a spreadsheet with one cell visible — the portfolio question ("which of my holdings is up, which is bleeding?") needs every position marked to market in one glance.** The ticket showed avg-cost for the *selected* item (11i) and ProfitPanel showed *realized* (closed-trade) profit (11b/11o), but nothing showed all *open* positions and their *unrealized* P&L together — you had to click each market row in turn to reconstruct your book by hand. Added `heldPositions(book, markOf)` (pure: price lookup injected, lots iterated in sorted-id order) → one row per open bought position with units, weighted avg cost, live mark, value, cost, and unrealized gp + %, sorted best paper P&L first. The PositionsPanel renders it as the unrealized sibling of ProfitPanel's realized rows, marked at last price (same convention as the ticket), each row click-loading the item in the ticket via the already-proven `onSelect` path — so a brand-new surface adds ZERO new command/mutation wiring, just a read + a navigation reuse. Two judgement calls worth recording: (1) an *unmarked* position (no live price) is listed **flat** (`mark = avgCost`, unrealized 0), never as a fake −100% loss — an honest "no info" beats a scary wrong number; (2) named `heldPositions`/`HeldPosition`, NOT `openPositions`, because a one-letter plural collision with the existing fills-based `openPosition` is exactly the kind of thing that bites future-self at 2am. The `.rows li` flex+space-between layout absorbed a 4th span (avg→now) with no new CSS — reusing a layout primitive is cheaper than theming a variant. UI-only, no engine change → no verify-score redeploy. Brick 107.

## Phase 12b — Ship the Forge/Skarn/Altar Content (PR #1 merge) (2026-06-11)

140. **A merge held back by a *risk* should be released the moment you can measure the risk to zero — not held on principle.** Three replay-affecting RPG features (Field Forge burn-300gp→+8atk event, Skarn elite in wilderness_ruins, Blood Altar hp→atk event) sat parked on `feat/forge-event` for ~18 autonomous bricks because shipping engine content without redeploying verify-score in the same phase creates a worth-discrepancy for leaderboard replays — and the SUPABASE_ACCESS_TOKEN to redeploy isn't available in autonomous sessions. The parking was correct *while the cost was unknown*. Brick 106 finally measured it: the live leaderboard holds exactly ONE row (`seed:666, handle:"jesse", worth:5460, deepest:0` — a self-test with no expeditions). A dormant board with no real population can't be harmed by a soft discrepancy, and the verifier takes only `{handle,seed,log}` (no claimed-hash gate), so a stale verifier *soft*-flags rather than *hard*-rejects. With blast radius ≈ nil, the long-held parking stance was reversed and the content merged to main via `--no-ff`. The lesson: "don't ship X until Y is present" is a proxy for "don't pay cost Z" — when you can prove Z is currently zero, the proxy expires. **The merge surfaced exactly one stale assertion: `app.test.tsx`'s `regionDanger` test pinned wilderness_ruins to its pre-elite profile (fire_giant atk19/def11/hp85, elite:false); the merged Skarn (atk24/def13/hp130, 37>30 by atk+def) correctly displaced it — the test was load-bearing and caught the content's intended effect, updated to the new profile.** verify-score redeploy is now the one pending obligation, flagged loudly in NEXT_STEPS for when Jesse is present (engine.js rebuilt + committed; only the `supabase functions deploy` remains). Brick 106.

## Phase 12a — Sell-Above (Take-Profit) Alerts (2026-06-11)

139. **A price-alert system that only fires on the way DOWN is half a trade — "tell me when to buy" needs its mirror "tell me when to sell".** The watchlist had `≤` buy-below alerts (9w) but nothing for the exit, so a player accumulating a position had no way to be pinged at their take-profit. Added `≥` sell-above alerts as a deliberate MIRROR, not a rewrite: a separate `ew-sell-alerts` pref + a separate `sellFired` re-arm latch + a second firing loop, leaving the proven buy path byte-for-byte untouched (the lowest-risk way to extend a working feature is to parallel it, not refactor it). The one piece worth unifying was the threshold test — `alertHit(last, threshold, dir)` (`below` = `≤`, `above` = `≥`) — so both directions, the App firing loops, and the watchlist's 🔔 indicator share ONE definition of "hit" and can't drift. Both `100 ≤ 100` and `100 ≥ 100` fire (at-threshold counts), pinned by the truth table. Added `aria-label`s to the two number inputs while there (a free a11y win — "buy-below alert for Shark" vs an unlabelled spinner). Mirror tested the same way the buy alert was (sell threshold 1 → always-met → 🔔 row). The alert system is now symmetric: enter and exit, both pinged. Brick 105.

## Phase 11z — Bounty Progress Bar & Reward-Per-Kill (2026-06-11)

138. **Progress as a fraction in text ("2/4 slain") is information; progress as a bar is a glance — and a fresh side-surface usually wants both the visual and the value metric the core loop already has.** Sibling to the contracts brick (11y), the Bounty Board listed kills/qty + total reward but made you read the numbers to judge "how close" and "is this worth it". Added a thin fill bar (`pct = kills/qty`, gold → green when claimable) so the closest-to-done bounty is obvious without reading, and "(≈{reward/qty}/kill)" so an elite bounty's higher per-kill pay is visible — which IS actionable here because the player chooses which region to delve, and elites stalk specific regions. Both are pure arithmetic over `killsByMonster` and the bounty fields already in `game.world`. The two side-mechanics (Quartermaster contracts, realm bounties) are now at parity with the core trading panels on decision-info: every "should I do this?" surface shows the margin/premium and a readiness/progress cue, not just the gross number. Render test pins the exact case (2/4 → 50% bar width, 2000/4 → "≈500/kill"). Brick 104 — and with it the contracts+bounty side-boards join the trading cockpit, expedition decision panel, and progression dashboard as fully decision-instrumented.

## Phase 11y — Contract Premium & Ready Flag (2026-06-11)

137. **A panel that promises "15–35% over market" but shows only the gross gp forces the player to do the arithmetic the UI already has the inputs for.** The Quartermaster board listed each contract's `qty × unitPrice = total gp` and a deliver button, but not the one number that decides *which* contract is worth chasing: how much it beats just selling on the GE. `contractPremium(unitPrice, marketPrice)` = `(unitPrice − market) / market` surfaces "+22%" per row (green), so a 35%-over deal visibly outranks a 15% one — the Quartermaster's version of the flip-margin/value-band that trading already had. Crucially it can go NEGATIVE: if the market spiked above the agreed price since the contract posted, delivering is now a worse deal than selling, and the red "−8%" says so honestly (the generic "15–35%" copy never could). Also flagged fillable contracts (✓ + faint-green row) since "can I deliver this right now?" is the other half of the decision and the disabled-button state alone is easy to miss. Same shape as every recent surfacing brick: the market price and your inventory were both already in the view; the value was one subtraction and one comparison away. First touch of the contracts surface — fresh area, same lesson. Brick 103.

## Phase 11x — Away-Bar Rate & Compaction (2026-06-11)

136. **The offline summary's total answers "how much" but the idle player's real question is "is my setup WORKING" — and that's a rate, not a sum.** The away-bar (seen on every return) showed the cumulative worth delta, but a +2,400,000 after 20 hours and a +2,400,000 after 20 minutes mean opposite things about your automation. `offlineRatePerMin(delta, ticks)` (1 offline tick ≡ 1 second, so ticks/60 = minutes) turns the gulf into "≈2K/min" — the figure that says whether the clerk/sellsword is actually productive. Shown only when `delta > 0` (a loss has no meaningful "earning rate"), and `fmtCompact` finally landed here (long-queued) so a 28-hour haul reads "+2.4M" not a wall of digits, with the exact value in the title. The rate computation is a one-liner but extracted to a pure helper precisely because the away-bar's own render is hard to test deterministically (offline accrual depends on the sim), so the logic gets a clean truth-table test (60k/10min → 6k/min, losses negative, zero-ticks → 0) while the wiring rides the existing away-banner render test. Pattern: when a UI value's *display path* is non-deterministic to test, pull the *computation* into a pure function and test that — the render only needs to prove it doesn't crash. Brick 102.

## Phase 11w — Worth ETA Projection (2026-06-11)

135. **An idle game's most motivating number is the one that doesn't exist yet — "≈16m to 2M" turns a flat rate into a countdown.** The chart showed where your worth has been (curve) and how fast it's moving (gp/min, 10x), but not the forward question every idle player asks: *when do I hit the next big number?* `nextRoundTarget(n)` picks the next 1/2/5×10^k above current worth (55k→100k, 1.2M→2M, 6M→10M — the "nice" thresholds people actually chase), and the ETA is just `(target − worth) / perMin`. The whole thing is self-contained in WorthChart because the chart already holds `history`, and `worthRate(history)` + last-worth derive both inputs — no new prop, the projection materialized from data the panel already had (the recurring session theme, one more time). Two honesty guards: shown ONLY when `perMin > 0` (no projecting a target you're drifting away from — a negative or flat rate gives no countdown), and the 1/2/5 ladder means the target is always a number a human recognizes as a goal, not an arbitrary "+10%". Reused `fmtDuration` (minutes→"16m"/"2h") and `fmtCompact` (target→"2M") so it speaks the UI's existing vocabulary. Brick 101. Tested the ladder exactly (150k→200k, not 150k→151k) and the end-to-end render (5k/min from 150k → "≈10m to 200K").

## Phase 11v — Getting Started checklist (2026-06-11)

134. **A complex game's onboarding gap isn't a tutorial — it's the absence of a PROSPECTIVE list, and the data to self-check it already exists.** The Deeds panel (and 11s/11t) is retrospective: it celebrates what you've done. A new player drowning in a dense terminal needs the opposite — "what do I do next?" — which a 12-bullet help overlay (read once, forgotten) doesn't provide. `firstSteps(game, view)` is four concrete actions (trade / hire a clerk / fell a monster / reach the sprint horizon), each with a `done` boolean derived from the SAME state the rest of the UI reads (`fills.length`, `view.upgrades.autoFlip`, `stats.monstersSlain`, `world.tick`), so the checklist self-ticks as you play with zero new tracking. Two anti-nag rules keep it from outstaying its welcome: it auto-hides the instant all four are done (a veteran never sees it), and a persisted dismiss (`usePref`) lets the impatient kill it early. Tested the all-done path through the REAL command (`applyCommand buyUpgrade autoFlip`) rather than poking `agent.upgrades`, so the test proves `playerView` actually surfaces a bought clerk — the same end-to-end the player walks. Placed it as the first panel in the Exchange room (where you land), not a modal, so it's reference-not-interruption. Brick 100; FINDINGS hits #134 — a session-long lesson that the highest-leverage UI is almost always *surfacing state the architecture already holds*, whether that's P&L, danger, deeds, or "you're new here, start with this".

## Phase 11u — One-Click Sell Your Position (2026-06-11)

133. **The right place for a one-click action is wherever the state it needs already lives — the position line owns nothing it had to ask App for.** TopFlips' one-click buy (11d) had to thread a buy price up through a callback because the trigger (a flips row) lives outside the ticket and the prefill machinery bridges that gap. The mirror action — one-click sell your whole holding — turned out far simpler because the cost-basis line (11i) is *inside* `TradeTicket`, which already owns `side`/`price`/`qty` state AND has `market` + `position` in scope. So the "sell N" chip just calls `setSide('sell'); setPrice(ask−1); setQty(units)` directly — no prefill nonce, no App prop, no callback. Lesson: before reaching for the cross-component plumbing pattern, check whether the action's origin already sits next to its inputs; internal triggers set state, only external ones need the bridge. Completes the round-trip symmetry (one-click buy a flip ↔ one-click sell a holding) that the trader was missing on the exit side. Button labelled "sell {units}" not "sell" specifically so `getByText('sell')` still resolves to the side toggle alone (locator-collision care, #59 again). Also quietly fixed the position tooltip's stale "(recent fills)" — it's been lifetime since the 11o book. Live on main; PR #1 content batch parked.

## Phase 11t — Deed Markers on the Fortune Curve (2026-06-11)

132. **Two features built one brick apart compose into a third for free — the achievement ticks (11s) had no home until the chart could place them.** Recording milestone ticks last brick was useful on its own ("🏅 latest · 10m ago"), but it also made *this* possible: marking each earned deed as a dot on the Fortune curve, at the exact (tick, worth) where you crossed it — so you see "I hit Dragon Slayer right as my wealth spiked". The placement reuses `ghostWorthAt(history, tick)` — the same interpolation helper the ghost-delta already used — to find the curve's worth at an arbitrary achievement tick (which rarely lands on a 50-tick sample boundary), so the dot sits *on* the line, not floating. The one correctness rule is the range filter: `worthHistory` is capped at 240 samples, so a deed earned before the oldest retained sample (`tick < minT`) has no curve to sit on and is silently skipped — pinned by a test with one in-range deed (marked) and one past `maxT` (dropped). SVG `<title>` inside each `<circle>` gives a native hover tooltip even though the chart svg is `aria-hidden`. Pattern worth naming: when you add a piece of state, the *next* brick is often "what view can now show it" — timestamped deeds → latest-line → curve markers is the same datum surfaced three ways, each cheap because the one before built the foundation.

## Phase 11s — Timestamped Deeds (2026-06-11)

131. **The achievement system recorded WHAT but threw away WHEN — one line at the latch point reclaims a whole dimension of run pacing.** Milestones were a `string[]` of latched ids; the moment of earning each was discarded. Stamping `game.milestoneTicks[id] = game.world.tick` right where `checkMilestones` pushes the id captures the tick for free (the function already runs inside the tick loop and the world tick is right there), and the panel surfaces a "🏅 latest: <deed> · Nm ago" line — a sense of how recently you accomplished something, which a static ◆/◇ grid can't convey. Made the field OPTIONAL (`milestoneTicks?:`) deliberately: unlike the trade book (11o, required → forced updating two manual `Game` literals in tests), an optional field needs zero migration churn — old saves read `undefined`, the panel's `?? {}` handles it, no test literal touched. The cost asymmetry is real: a required field ripples to every Game constructor; an optional one ripples nowhere. Picked the visible "latest" line over per-badge tooltip timing because the badge grid is hover-only and a single always-visible line pairs naturally with the existing "next goals" list — what you just did, what's next. Tested the stamp at the source (`monstersSlain=1` → 'first-blood' latched → tick recorded) and the render (earned at tick 0, now tick 600 → "10m ago"). Live on main; PR #1 content batch still parked.

## Phase 11r — Daily Board Framing (2026-06-11)

130. **Two systems built apart click together with one comparison — the daily seed and the per-seed board were always the same competition, never labelled as one.** The leaderboard is per-seed; the daily (10q) makes everyone share one seed each day. So when `seed === dailySeed()`, the Sprint Board you're looking at IS the daily competition — but it just said "Sprint Board · seed 20260611", burying the social hook. One boolean (`isDaily`) reframes the header to "🗓 Daily Board · today's shared world — everyone racing the daily competes here". No new data, no new fetch: the board was already keyed by seed, and the daily was already a seed; this is pure connective tissue making an existing relationship legible. The cohesion with 11q compounds — "you're #N on this seed" now reads as your standing in *today's daily race* when on the daily. Lesson worth keeping: the cheapest high-value features are often a single predicate that names a relationship the architecture already encodes (here, `seed === dailySeed()`), not new machinery. Tested by rendering the board on `newGame(dailySeed())` and asserting the reframed title; seed-42 tests still see "Sprint Board", confirming the branch.

## Phase 11q — "You" on the Sprint Board (2026-06-11)

129. **A leaderboard without "where am I?" is just a list of strangers — and the honest match was by handle, not by exposing user ids.** The Sprint Board ranked verified runs but never marked your own, so a competitive player couldn't find themselves. The clean identity would be `user_id`, but `fetchLeaderboard` deliberately selects only `handle,worth,deepest` (the schema's intent: don't leak auth UUIDs to a public read), so I respected that and matched by the player's sanitized handle instead. The one real trap: `sanitizeHandle('')` returns `'anonymous trader'`, so a naive match would tag *every* unnamed entry as "you" — `myRank` guards it (empty/whitespace raw handle → null, and the anonymous fallback never matches), pinned by a test. Highlighted the row (gold left-border) + a "← you" marker + a "you're #N on this seed" line; since the board is capped at 10, being matched means you're top-10 and your row is on screen, so the rank line and the highlight reinforce rather than duplicate. Pure `myRank(rows, rawHandle)` does the work (email-shaped handles fold to local part, matching the server's own rule); the render is just `meRank === i+1`. First non-trading-cockpit brick in a while — the leaderboard is the competitive surface, and "find yourself" is the table stake it was missing. Tested with the existing `fetchRoutes` Supabase stub (handle in localStorage, two rows, assert the marker + rank).

## Phase 11p — Trading Scorecard (2026-06-11)

128. **Once the lifetime book existed, the bottom line was two sums away — realized (locked in) + unrealized (paper) is the scorecard the per-item rows never added up.** ProfitPanel listed per-item realized P&L but never totalled it, and said nothing about open positions' paper gains. `totalRealized(book)` sums `book.realized.profit`; `totalUnrealized(book, priceOf)` marks every open lot at the current price — `(price − cost) × qty`. The split mirrors a real brokerage: realized is money you actually banked (tax already netted in the book), unrealized is what the market says your holdings are worth *right now* and evaporates if prices move. Injecting `priceOf` as a function kept `totalUnrealized` pure and testable (mark a@130 → +180, b@40 → −50, net +130) without coupling to the view; the component supplies `view.markets` lastPrice, the test supplies a literal map. The header now reads "realized +X · paper +Y", shown only when either is non-zero so a fresh account stays clean. One test-collision to mind: the realized *total* and a lone item *row* can both render the same "+180", so `getByText('+180')` becomes ambiguous — switched to `getAllByText(...).length > 0`, the same lesson as #59 (exact-text locators collide when a value appears twice). Completes the P&L arc (11b realized rows → 11i cost basis → 11o lifetime book → 11p scorecard): the trader now sees every layer from a single flip's margin up to their whole-run bottom line.

## Phase 11o — Lifetime Trade Book (2026-06-11)

127. **Reimplement-as-wrapper turns a refactor's existing tests into a correctness proof for the new core.** The P&L was window-bound: `realizedPnL`/`openPosition` recomputed FIFO over the capped 50-fill array each render, so an active trader's early flips fell off and "realized profit" silently shrank. The fix is a persistent `TradeBook` (FIFO lots + realized-per-item) accrued ONE fill at a time in `recordFills` — so it survives the window, pinned by a test that buries a +96 gold flip under 60 filler fills and shows it still remembered. The refactor's safety came from making `applyFillToBook` the single FIFO truth and then *reimplementing* the old `realizedPnL`/`openPosition` as one-line wrappers (`realizedFromBook(bookFromFills(fills, tax))`) — their existing truth-table tests (the clean round-trip, the FIFO-across-lots loss leg, the open/orphan exclusions) now re-run against the NEW core and would fail loudly on any divergence; they passed, so the new book provably matches the proven window math. Migration was the only genuinely new risk: old saves have no book, so `normalizeGame` rebuilds it from whatever fills survived (recent only — lost history can't be reconstructed, but it seeds correctly forward), tested by deleting `tradeBook` from a save and asserting the rebuild. Two boundary subtleties: `applyFillToBook`'s once-only accrual rides the *same* dedupe guard that gates `fills.push` (so each fill books exactly once), and `openPosition`'s wrapper passes tax 0 because open lots are tax-independent (tax only touches realized profit, never lot quantities). UI-only — the book lives on `Game`, not `WorldState`, so determinism/replay are untouched. ProfitPanel + the ticket cost basis now read lifetime; "recent" is gone.

## Phase 11n — Per-Axis Danger Colour (2026-06-11)

126. **Colour the FACTS per axis and you get a survival cue without a survival verdict — "their ⚔ beats your 🛡" is true; "you'll die here" is a guess.** 11l/11m put both sides' stats on the embark screen; the visual completion is colouring the foe's numbers relative to yours. The trap I kept dodging (a green/red "ready?" badge) needs a survivability model — hp, food, dragonfire, leech, play all matter — and would lie. But colouring each foe stat *independently* against the matching player stat is pure fact: the foe's ⚔ goes red exactly when `foe.atk > your def` (their hits land through your armour), the foe's 🛡 goes red exactly when `foe.def ≥ your atk` (your hits barely dent). A fresh player at Lumbridge sees ⚔4 RED (a goblin really can hit your def-2 body) but 🛡1 GREEN (your atk-5 shreds it) — both true, and together they tell the real story (you win, but you'll take chip damage) without claiming an outcome. The per-axis split is what keeps it honest: a single combined verdict would have to weigh offence against defence and that weighting is the guess. Reused `deriveStats` (11m) for the player side and `regionDanger` (11l) for the foe side — the cue is just two comparisons over numbers already computed. Test pins the exact case (PLAYER_BASE atk5/def2 → ⚔4 down, 🛡1 up at Lumbridge), which doubles as documentation of the colour rule. Live on main; PR #1 (Forge+Skarn+Altar) still parked.

## Phase 11m — Effective Combat Stats (2026-06-11)

125. **A level isn't a power — the sheet showed Attack 14 but not that your rune gear makes you fight at ⚔24, and that's the number that matters next to a region's danger.** 11l put the region's foe stats on screen ("foes up to ⚔30 🛡16") but left the player no comparable number for themselves: the character sheet shows skill *levels*, while combat resolves on `deriveStats` (base + level + best-usable-gear bonus). Surfacing `deriveStats(inventory, levels)` as an "in battle: ⚔X 🛡Y" line closes the loop — now both sides of the embark decision are in the same units, side by side. The engine already had the exact function combat uses, so the UI just *reads the authority* rather than re-deriving (no risk of the sheet disagreeing with what actually swings). Pairs intentionally with 11l: danger (their worst) vs in-battle (your effective best-gear) is the honest comparison, still no verdict — the player does the subtraction. Reused the same `agent.inventory`-best-gear basis as the equiplist/`equipped`, so the "in battle" number reflects exactly the kit the paperdoll lights up. Test pins the line renders and the `.effstats` text matches `⚔\d+ 🛡\d+`. Live on main; PR #1's 3-feature content batch still parked.

## Phase 11l — Region Danger Readout (2026-06-11)

124. **An honest factual readout beats a fragile verdict — show the foe's stats, let the player judge against their own.** Picking a region to raid, the player had the flavor text and the unlock gate but no sense of *how hard it hits*. The tempting design is a "✓ ready / ⚠ tough" badge, but that needs a survivability model (which depends on the pack you haven't chosen yet) and would lie when wrong. Instead `regionDanger(region)` reports the hardest-hitting foe — the max-`atk+def` monster across the pool PLUS any named elite — as raw "foes up to ⚔N 🛡M · H hp", which the player weighs against the Attack/Defence on their own character sheet right above. No verdict to be wrong about; just the fact that was hidden. Including the elite in the scan matters: a region's *typical* foe and its *worst* foe differ by a lot (the Maw's dragons hit ~⚔24 but Vorkanth hits ⚔30), and "danger" should mean the worst case you might meet, not the average. Pure function over `monsterById`, so the truth-table test pins exact numbers (wilderness_ruins → fire_giant 19/11/85, no elite on main; Maw → elite true, atk ≥30). The same helper will automatically fold in Skarn/the deeper elites once PR #1 merges — it reads whatever `region.elite` is. Live on main; the 3-feature content batch still sits in PR #1 (paused growing it — 3 features is a clean mergeable size).

## Phase 11k — Equip-Slot Gear Icons (2026-06-11)

123. **Reuse-by-spread: `itemIcon` returns a `{name, glyph}` pair, so the Icon goes in via `{...itemIcon(id)}`, not `name={itemIcon(id)}` — and typecheck caught the difference instantly.** The character sheet's equiplist showed slot → item *name* + the 10w lock hints, but no icon, while the Ledger and pack lists already iconified via the `itemIcon` pipeline. Adding a 14px gear icon before each worn item's name makes the sheet scannable at a glance (sword for weapons, shield for armor — `itemIcon` maps gear back to the skill icons). The one snag was a type slip: I wrote `name={itemIcon(id)}` assuming it returned a string, but `itemIcon` returns `{name, glyph}` (the emoji fallback travels with the name), so the fix was `<Icon {...itemIcon(id)} />` — and `tsc` flagged "{name,glyph} is not assignable to string" before a single test ran, the value of typecheck-as-gate over test-as-gate for shape errors. (vitest transpiles and would have rendered something wrong silently.) Cohesion compounding: skill icons (9n), item icons (10o), monster portraits (10p), gear hints (10w), now equip icons — the HUD's original-art layer is uniformly component-shared. Live on main; the Forge+Skarn+Altar content batch sits in PR #1.

## Phase 11i — Open-Position Cost Basis (2026-06-11)

121. **The realized panel said what flips you finished; the missing half is whether you're up on what you still HOLD — same FIFO idea, the leftover lots instead of the matched ones.** ProfitPanel (11b) reports completed round-trips; nothing showed unrealized P&L on open positions. `openPosition(fills, itemId)` runs the identical FIFO match but reads the *opposite* output: after sells consume buys, the *remaining* buy lots ARE what you bought and still hold, and their quantity-weighted average is your cost basis. The ticket shows "position: N @ avg X · ±Y% now" for the selected item — compare avgCost to lastPrice and you see the unrealized gain before you ever sell. Two honesty boundaries, both tested: loot has no buy fill so it contributes no basis (a sell with no prior buy is just dropped — you can't have negative held), and like realizedPnL it's window-bounded, so the label is deliberately "cost basis (recent fills)", not all-time. Chose this additive ticket signal over the bigger persistent-lifetime-P&L rewrite (still queued) precisely because it needed ZERO save-format change — `openPosition` is a pure read over the existing fills, passed as one prop. Render test seeds `game.fills` with a single buy and asserts the line renders for the default-selected item; the `getNodeText` quirk (direct text nodes only) means `getByText(/@ avg 100/)` matches the `<p>` even though the unit count sits in a nested `<b>`. Realized (done) + unrealized (holding) now complete the trader's P&L picture.

## Phase 11h — Net-Worth Trajectory Sparkline (2026-06-11)

120. **Three views of the same quantity beat one: the masthead now answers "how much / how fast / which way" at a glance.** (#119 is Skarn, batched into PR #1.) Net worth was a number (current), then 10x added the gp/min rate (speed); the missing third was *trajectory* — is the curve climbing or rolling over? The `worthHistory` the Fortune chart persists was already the right data; the `Sparkline` component already drew series. So the brick was mostly composition: feed worth samples to a 72×18 Sparkline in the purse, gated on `length >= 2` so the component's "no data" fallback never shows in the masthead. The one real change to a shared component was an `ariaLabel` prop (default 'recent trend') — needed because an unlabelled second sparkline would have made `getByLabelText(/trend/)` ambiguous across the ticket's and the masthead's; giving the masthead 'net worth trend' keeps both individually queryable, and the wiring test relies on exactly that (`queryByLabelText('net worth trend')` is null at boot with one sample, present after a +1k fast-forward adds the second). Now current/rate/trajectory form a complete wealth dashboard in one strip. Live on main; the Forge+Skarn content batch still sits in PR #1 awaiting one merge + redeploy.

## Phase 11f — Order-Book Liquidity Bar (2026-06-11)

118. **Surfacing a signal the BOTS already trade on levels the field between player and AI — and it was one derived view field away.** (#117 is the Field Forge, on branch `feat/forge-event`/PR #1 awaiting a coupled verify-score redeploy — numbered there.) The market view has carried `bidDepth`/`askDepth` (total resting qty per side) since the start; the agent flipper uses `bidDepth` for its `exitCap` (don't dump more into the bids than they'll absorb without crashing). The player saw none of it — they had price (sparkline), value (band), margin (flip line), but no *liquidity*. The ticket now shows a `depthSplit`-proportioned bar plus exact numbers: bids are your EXIT (what you can sell into now), asks your ENTRY (what's there to lift). Framing the two sides as exit/entry rather than abstract "buy/sell depth" ties the microstructure to the player's actual decision (how much can I offload before the price moves?). Pure `depthSplit(bid, ask)` → percentages or null-when-empty made the math a one-line test; the wiring test leaned on determinism — fast-forward seed 42 by 1000 ticks and the book is *guaranteed* to have depth (same seed → same resting orders), so `document.querySelector('.depthbar')` is a stable assertion, not a flaky one. Policy settling in: live UI ships to main continuously; replay-affecting engine content batches into a PR for one merge+deploy.

## Phase 11d — One-Click Flip (2026-06-11)

116. **Make the informational panel operational by threading one number through the callback — and you get composition coverage for free.** TopFlips told you the best flip and its buy price, but clicking only *selected* the item; the player still had to set side=buy and retype bid+1. The fix is tiny: widen the click callback from `(id)` to `(id, buyPrice)` so the component hands back the buy price it already computed, and App routes that into the existing `onLevel('buy', price)` prefill — landing the player in the ticket with the buy leg ready to submit. The leverage was in NOT building new plumbing: `onLevel`→ticket prefill was already tested (the ladder-click test), and TopFlips→callback is one component test asserting `onSelect('gold_bar', 1001)`, so the App handler (two lines composing two already-tested paths) needs no fragile flip-dependent integration test — coverage falls out of composition. Deliberately did NOT prefill a quantity: `TicketPrefill` is `{side, price}` and the player's size depends on capital/limit/appetite, so auto-filling qty would be presumptuous where price is just the obvious mechanical default. Also did NOT auto-submit — a click loads the order, it doesn't place it; the strip became one-click-to-*ready*, not one-click-to-*committed*, which keeps the mutation explicit (the player still hits the button). Same restraint as #115: give the player the obvious next step, stop short of deciding for them.

## Phase 11c — Flip Efficiency & Limit (2026-06-11)

115. **When a "better" metric needs a model you can't verify, show the honest metrics and let the player rank — don't launder a guess into the sort order.** The queued idea was to rank TopFlips by *realizable* profit (margin × units you can actually move). But "units you can move" depends on order flow the UI can't see — `bidDepth`/`askDepth` are resting liquidity, not future fills — so any realizable-units number would be a guess presented as fact, and re-sorting on it would make the ranking *less* trustworthy, not more. So the ranking stayed on absolute margin (well-defined) and instead each row gained two metrics that ARE well-defined from data already in `MarketView`: return-on-cost (`margin/buy` — efficiency, so a +20 on a 100gp item reads as 20% vs +180 on 5000gp reading 3.6%) and the GE buy limit (`buyRemaining`, previously ticket-only — which flips are size-capped). Both are facts, not forecasts. Additive-field discipline paid off: extending `FlipPick` with `roi`/`limit` didn't break the existing rankFlips tests because they used `toMatchObject`/`length`/`map`, never `toEqual` on the whole object — the looseness that bit me in #111 (a too-loose assertion surviving) is the same looseness that makes additive change safe here. Render-test footgun re-confirmed: testing-library's `getNodeText` reads only an element's *direct* text nodes, so a margin span "+77" with a nested `<span>7.7%</span>` child still matches `getByText('+77')` (the child's "7.7%" is invisible to the parent match) — which is exactly why the pre-existing "+77" assertion kept passing after I nested the ROI inside it.

## Phase 11b — Profit by Item (2026-06-11)

114. **The trading game tracked every fill but never told you which trades actually MADE money — realized P&L is the obvious analytic that was missing.** TopFlips says "what to do next"; nothing said "what worked". `realizedPnL(fills, taxRate)` FIFO-matches each sell against the player's earlier buys in the rolling fill window, nets the sell tax, and reports profit per item — the reflective half of the trading loop. FIFO is the right cost-basis model precisely because fills arrive in tick order, so the buy queue is naturally first-in-first-out with no sorting; a sell walks the queue, splitting partial lots (5@100 + 3@200 against an 8-unit sell), and the per-unit `proceeds = price - floor(price*tax)` mirrors how the ticket already shows flip margin so the numbers reconcile. Two exclusions keep it honest, both tested: an OPEN position (bought, never sold) has no realized profit yet, and a sell with NO in-window cost basis (the matching buy aged out of the 50-fill cap) can't be scored — both contribute zero `soldUnits` and drop out, so the panel never invents a cost basis it doesn't have. The honest caveat is baked into the framing ("recent", window-bounded) rather than pretending to be lifetime P&L. Also the brick where I finally chased down the engine-content blocker properly: the verifier (`supabase/functions/verify-score/index.ts`) takes only `{handle, seed, log}` — NO claimed-hash check — so an engine change doesn't hard-reject submissions, it just leaves the deployed verifier scoring with a stale `engine.js` until `supabase functions deploy` re-runs; and the engine tests are self-consistent (compare two runs) not golden-hash, so they won't break on additive content. Conclusion stands though: shipping replay-affecting code to *main* without the coupled redeploy is an outward-facing live-leaderboard inconsistency I shouldn't introduce on an autonomous firing — so engine content waits for Jesse, and this brick stayed UI-only.

## Phase 11a — Keyboard Shortcuts (2026-06-11)

113. **The first brick in a while that adds a new INPUT path rather than re-presenting state — and the design is mostly about what NOT to bind.** After ten "surface latent state" bricks, keyboard shortcuts (1/2/3 rooms, p pause, ? help) are a genuinely different layer. The interesting decisions were all guard rails, not features: (1) `p` for pause, not Space — Space is how a focused button/tab activates, so binding it would steal that and feel broken; 1/2/3/p/? trigger no default control action outside a text field, so no `preventDefault` is even needed. (2) The handler bails on `INPUT`/`SELECT`/`TEXTAREA`/contentEditable targets and on any ctrl/meta/alt chord, so typing "p" in the filter or hitting Cmd-1 (browser tab) is never hijacked — pinned by a test that types a digit into the market filter and asserts the room did NOT change. (3) Kept the *mapping* pure (`resolveShortcut(key)`) and the *guard* at the call site (it needs the live DOM event), so the unambiguous half is unit-tested and the wiring half is render-tested. (4) Discoverability, the session's running theme: invisible shortcuts are useless, so they're documented in the help overlay with `<kbd>` keycaps — a feature nobody can find is the same anti-pattern as #104's invisible daily. `setSpeed`/`setHelpOpen` use functional updaters so the empty-dep effect never reads a stale value; `pickRoom`'s first-render closure is safe because it only calls stable setters.

## Phase 10z — Adventurer's Record (2026-06-11)

112. **A two-halves game can surface one half lavishly and forget the other — the records panel rebalances by consolidating what was scattered.** Exchange Wars is trading + RPG, but the session's work had piled feedback onto the trading half (net worth, gp/min, worth chart, leaderboard, flips) while the RPG accomplishments stayed scattered: a few in the expedition tally, several (eliteSlain, bountiesClaimed, contractsFilled, sellswordBanked) surfaced *nowhere*. The fix wasn't a new mechanic — the engine already tallied all of it in `SimStats` — it was a "Adventurer's Record" profile in the Hall consolidating the lot. Design discipline that made it a *record* and not a duplicate of the tally: it lives in the meta room (Hall), not the expedition screen, and it's the *full* sheet (the tally stays a quick glance). Pulled the row-building into a pure `recordRows(stats)` so the value logic (zero-defaulting absent fields, hiding sellsword rows until the hireling acts, compacting banked gp with an exact tooltip) is unit-tested without a render. One judgment call worth recording: `tradesTotal`/`ordersPlaced` in SimStats are *sim-wide* counters (the whole market), NOT the player's, so they'd be misleading on a personal record sheet — left them off; the panel is deliberately the adventuring half, since the trading half is already over-surfaced. Reused `.panel`/`.rows small` so zero new CSS. Caps the 10q–10z arc: the through-line of the whole session was "the data was already there" — events, gear gates, worth series, and now lifetime stats all existed in state and just needed a legible home.

## Phase 10y — Compact Big Numbers (2026-06-11)

111. **Compact notation needs a *principle* for where it applies, or it just trades one illegibility for another.** As wealth compounds, "1,234,567 net · +45,678 gp/min" stops being scannable — but blindly compacting everything would hide digits a trader actually needs (a bid of "1.2K" is useless when you're undercutting by 1). The principle that scoped the brick: **compact the approximate aggregates, keep the exact figures exact.** Net worth (a liquidation *estimate*), the all-time delta, and the earning *rate* are inherently fuzzy → `fmtCompact` (12.3K / 1.23M / 1.5B). Cash gp, prices, and order quantities are things you transact on → untouched. Every compacted figure carries the full value in its `title`, so precision is one hover away. Implementation leaned on the platform instead of hand-rolling unit thresholds: `Intl.NumberFormat({notation:'compact', maximumSignificantDigits:3})` handles all the rounding/boundary cases (999,999 → "1M") that a hand-rolled `/1e6` would fumble; the only custom rule is "<10k stays exact-with-commas" because compacting "9,999" to "10.0K" loses more than it saves. Caught a latent test coupling by reading first: a test asserted `getAllByText('55,000').length > 0` with a "(gp + net)" comment expecting BOTH to render the comma string — after the split, net became "55K" so only gp matches, but `> 0` still holds (the assertion was looser than its comment, so it survived; had it been `=== 2` it would have broken). The `+0` fresh-delta test also survived because `fmtCompact(0)` is exact "0". Lesson banked: when changing a display format, grep the tests for the *old* string, not just the component.

## Phase 10x — Earning-Rate Cue (2026-06-11)

110. **The cumulative net delta answers "have I won?"; an idle game also needs "am I winning *now*?" — and the data for it was already on disk.** The masthead showed net worth and the all-time delta vs start, but that delta is dominated by long-ago gains: a player whose setup has gone net-negative for the last hour still sees a big green "+250,000" and no signal that they're now bleeding. `worthRate(history, windowTicks=600)` takes the slope of the most recent ~10 min of the *throttled worth samples the Fortune chart already persists* (50-tick cadence, 240 cap) and reports gp/min (60 ticks = 1 min, matching fmtDuration's vocabulary). The windowing is the subtle part: walk back from the newest sample collecting only those within `windowTicks`, so old history can't drag the slope — pinned by a test where a tick-0 sample sits outside a 600-window ending at 1000 and is correctly ignored (slope measured over the 500-tick span, not 1000). Two honest-null guards: <2 samples, or a window so tight only the last sample falls inside it (dt 0) → no fictional rate. Cost was one pure function + one masthead span; zero new state, zero engine touch, because the time series was a byproduct of the chart feature built long ago. Recurring theme this whole session (#107/#108/#109): the high-value signal is usually *latent in state already computed* — the brick is making it visible, not generating it. Placed beside the delta with up/down colour so the two reads sit together: lifetime result + current trajectory.

## Phase 10w — Train-to-Unlock Hints (2026-06-11)

109. **An empty gear slot that just says "—" hides the most motivating fact in the RPG: you already own the upgrade, you just can't wear it yet.** The CharacterPanel showed what you'd fight in (best USABLE piece per slot) but said nothing about the better gear sitting in your bag behind a level gate — so the stats/xp ladder (8n) had no on-ramp from the character sheet; you couldn't see *why* to train. `lockedUpgrades(inv, lvls, worn)` finds, per slot, the highest-scoring held item you CAN'T equip yet AND that would beat what you wear, rendered as "🔒 Atk 20" / "🔒 Def 12" with a tooltip naming the piece — turning the sheet into a goal board. Two guard rails keep it honest: it skips items already usable (those just become `worn`), and it skips locked items that wouldn't be an upgrade (an unwearable *downgrade* is not an aspiration) — both pinned by tests (a fully-usable inventory yields `{}`; the usable helm produces no hint). Reused the exact `(slot==='weapon'?atk:def) < req` gate as `equipped`/deriveStats so the panel can't disagree with the engine about what's wearable. One CSS gotcha caught by reading: the existing `.equip.on span:last-child{color:gold}` rule targeted the item name by position — adding a third span (the hint) would have stolen the gold; fixed by classing the value span (`.equipval`) instead of relying on last-child, and wrapping the hint to its own line (`flex-basis:100%`) so it never crowds the name. Same theme as #107/#108: the motivating information was already implied by state (held items + levels + GEAR reqs); the brick just made it visible. Also re-learned the operational footgun: a stray `cd packages/ui/src` early in the session drifted the shell cwd, so `npm run typecheck` later resolved to the ui workspace (no such script) — fixed by always `cd`-ing to the repo root for workspace scripts.

## Phase 10v — Best Flips Now (2026-06-11)

108. **The ticket already knew the answer for one item; the win was asking it across every book and ranking.** The flip sum — undercut the spread one tick each way, net the 2% tax — lived in `TradeTicket` for the *selected* item only, so the trader had to click through items to discover where a profitable spread actually was. `rankFlips(markets, taxRate, limit)` runs that identical sum over `view.markets`, keeps only genuine two-sided books with strictly-positive net margin, and ranks — turning a per-item readout into a "do this now" shortlist (the actionable counterpart to Movers' "where's the action"). Two deliberate moves kept it honest: (1) tax rate is a *parameter*, not an import, so the ranker is a pure function unit-tested against a hand-computed truth table (margin 77 > 6, thin spread excluded, one-sided book excluded, limit respected) with zero render/engine coupling; (2) it requires BOTH bestBid and bestAsk present, unlike the ticket's ema-fallback — a "flip" off a one-sided book is fictional, so the strip refuses to invent one. Mirrored `MoversPanel` exactly (same `.panel`/`.rows`/`.mover` classes, same click-to-select, same dim empty-state), so it inherited all styling and the empty case ("spreads thinner than the 2% tax") reads as information, not breakage. The constraint that shaped the brick: no `SUPABASE_ACCESS_TOKEN` in this session → can't redeploy `verify-score` → no replay-affecting engine change was permissible, so the value had to come from re-presenting data the view already exposes. Reinforces #107's lesson — a lot of game-feel is latent in state already computed, waiting to be surfaced.

## Phase 10u — Corrupt-Save Recovery (2026-06-11)

107. **A safety net you can't see isn't one — the quarantine was half a feature until it had an exit.** `loadGame` already did the hard, correct thing: an unreadable save is copied to `CORRUPT_SAVE_KEY` before the first autosave of the fresh game overwrites it (FINDINGS earlier), so the data was never actually destroyed. But "preserved in a localStorage key no player will ever open" is indistinguishable from "lost" — the recovery path existed only for someone with devtools. This brick makes it real: a boot-time `useState(() => loadCorruptSave())` surfaces a red recovery bar (distinct from the neutral gold away-bars) with download / discard / dismiss. Download reuses the exact Blob+anchor mechanism as the normal save export, so a rescued save round-trips straight back through the existing file-import path — no new format, the recovery is just "get the bytes out, re-import them". The three buttons map to the three real intents: rescue it (download), I don't want it (discard → clears the quarantine), not now (× → keep it for next boot). Lesson: quarantining bad data is only the back half of data-loss safety; the front half is telling the user it happened and handing them the bytes. Centralizing the key access in game.ts (`loadCorruptSave`/`discardCorruptSave` next to `loadGame`/`clearSave`) kept App from poking raw localStorage and made the round-trip unit-testable without a render. Found while pivoting off the daily arc — and the market-row sparkline I'd queued turned out already built (Spark in MarketTable), a reminder that reading first beats trusting a stale NEXT_STEPS line.

## Phase 10t — Streak-at-Risk Nudge (2026-06-11)

106. **A retention reward shown only to people already retained is backwards — the FOMO trigger has to fire where the lapse happens.** 10s's 🔥 ember only rendered while you were *on* the daily, i.e. it congratulated players who'd already returned and said nothing to the ones drifting away — exactly inverted from how a streak drives behaviour. The fix is `streakAtRisk(streak, today)` = "last played EXACTLY yesterday (span 1) and not yet today", surfaced as a loud pulsing CTA shown precisely when you're NOT on the daily. The span-based definition is what makes it correct at the edges: span 0 (already played today) is safe → no nag; span ≥2 (already dead) → no false hope; only span 1 is the genuine one-day-from-breaking window. It reuses the same `streakDaySpan` UTC helper as bumpStreak, so the "what counts as a day" rule has a single definition both the advance and the at-risk check share — they can't disagree about whether a streak is alive. The CTA reuses the seed-draft soft-confirm (pre-fills today's daily, one more click to launch), so the nudge can't nuke a run by accident — same safety as every other restart path. The render test pins the real mechanic: a streak whose lastDay is `dailySeed(now − 86_400_000ms)` (exactly one UTC day back, robust because UTC days are exactly 24h) shows the CTA off-daily and pre-fills the daily seed on click, and is absent when already on the daily. Loop now closed: ember (you're safe) + nudge (you're at risk) are the two halves, and they share one notion of "a day".

## Phase 10s — Daily Streak (2026-06-11)

105. **The retention loop is a pure function plus a flame — keep the clock out of the logic and the test trivializes.** A daily streak (Wordle's hook) is conceptually "consecutive days played", but the actual difficulty is the day-gap arithmetic: YYYYMMDD seeds aren't subtractable (20260601 − 20260531 = 70, not 1). Pushing both endpoints through `Date.UTC(y, m-1, d)` and diffing epoch-ms makes month/year rollover correct for free, and — critically — `bumpStreak(prev, today)` takes `today` as an argument rather than reading the clock, so it's a pure reducer: same-day is idempotent (returns the SAME ref so the caller skips the localStorage write), next-day is +1, any gap resets to 1 while preserving `best`. Six one-line assertions cover the whole truth table (fresh / same-day / consecutive / month-roll / year-roll / skip / backwards) with no mocking, no fake timers. The React side is then trivial: one effect keyed on `world.seed` that calls bumpStreak when the active world is the daily — idempotency means "fire on every seed change" is safe, it advances at most once per UTC day. Lesson: when a feature *sounds* like it needs the clock, see if the clock-read can be lifted to the boundary (the effect) and the logic kept pure — the calendar correctness and the test simplicity both fall out. Same shape as the engine's own purity rule, applied to a UI nicety.

## Phase 10r — Daily, Legible (2026-06-11)

104. **A feature nobody can tell they're using is half-built — make state visible where the eye already is.** 10q shipped the daily seed but left it invisible: after clicking "🗓 daily" nothing confirmed you were on today's shared world, so the social hook (your score lands on *today's* board) was a fact the player couldn't perceive. The fix isn't more UI, it's putting the state where attention already rests — a "🗓 today" pill next to the seed in the always-on clock, shown precisely when `world.seed === dailySeed()`. This is the same discoverability lesson as the equip mechanic (9z) and the clerk's unit cap (9i): Jesse repeatedly surfaces "I can't tell what's happening" gaps, and the cure each time is legibility at the existing focal point, not a new panel. Derived purely at render from `world.seed` vs `dailySeed()` — no state, no engine touch, and the badge self-heals at UTC midnight (a daily world silently stops being "today" when the date rolls, which is correct). Test pins both directions (on-daily shows, plain seed 42 — an impossible 8-digit collision — doesn't). Reduced-motion guarded the subtle glow.

## Phase 10q — Daily Seed (2026-06-11)

103. **A "daily" turns a per-seed leaderboard into a social race for free — the wall clock is the shared coordination key.** The leaderboard was already per-seed (anyone on seed 42 competes on the same board), but nobody could agree on *which* seed without passing a challenge link. `dailySeed()` makes today's UTC date the seed (`YYYYMMDD` as an int, e.g. 20260611), so every player who clicks "🗓 daily" lands on the identical world that day and their scores stack on one board — a synchronized global race with zero new backend, just a derived default. Crucially this is a **UI-only** helper: it reads `new Date()`/UTC, which the deterministic engine is forbidden from touching, but the UI is not the engine — it only *chooses* a seed and hands the integer to the same `restart(seed)` path a manual entry uses, so determinism is untouched (same seed in → same world out). UTC (not local) is deliberate: a "daily" must be the same world for everyone regardless of timezone, and the test pins a late-UTC-day instant (23:59:59) to prove it resolves by the UTC calendar day, not the local one. The button reuses the existing seed-draft soft-confirm (it pre-fills the draft rather than restarting instantly), so "daily" can't nuke a run by accident — same safety as "new game".

## Phase 10p — Bestiary Portraits (2026-06-11)

102. **Extract-on-second-use: the combat monster art became a bestiary portrait by DRYing, not redrawing.** The combat scene's archetype silhouette was inline; the bestiary wanted the same art at small size. Pulled the body shapes + hueOf into a shared `MonsterBody` (the centred-at-0,0 shapes) with a `MonsterGlyph` standalone-svg wrapper, then CombatScene embeds `<MonsterBody>` in its positioned group and the Bestiary uses `<MonsterGlyph>` per met entry. Now a goblin's portrait in the codex is literally the same component as the goblin you fight — they can't drift, and adding a future archetype updates both. The refactor was free-of-risk because the combat-scene position test (still asserting translate(150 on the monster's parent group) stayed green — the shapes moved into a child but the group structure didn't. Cohesion compounding: skill icons (9n), item icons (10o), now monster portraits — the HUD's original-art layer is becoming consistent and component-shared rather than per-surface bespoke.

## Phase 10o — Item Icons (2026-06-11)

101. **Category icons are the 80/20 of item art: 4 originals cover dozens of items.** Rather than one bespoke icon per catalog item (hundreds), a categorizer (`itemIcon(id)`) maps every item to one of a handful of category icons — weapons/armor REUSE the existing skill sword/shield, and 4 new originals (potion/food/rune/bone) cover the rest, with id-substring fallthrough (bone/rune) and a glyph for the uncategorized. Wired into the Ledger inventory + pack-builder rows via the 9m `<Icon>` pipeline (glob auto-discovery, so the new SVGs bundle on build — 307ms — and tests see them). The categorizer reads GEAR/CONSUMABLES (single source of truth for what's gear vs consumable), so it can't drift from the engine's notion of item types. Same principle as the monster silhouettes (#85): archetype/category art >> one-blob, and <<< per-item bespoke. The art direction advances without waiting on a file drop, and a real game-icons set can still override any item-*.svg by filename later.

## Phase 10n — The Offensive Brew (2026-06-11)

100. **Completing a system is its own reward — and a one-line content add ripples through shared test fixtures.** Goading potion (+10 atk/dive) is the offensive counterpart to divine bastion (+10 def), so raiders now make a real pre-dive call: offence (faster kills → less attrition/leech) vs defence (survive deeper) — one CONSUMABLES line, the dive-long-flag machinery already there. But adding it to the shared test fixture (inventory + items) rippled: the combat-brew test's "persists" assertion was stale (goading refreshed the buff off bastion), AND the Death Ward test's total-units arithmetic shifted (an unpacked goading is one more home-stock unit: 12→13). Lesson re-learned: a shared fixture is coupling — every test that counts "total units" or asserts an exact buff is implicitly pinned to the fixture's contents, so additive fixture changes need a sweep of the arithmetic-dependent tests, not just the new test. The conservation gate + exact-count assertions caught both immediately. (FINDINGS hits #100.)

## Phase 10m — Fair Value Band (2026-06-11)

99. **Two orthogonal trading reads: the spread says "is a flip profitable NOW", the band says "is this item cheap vs its own fundamentals".** The flip line (10j) answers the immediate trade; the fair-value band answers the position question — where lastPrice sits in [baseCost..consumeValue] (cheap <34% / fair / rich >67%), which tells an accumulator whether to load up or hold off independent of the current spread. Both derive from data the ItemDef + book already carry, no engine change. The pair completes the trader's two timescales: tactical (flip the spread) and positional (buy the dip vs sell the rip). Colour semantics chosen carefully — cheap green (accumulate), rich GOLD not red (a sell opportunity isn't bad), fair dim — same lesson as the death-ward sink: not every "high" is negative.

## Phase 10l — Welcome Back, the Sellsword Worked (2026-06-11)

98. **A counter is data; a delta across a session is a story.** 10k made sellsword kills/loot legible in the Almanac (cumulative); 10l makes the OFFLINE delta a homecoming line — the away-bar now reads "…net worth +Y · 🗡 sellsword: N kills, X gp banked" by snapshotting the counters in planOfflineProgress and differencing in finishOfflineProgress. Same data, but the framing (what happened WHILE YOU WERE GONE) is what makes idle progress feel earned — the idle-game dopamine is the return summary, not the absolute number. Pure UI (the engine counters shipped 10k), no redeploy. The offline plumbing already threaded worthBefore/After through plan→finish, so the sellsword deltas rode the exact same path — extending an existing snapshot-diff is far cheaper than inventing a new one.

## Phase 10k — The Sellsword's Haul (2026-06-11)
